mr president, i would like to thank you. i regret that i have only just arrived, due to a train delay, and have therefore not been able to hear the previous debate. i have asked to speak because last year i was rapporteur for a report on the local dimension of the european employment strategy. i spoke very strongly in favour of some of the aspects which napolitano spoke in his report, namely the importance of a bottom-up perspective, that is to say, the importance of mobilising from below. i still disagree with napolitano's perspective. the very heart of his perspective seems to be to try to somehow reduce or even eliminate the importance of national political democracies. he places a very strong emphasis on the local and regional level on the one hand and the commission and the eu level on the other, which i think is a very dangerous line of reasoning. i believe, after all, that national democracies are the very democratic foundation of the european project. i therefore believe that this local and regional development should take place within the framework of these national democracies. the nations, especially governments and parliaments, must be the link between the local, regional and european levels. the commission's proposal is therefore not acceptable. it can only lead to a weakening of european democracy, and none of us can wish that.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the states are the backbone of europe and the regions its flesh and blood. this is the view of jordi pujol, president of catalonia. it is true that regions, but also municipalities, i.e. local units, are closer to many citizens and perhaps more natural than the european institutions in particular. in addition, local and regional authorities implement a large part of eu decisions. it is therefore appropriate that they should be given a stronger role as we review european governance. these entities must be involved early in any form of referral procedure when proposals are formulated. they can play an important role in evaluation and follow-up and in safeguarding the principle of subsidiarity, as suggested in the report. we cannot resolve internal constitutional conflicts here, but we must send a clear signal that we attach great importance to the local and regional levels and enshrine this in the treaty. it is therefore desirable to integrate, for example, the charter of local and regional authorities. i would like to see as few amendments as possible to this report. i am convinced that it will be an excellent basis for discussion in the convention, among others.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur. the liberal group supports the swiebel report. we think that the report provides an important and useful review of the situation of fundamental rights in our own union. the scope of the report may be questioned, as regards both its subject matter and its political content. we can also disagree about some of the wording and examples, but i think we can learn from that work for next year's report. we therefore do not share the fundamental criticism of the report. we demand that many other countries respect the demands of fundamental rights. that is why, mr president, it is all the more important that we examine the situation on our own farm. it's good to look at the backyard, but sometimes it's good to look at your own backyard. unfortunately, there are too many examples in this report to show that the situation could have been much better in our own countries. we all agree on the importance of fighting with all our might those who want to damage the foundations of our society with violence and terror. there is, of course, a broad consensus. there may be reason to pause at times and ask whether the end always justifies the means. can the struggle of democracies for democracy and an open society, through the repressive means to which we are sometimes forced, also threaten the values we defend? the question, mr president, deserves to be formulated. we think that the introduction of a sunset clause, i.e. legislation with a time limit, as a rule, for this type of specific legislation which infringes on personal privacy, is a reasonable way forward. a related current topic, which has nothing to do with this but indirectly, namely the debate on torture in the united states, which has been raised by the economist among others, naturally also affects us here in europe. we liberals also welcome an open discussion on the poor conditions in many of the prisons in the eu as well as on the behaviour of the police. i am convinced that the democratic institutions are sufficiently solid to cope with this debate. our democracy is also strong enough to allow for open discussion of the means. my own country is also criticised in this report. the swiebel report is important, and it is important that the debate in this parliament on racism, xenophobia and anti-semitism be held. let us not, therefore, turn this discussion into an internal national matter. this is about something much bigger. in conclusion, i would like to highlight the union's policy on asylum and refugees. unfortunately, many violations of fundamental rights have occurred and are occurring in these areas. not least, refugee children are being harmed, which should put all governments to shame. if we cannot agree on anything else, at least in this parish we should be able to agree that all children need extra protection. mr president, public opinion is important. let's not forget that. perhaps we can look at what happened this weekend in the united states as proof that opinion formation can actually work. we have amendments from the liberal group on family relations and the concept of the family. it's more clarifying.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the european parliament, like the eu member states, has a responsibility to defend human rights and fundamental freedoms. at the same time, there is a dangerous tendency to equate democratic freedoms, such as the right to speak, think and vote, with far-reaching social demands. this is a weakening of the foundations of democracy. if all rights are equally important, in the end no rights become particularly important. the value of an annual report from the european parliament is questionable if it is a palette of subjective opinions. political wish lists easily result in inventing new things instead of focusing on the really fundamental rights. if we attack from this, the broad perspective, i am surprised that the right to good administration and the right to democratic elections are missing. they should be more relevant than many of those in the report. it has been shown that even the present member states need to be reminded of them. there should be sanctions in the eu against member states which fail to maintain democratic elections and where there is proven electoral fraud. the convention should reflect on this. however, there are new positive aspects to the report which are worth mentioning. data protection and transparency are included. these rights must be developed in the treaty. data protection and the right to documents are in the bill of rights. the right of citizens to information article 255 of the treaty is now ripe for further concretisation. in conclusion, the commission has the european parliament has an important role to play in matters relating to human rights and fundamental freedoms. i hope that the convention will strengthen this role by creating a democratic decision-making system and a right for citizens to go to court and, in some cases, to obtain compensation if their rights have been violated. clearly, this matter needs further development. swiebel's made that clear. that's good, but i'd rather concentrate and dive next time than just widen. for the future, we obviously need to develop the methods.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i should like to begin by thanking my colleague, mrs swiebel, for the great work she has put into this report. there is no doubt that we in the eu are lucky in respecting freedom and rights. that is why we are having this debate, with the aim of continuously improving, maintaining a high level of protection and openly discussing the shortcomings that exist. it is an important issue which concerns the most fundamental element for the proper functioning of the eu, mutual trust between the member states. we're not perfect. there is a misuse of justice and a lack of respect for fundamental rights and freedoms, and not just 'among everyone else'. my own country, sweden, is being singled out in connection with a high-profile death, where the actions of the police, the prosecutor and the medical examiner have been heavily criticised. i would like to make it clear that the swedish government has long since responded to the criticism and has launched an investigation, which was completed last year. there's a clear civil rights angle to that investigation. another very important issue concerns the rights of european citizens in legal proceedings, such as detentions. there are member states where people are detained for long periods without trial; we must discuss this issue. our young people travel all over europe. no one on eu territory should have to worry about the possibility of personal freedom being restricted by poorly functioning procedures in police stations and prisons. we must therefore make it very clear that all european citizens are guaranteed certain rights in legal proceedings in all countries. i look forward to the commission's further work and proposals on this matter. trafficking in human beings, especially women and children, is a reality in our european union. the eu is often the final destination for people who are being driven from their countries of origin in desperation and confusion. it is a modern-day slave trade, and we must ask ourselves how we can stop this development. i am concerned that many eu countries accept different forms of prostitution. this makes the eu a favourable market for traffickers from all over the world. the link between human trafficking and prostitution is clear. they're two sides of the same coin. i would have liked the report to have been clearer on this point. mr president, i would like to thank you. we must make great demands on ourselves in the union now that we are making demands on the applicant countries.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the wide divisions which emerged in the vote on this report in the committee concerned must mean that swiebel has not sought a unified solution. she has selectively presented a number of areas which are not only deeply controversial politically such as family law and family law but also subjects which clearly do not fall within the european union's sphere of competence. swiebel may search the un charter of fundamental rights, the european convention on human rights and the eu charter of fundamental rights for a reason why these issues should be included in a report on fundamental rights. mrs swiebel, does the fact that polygamy is not accepted in the european union mean that muslims living in europe are being discriminated against? is this a violation of human rights? i think that calls for an answer when so many new substances have been added to this substance. i also ask swiebel why, when she raises so many new subjects, she does not mention anything about the netherlands having legalized prostitution. thus, there is a need for the 'import' of women in a country where, oddly enough, prostitution is considered to be a legal and approved activity. why does swiebel not mention his own country's active euthanasia policy, which is abusing doctors and ending people's lives in an unworthy way? there is abuse of the so-called active euthanasia. why is there no mention of the right of parents to raise their children in a drug-free society where parents feel the law is on their side? swiebel has been very selective. we christian democrats will of course vote against this, because swiebel has failed to fulfil an important mission in the european parliament.
the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion on the proposal. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to begin by thanking rapporteur markov for his good cooperation in the preparation of the report. the main issues are three: health and safety at work, road safety and equal conditions of competition. the social affairs committee has focused on health and safety, but these issues are interrelated. good health and safety of drivers, such as resting at the wheel, is conducive to road safety. similarly, minimum rules throughout the internal market ensure that competition is not distorted. it is a little strange that there are proposals to reject this report. there is a great deal of agreement between the council, the commission and, i hope, the majority in parliament that this 18 year old regulation now needs to be changed, partly as a result of the use of digital tachographs. one of the causes and the main problem is in fact the poor enforcement of the existing regulation. i can see for myself that the enforcement is poor in my own country. i would therefore like to stress one very important proposal in the new regulation, namely that the supervisory authorities of the member states can report breaches of rest periods, even if the breach has occurred on the territory of another member state. there are a number of aspects which we social democrats, both from the social affairs committee and the transport committee, would like to see reinforced in the existing report. this applies, for example, to the minimum daily rest period and the maximum driving time. we have therefore tabled amendments nos 105 and 106. we also believe that it is a minimum requirement in terms of working conditions that a driver should not be forced to spend his long weekly rest in the cab. we have therefore tabled amendment no 107. unfortunately, this amendment is incorrectly translated in the swedish version, so the english version applies. amendment no 110 is about not paying compensation to drivers for the corrected distance. the agreement in this area is directly reprehensible. in writing. (sv) we, the swedish social democrats, put forward the following explanation of vote on amendment no 4. : the full opening of the market for national rail passenger services by 1 january 2008 at the latest should be achieved under certain basic conditions. for example, there must be guarantees that rail services will continue to cover the whole of the eu and will not leave less profitable lines unused, which would be disastrous for countries with large areas and small populations, such as sweden. in addition, guarantees of safety, quality and social rights must be in place before the railway market can be realised.
in writing. (sv) the proposal in the report to give the committee of the regions the right to bring an action before the european court of justice in the event of an alleged breach of the principle of subsidiarity and to defend its rights is unfair. the member states are not homogeneous. they range from federal states to single centralized states. it is therefore difficult to devise policies for cooperation within the eu, as regards the role of the committee of the regions and regional and local authorities. the committee of the regions has no democratic legitimacy and we believe that it should be phased out rather than expanded. the democratic deficit in the eu today makes it more important to develop the role of national parliaments first and foremost. the eu's members are in fact states, not regions.
in writing. - i voted in favour of this report. the fact that the european parliament clearly states in recital g that 'the safeguarding and strengthening of regional and local autonomy in the various european countries is an important contribution to the construction of europe based on the principles of democracy, proximity and decentralisation of power' is very positive. as i have long advocated this in my experience at local level, i am very pleased to see that the report also calls for the definition in a well thought-out european constitution of how the principle of subsidiarity is to be exercised, applied and monitored. it is quite appropriate to create an instrument enabling local authorities and departments/regions/länder, through their member state or the committee of the regions, to ask the european court of justice to rule in cases where the eu exceeds its powers. another perspective that is important for citizens is adopted in recital h, which proposes that 'a hierarchical and pyramidal conception of the institutional system of the union be abandoned'.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i had the privilege of speaking to the afghan leaders at the bonn conference at the invitation of un mediator brahimi. i was then chairman of the committee on women's rights and gender equality and had listened to a representative of the organisation rawa on the situation of women in afghanistan. i was therefore able to put to the leaders the obvious demand that women should be included in the provisional and the permanent government and not be marginalized in the reconstruction work. the committee organised a conference for women from afghanistan in conjunction with ngos. we then set out the common priorities in a resolution adopted by parliament at the december 2001 part-session. on behalf of the committee i asked the committee on budgets to make a specific amendment on support for the women of afghanistan, which the committee and parliament did. why am i giving you this background? because i see very little of it in the joint resolution. the word 'women' and their human rights is missing. the resolution does not mention the priorities of women, but the afghan government does. the requirement that all aid projects should have a gender perspective is also missing. women's rights are quickly forgotten in the male world we live in. the strong and driven minister of gender equality in the afghan interim government was elevated and women in afghanistan are now almost invisible as actors, despite the fact that probably two-thirds of the population is made up of women and children. my question to the commission and the council of ministers is, how is this to be interpreted, have women been forgotten again, are there no demands on women to the afghan government? the women were victims of both the northern alliance and the taliban. women are the most important actors of peace. a policy for afghanistan must be a policy for the majority, for women and children.
madam president, i would like to thank you. as we all know, the question of a comprehensive and rapid ban on animal testing of cosmetics was the major issue in this settlement. the agreement we reached has been met with criticism from many quarters. animal welfare organisations, in particular, have criticised it for taking too long to implement the ban. i agree with that criticism. i think we could have gone faster. however, it is important to criticize the one who deserves criticism. if it had been the european parliament that had decided the matter, it would have been quicker, perhaps the ban would have been in force already. in fact, the council of ministers of the european union is the one to blame in this case. in particular, some member states did everything to delay the ban. the credit for the ban should go to the european parliament. the person who deserves the most credit in this respect is our rapporteur, mrs roth-behrendt. i have rarely seen more energetic, competent and powerful leadership of a parliamentary delegation in a settlement, and it felt very good to work with roth-behrendt. i thank her for this brilliant work.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i join the chorus of the roth-behrendt tribute. i think you are a fine and excellent example, not only of a good parliamentarian but also of what we so badly need, namely powerful women in politics. thank you for your work, your commitment and your temperament! we need you! it is simply to state that a compromise is a compromise. i can fully understand the distress of animal welfare organisations that it takes so long for the ban to be imposed, that so many thousands of animals are being sacrificed for the sake of our vanity. i am glad, however, that we did reach some kind of limit, something to take in, something to hold on to. i am also very pleased that consumer protection has been taken seriously, that we are getting declarations of contents and that there are going to be bans on certain substances which are clearly inappropriate, perhaps especially in products we use on children and young people who are more vulnerable than we are. i would like to look ahead to the future and broaden the debate a little on animal welfare. we are again seeing these terrible films about animal transport, slaughter and how we in europe treat our animals. what if parliament had influence, had a say in agricultural policy? imagine that situation, given the attitude and opinion of an overwhelming majority of this parliament on animal welfare! imagine how far we could have come, even if we know that democracy's decision-making is the compromise. the compromise, the democratic compromise, promises us only one thing: we will never get exactly what we want.
in writing. (sv) the we, the swedish social democrats, put forward this explanation of vote on the following points: point 23 is this: we object to the term "forced prostitution". we consider prostitution to be always compulsory. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this problem, and it is not surprising that the commission has taken a number of measures to combat it. we cannot talk about voluntary prostitution, so we cannot talk about forced prostitution. the following paragraph is added: we believe that sweden should sign ilo convention no 169 on indigenous peoples. however, the signing of the convention requires amendments to swedish law. for example, the extent of land traditionally used or owned by the lapps must be clearly defined. the government has appointed a special committee to define the boundaries of the reindeer breeding area. the study should be completed by the end of 2004 and then submitted for consideration. this means that it will be several years before sweden can ratify the convention. point 133: in the explanatory memorandum to paragraph 133, sweden is singled out for two deaths that attracted public attention, which strongly blamed the police, the prosecutor and the investigator for the way they acted. we would point out that this critical climate prompted the swedish government to carry out an inquiry into the matter, which was completed last year. the investigation was conducted precisely from the point of view of human rights and it takes a negative view of various aspects of the inquiry.
in writing. (sv) the the conservative delegation in the european parliament considers it of paramount importance that parliament should draw up an annual report on the way in which the member states are respecting human rights. human rights are the cornerstone of european cooperation. it is self-evident that the demands we make on our states must be as stringent as those we make on the candidate countries and other countries outside the eu. it is unfortunate, however, that the report does not focus solely on fundamental rights, but goes further by formulating some political ambitions. whether or not we share these political visions, this approach risks undermining the importance of fundamental rights. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. we also vote against paragraph 43, because it is not worded correctly. of course, the principle that we cannot convict someone twice for the same crime is fundamental. this does not mean, however, that imprisonment and deportation should be ruled out as punishments in the case of serious offences. we also oppose eu intervention in matters concerning the different types of family relationships, as we believe that, under the principle of subsidiarity, family policy falls within the competence of each member state individually.
in writing. (sv) the we think the report is very good and therefore we support it. however, we do not share the rapporteur's absolute conviction that the only way to achieve sustainable respect for human rights in the european union is to introduce the charter of fundamental rights into future treaties.
in writing. - i voted against the report. it is a unique event in the history of the european parliament that a rapporteur presents a report on human rights which is, rightly, so severely criticised and which has such a short majority (274 votes in favour, 269 against and 14 abstentions). the report is the very expression of a selective political choice. the term human rights is misused. this is because the rapporteur is deviating from and going far beyond the objective and mission of the report (e.g. family law or the property rights of greek monks on mount athos), which constitute the eu's charter of fundamental rights. human rights are being watered down and reduced in value. it's as bad as that. i wonder very much about the validity of the statement in paragraph 133 that a 'climate of impunity' for misconduct and abuse of violence by police and prison staff must be established in seven eu member states, including sweden. the european christian democrats, however, succeeded in deleting paragraph 83 (279 votes in favour, 259 against and 9 abstentions) calling on the member states to 'extend the concept of marriage to people of the same sex' as it is not a competence of the eu.
in writing. - (sv) the schengen agreement has increased the freedom of citizens by allowing them to move within the member states without presenting a passport at internal borders. it is clear that such a reform requires more effective control of the common external borders. in this context, it is of the utmost importance to establish close cooperation between current and future member states in order to ensure equivalent border control along all external borders, in particular through a training effort and common guidelines. we are, however, skeptical about the idea of an eu-funded border force. the monopoly of the national police in the fight against crime and border surveillance in the territory of the member state is fundamental and must be preserved. the police of another country must not be allowed to act in another member state, except in very special circumstances, as specified in the schengen agreement. these rules should, as far as possible, also apply to border control. the decisions and traditions of the member states with regard to police work, in particular with regard to identity checks and arrest procedures, differ in many respects. with a common border force, there is a risk that these decisions will not be fully respected. the increased need for staff to ensure effective surveillance of the eu's external borders should instead be met by providing economic assistance to the border authorities of the member states concerned.
in writing. - (sv) in the presidency conclusions of the seville european council in june 2002, the council invited the commission to carry out a study on the sharing of responsibilities between the member states and the union in the management of the external borders. this was to be done before june 2003, which is why the results have not yet been presented. the european parliament nevertheless chose in the resolution to move ahead with the study and to ask the convention to examine the possibilities of legally justifying the creation of a european border guard corps and the council to set up such a force. it is clear that closer cooperation is needed in this area, but we believe that a proposal of this length must be based on a proper study from a constitutional, legal, democratic and financial point of view before the question can be settled. a border surveillance force, whatever temporary mandate is imposed on it, is nevertheless a new institution and a new legal competence of the eu.
in writing. - (sv) we are somewhat doubtful about the content of the mantovani report. we support the eldr group's amendments which advocate cooperation instead of an open method of coordination. we are against the development of common standards for the monitoring of health services and of strategies for the development of health services in this area. as swedish liberals, we see european integration as an opportunity to solve cross-border problems, such as the environment, trade, cross-border mobility, human rights and conflict management. we also believe in the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions must be taken as closely as possible to the people concerned. it must be clear to all citizens that the eu should only deal with the issues that it is best placed to deal with, namely crossborder issues. all other issues must be dealt with at local, regional or national level. health care and care for the elderly are examples of sectors in which the eu should have only limited competence. each member state must have full responsibility and the right to decide these matters for itself, since these are clear examples of sectors which are not directly cross-border. health care and care for the elderly are issues that have traditionally been high on the liberal agenda.

the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. let me begin by congratulating mr markov on a good report. the ebrd has played, and will continue to play, a major role in the - the next item is the joint debate on the following six motions for resolutions on the humanitarian crisis in north korea: - b5-0036/2003 by mr ford and mr van den berg, on behalf of the pse group, on the humanitarian situation in the democratic people's republic of korea; - b5-0039/2003 by mr tannock, mr gawronski, mr perry and mr maij-weggen, on behalf of the ppe-de group, on the humanitarian crisis in north korea; - b5-0043/2003 by mr watson, mr malmström and mr andreasen, on behalf of the eldr group, on the famine in north korea; - b5-0045/2003 by mr lagendijk, mr turmes, mr ahern, mr gahrton, mr schroedter, mr wuori and mr maes, on behalf of the green group, on north korea; - b5-0048/2003 by mr belder, on behalf of the edd group, on the humanitarian crisis in north korea;
commission. - madam president, my colleague chris patten has more than once had the opportunity to speak about korea in this chamber and, when referring to north korea, he often uses the phrase 'it is a strange country'. some of you have pointed out the contradictions that are occurring in north korea. on the one hand, it is a country with a gigantic army, with a secret uranium enrichment programme - it is the first country to withdraw from the non-proliferation treaty and threatens its neighbours - and on the other hand, it is suffering from serious internal difficulties. the end of the cold war brought with it the disappearance of its sources of supply of oil and raw materials at low prices, and the country is not able to produce enough items to meet its needs. there's a shortage of medicines, there's not enough energy, etc. how can the european union respond to such a contradictory situation? it is clear that we cannot continue to do things as we have done them. this is why last november the partners of the korean peninsula energy development organization decided to suspend heavy fuel shipments to north korea. at the same time, i believe it is essential - and some of you have suggested this - to continue making efforts, to continue dialogue, to bring them to their senses. we are currently discussing with the member states of the union how best to achieve this objective, and we are also pleased to see that the united states is raising some of these issues with a view to entering into a dialogue with north korea. it is true that while we are concerned about what is happening in the dprk, we must not - nor do we want to - punish the weaker and more disadvantaged sections of the korean people, especially punishing them for the mistakes of their government. some of you have spoken of grotesque governments that do not respond to the reality of the country. that is why, after the november council discussions, the ministers agreed that we must continue our humanitarian aid activities, including food aid. you have proposed that we keep the aid, but you also warn that we must be much more careful about its control. the council fully shares your view. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. some of the information we are currently getting could be useful to this parliament. as you know, echo is the largest external donor of humanitarian aid to the dprk, excluding food aid, and has provided over eur 62 million in humanitarian aid since 1995. aid shall be granted impartially. it is provided to those most in need through european union ngos, the red cross and un agencies. in 2002, eur 11.5 million was allocated to the rehabilitation and protection of water supplies, wells, sanitary structures, distribution of water containers, chlorine tablets, hygiene materials, medicines, etc. as far as food aid is concerned, we are currently a relatively modest donor. in 2002 - and some of you have referred to this - we supplied 23 000 tonnes of wheat, worth 5 million euros, to the world food programme for north korea. last week we approved an emergency food aid package worth 9.5 million euros. it involves 39,000 tonnes of cereals for children in particular, in response to the appeal made last november by the world food programme in favour of north korea. it was granted by echo only on condition - and we are again concerned about the problem of monitoring - that the dprk accept the requests of the world food programme; this will mean that better monitoring and on-the-spot checks will be carried out. as regards the latter request, the commission, italy, norway and switzerland are the only ones who have committed themselves to moving forward along this path.
- this debate is over. the vote will take place this afternoon after the debate.
- the next item is the joint debate on the following five motions for resolutions on equatorial guinea: - b5-0038/2003 by mr sauquillo pérez del arco, mr carlotti, mr martínez martínez, mr obiols i germà and mr carnero gonzález, on behalf of the group of the party of european socialists, on the presidential elections and the lack of respect for human rights in equatorial guinea; - b5-0040/2003 by mr ferrer and mr posselt, on behalf of the group of the european people's party, on equatorial guinea; - b5-0044/2003 by mr sanders-ten holte, mr sánchez garcía and mr gasòliba i böhm, on behalf of the eldr group, on the situation of human rights in equatorial guinea; - b5-0046/2003 by mr rod, mr isler béguin and mr maes, on behalf of the green group in the european parliament, on the presidential elections in equatorial guinea; - b5-0050/2003 by mr marset campos and mr gonzález álvarez, on behalf of the gue/ngl group, on the presidential elections and the lack of respect for human rights in equatorial guinea.
commission. - madam president, the commission shares your concern about the human rights situation in equatorial guinea. we believe that the situation has deteriorated considerably in the last year, breaking the slightly positive trend which seemed to be taking place until the end of 2001. this degradation calls into question the real will of the president obiang regime to democratize and respect human rights. we also share with you our doubts and our assessment of the 15 december elections, which were called early, without respecting the provisions of the constitution itself and without allowing the presence of electoral observers. they cannot, of course, be described as democratic elections. the general pardon, issued by president obiang in october last year, should also apply to all political opponents arrested in the spring of 2002 and sentenced in trials without guarantees. we all agree that cooperation with equatorial guinea is very much conditioned by the difficulties in the democratization process and by the development of human rights in that country. cooperation was blocked for several years, and five years ago partial cooperation was initiated. it is true that the national indicative programme for the eighth european development fund, signed in july 2000, identified as a priority area for our action support for democracy, human rights, civil society and the rule of law. it also states that the availability of european funds for other actions is subject to the guinean government's proven efforts in this field. our project is therefore aimed at promoting democratization and respect for human rights. as regards paragraph 7 of its resolution, i would point out that the commission proposed to the government of equatorial guinea last october to undertake, as soon as possible, a joint evaluation mission to assess the implementation of the activities relating to the consolidation of the rule of law and the promotion and respect of human rights, as provided for in the cooperation programme. the assessment of this situation and its conclusions are a prerequisite and indispensable element for any decision on the continuation of cooperation activities in that country and, in particular, for the signing of the new national indicative programme of the ninth edf. finally, the commission considers it necessary - as some of you have said - to redouble efforts and international pressure at all levels - especially at the international monetary fund, but not only at it - to ensure that oil resources are managed transparently and efficiently, so that the increase in these resources translates into an equivalent improvement in the living conditions of the equatoguinean population.

- the next item is the joint debate on the following five motions for resolutions on chechnya: - b5-0037/2003 by mr paasilinna and mr sakellariou, on behalf of the pse group, on the non-extension of the osce mandate in chechnya; - b5-0041/2003 by mr suominen, mr oostlander and mr posselt, on behalf of the epp/ed group, on the non-extension of the osce mandate in chechnya; - b5-0042/2003 by mrs malmström, on behalf of the eldr group, on chechnya; - b5-0047/2003 by mr staes, mr wuori, mr schroedter, mr cohn-bendit, mr isler béguin and mr hautala, on behalf of the green group in the european parliament, on the situation in chechnya; - b5-0049/2003 by mr ainardi and mr vinci, on behalf of the gue/ngl group, on the situation in chechnya.
mr president, mr solana and mr kinnock! it is very good that we have this debate. it is a very complex issue, and developments are so rapid that the situation changes almost daily. a resolution therefore very quickly becomes obsolete. the liberal group really appreciates the efforts you are making, mr solana, to try to bring the eu together. the greek presidency shares the same ambitions. it's not an easy task. at the same time, the eu is so clearly not united. throughout the iraq issue, different plots have been heard from different capitals. perhaps it has never been more obvious that the eu has failed with a common foreign and security policy. despite all the beautiful words and rhetoric, we are very naked, which is so very tragic. perhaps this can be compensated by trying to agree on a strategy for what will happen if there is a war, or if there is no war, after saddam hussein. what will happen to saddam hussein? what role should the eu play in the reconstruction process? how can peace, freedom, democracy and human rights be guaranteed to the people of iraq who have suffered for so long? how will the eu help the humanitarian situation and what readiness do we have to help the neighbouring countries for the refugees already pouring out of iraq? of course, we all hope that war can be avoided. it's clear that the inspectors under hans blix and elbaradei need more time. iraq and saddam hussein are playing a dirty game; you can't get away from the feeling that they have something to hide. iraq needs to step up. it is iraq that must prove that there are no weapons of mass destruction in the country, not the un. the question of war and peace is in iraq's hands. we feel that the inspectors need more time, a sentiment that many others in this room have expressed. when they report back to the security council in a few weeks, it is the un security council that will decide what happens next. the decision on a possible military attack, which unfortunately may be necessary, will be taken by the un. a unilateral american attack would be very unfortunate, not only in view of the consequences for the region, but also because in the very, very long term it would damage transatlantic relations. finally, i must say that i regret that individual members of parliament are going to baghdad on their own initiative. i am convinced that this initiative is well-intentioned, but it will be exploited by the iraqi dictator and reinforce the image of division in the world community. those who leave cannot influence saddam hussein. he has not shown himself amenable to argument. instead, they risk becoming his hostage and getting in the way of the inspectors' work. of course, it is up to each individual to travel, but the european parliament risks looking very ridiculous internationally. i would advise against this trip.
mr president, i would like to thank you. a while ago we had a visit to the european parliament by scott ritter, the former weapons inspector. he asked us to go to iraq to support the weapons inspection. he said that it would show not only to the united states and the world but also to the iraqis that arms inspections were not an american invention, but that it was the only way to peace and that arms inspectors had strong support, including amongst us who are war-haters and peace-lovers. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all vehicles with a maximum mass exceeding 300 tonnes. against this background, it is quite regrettable to listen to the liberals and their attempts to smear and cast suspicion on the thirty or so members of the european parliament who have decided to go to iraq for this peaceful purpose. of course, one can sit in the safety of europe and scoff at us who, in our desperation at the prospect of a great war, try to make at least a small contribution. i find that very distressing. we are for peace because we are against the death penalty as a method. saddam hussein is certainly a gangster, but if we are to have an international justice system, he must not be lynched, and there must not be a collective death penalty for the innocent. in the eu we do not even allow the death penalty for the worst criminal. my friends, we must have an international legal order and not some sort of return to a mixture of the middle ages and the wild west!
mr president, mr solana, i would like to thank you for your reply. war is never something inevitable that you just have to get used to. wars are decided by men and can be stopped by men. war can never be the right way to destroy weapons of mass destruction or to fight terrorism. we are on the verge of a war that would have devastating consequences for the people of iraq, for the entire region and for the entire world. at this stage, we cannot remain silent about the legal and moral problems of war. every politician should analyze what a war against iraq would lead to. millions of people would be forced to flee refugees that neighboring countries cannot take care of. the war could spread to israel and palestine, and the resistance against the west will grow with more terrorist attacks as a result. a very large humanitarian effort must be devoted to the 23 million people of iraq. is it worth the price? the war against iraq is not about saddam hussein's dictatorship or violation of un resolutions. if that were the case, the united states, the un and the eu would be involved in many wars around the world, such as in saudi arabia and israel, to name just a few. it is about the power of oil and the power of the region. the un charter gives the right as a last resort, when all resources are exhausted, to use force to save world peace. only the security council can decide that. however, neither the un charter nor international law provides for the right to use force for preventive purposes. preventive war is the policy of the strong, the strong, which overrides the international law on which the world rests. the eu must respond strongly to this! there is no defense for saddam hussein and his destructive policies. his and all other weapons of mass destruction will be removed, including those of the united states, the united kingdom and israel, but we have never advocated that this be done by force. give the inspectors plenty of time, and use that time to cooperate with the eu, the arab countries, russia and the un to find a peaceful solution.

- the next item is the report (a5-0004/2003) by mrs korhola, on the joint text approved by the conciliation committee, on a directive of the european parliament and of the council providing for public participation in the drawing up of certain plans and programmes relating to the environment and amending council directives 85/337/eec and 96/61/ec (pe-cons 3676/2002 - c5-0598/2002 - 2000/0331 (cod)).
the commission. madam president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your report. i am of course very pleased that this directive, which the commission proposed some two years ago, will now be adopted. it complements the environmental information directive. the eu is thus one step closer to implementing the aarhus convention. the principles of the aarhus convention are very important, because the environment has no 'voice of its own'. the commission's proposal for a directive on the protection of the environment is a very important one. this directive lays down basic requirements for public participation in environmental decision-making. the directive provides the necessary common framework for crossborder cases as well. however, the details are left to the member states. this is important, as public participation must become a natural part of the work of governance at all levels, and the forms must be adapted to the decision-making at the different levels. the directive covers both important plans and programmes to be drawn up under our environmental legislation and decisions on activities which may have a significant impact on the environment. such activities are covered by the environmental impact assessment directive and the directive on concerted action to prevent and control pollution. these two directives are now being supplemented by provisions on public participation and access to justice under the aarhus convention. the directive defines public participation as the provision of information to the public about the activities concerned and their right to participate. the public concerned shall be given the right to express its views on a matter at an early stage. finally, public authorities shall take due account of the results of public participation in their decision-making and inform the public of the decisions taken. the directive also contains provisions on the review of the legality of decisions covered by the provisions. i would of course like to thank the rapporteur, mrs eija-riitta anneli korhola, for her intensive work on the directive and express my gratitude to her and to the members of parliament's delegation to the conciliation committee for the efforts they have made to reach this consensus which allows us to adopt this important piece of legislation tonight.

the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. hiv, malaria, and tuberculosis claim millions of lives each year. at least five million people died in the poor countries last year alone. these three diseases are different in nature, and different types of interventions are required to prevent and treat them. but they do have one thing in common: they are primarily affecting the poor of the world. it is therefore logical to take a concerted approach in aid to combat them. we have been calling for the commission to step up its efforts in the field of health for many years. when the commission presented an action plan to step up the fight against these diseases at the end of 2000, it was therefore a welcome decision. commissioner nielson will be recognised for making the fight against these diseases a priority within the union. the regulation we are discussing here today is extremely important. it sets out the framework for the implementation of the action programme. an important part is the commission's own programme; it is important that sufficient human resources are allocated to make the programme effective. the second part consists of the eu contribution to the newly established global health fund. the commission's proposal for a regulation is, by and large, a good document. during the committee's discussions we have tried to strengthen the texts in a number of areas and to give more weight to the whole issue by raising the budget. we have had parallel talks with the council to try to reach consensus at first reading, an objective which i hope will be achieved. in the areas where we propose reinforcements, the importance of both prevention and support and care for those infected with hiv or suffering from aids is firstly recognised. a powerful effort to prevent these diseases cannot be made in a vacuum. secondly, it is therefore so important that, in parallel with these efforts, health systems in general in the poor countries should be strengthened. thirdly: although this regulation does not specifically support research, it underlines the importance of significantly increasing funding for research into hiv and malaria vaccines and more effective medicines. coordination with other donors is emphasised. the same applies, of course, to the coordination of measures in the field of reproductive health and family planning. in the report we make an important point about the importance of allowing countries that are badly affected by these diseases to import cheap and generic medicines. it is sad to see that this issue is still not resolved, despite long and intensive negotiations within the wto. as for the budgetary appropriation, we consider it insufficient in the light of the needs. estimates have shown that it would take $10 billion annually to combat these three diseases effectively. at present, a couple of billion us dollars are at stake. the eu's contribution to date has been small, but some positive things have happened during the case, including the budgetary settlement in the autumn on the global fund contributions. at the same time, it is clear that the eu's efforts to combat these diseases should not, of course, be guided primarily by what it takes to get a seat on the global fund's board. what is crucial, of course, are the needs of the disease work itself. the budget we are now proposing from the committee is also too small, but we still see it as a step forward that we have succeeded in raising the budget level. together with the european development fund, this will hopefully mean that over 500 million euro will be invested in this area over the next four years. i shall put to the vote an additional proposal which is necessary to bring our decision into line with the new rules governing budgetary matters. i am convinced that we will soon have to discuss further contributions to this work - mainly new, fresh money. i myself was recently in kenya and experienced how badly a poor country is affected by these problems. when the infection reaches the level of 2030 percent of the population of a country, the consequences are devastating. it is, mind you, not only africa that is affected, but we are now seeing a rapid spread also in countries like india and china. these three diseases will be a serious problem for a long time to come. when the new head of the global fund, richard feachem, was here recently, he mentioned that the hive epidemic will not peak for another 60 years unless much greater efforts are made in the form of prevention. 60 years! even today, after 20 years, more than 40 million have been affected. our imagination is too limited to comprehend the consequences of another 60 years of a galloping hive epidemic. that is reason enough to give strong support to today's report.
rapporteur. – mr president, last year parliament adopted a new version of our rules of procedure. rule 63a(2) of the amended text states: ‘when the committee responsible amends the financial endowment of the act it is considering, it shall request the opinion of the committee responsible for budgetary issues’. while working on this report, the committee on budgets was consulted, but at an early stage before the budget amounts had been finally agreed upon. we managed to reach agreement with the council and with the commission about an increase in the budget early in january. ideally, the committee on budgets should have been consulted again at a later stage and i am sorry this did not take place. to remedy the situation so as to comply with the rules of procedure, i am proposing an oral amendment both to the legislative text and to the resolution. i have of course discussed the amendment with the chair of the committee on budgets, terry wynn, and he agrees. thus, a new sentence should be added to the text in article 10 and amendment 42, reading: ‘the annual allocation is subject to agreement of the budget authority on the appropriate means of financing under the financial perspective or through the use of the instruments provided by the interinstitutional agreement of 6 may 1999’.
rapporteur. – mr president, consequently, we would add the following to the resolution: ‘considers that a financial envelope of eur 351 million is compatible with the ceiling of heading 4 of the financial perspective only if the budget authority decides to reduce other policies or through recourse to the provisions of the interinstitutional agreement of 6 may 1999’.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, commissioner lamy, for an interesting introduction to this debate. firstly, i think it is difficult to talk about these two meetings and the debate on globalisation as such as long as the crisis in iraq continues. if that crisis cannot be resolved constructively, preferably non-violently, i believe that our debate on globalisation and our attempts to find constructive solutions to a number of subproblems will be damaged for a long time to come. this debate has not been going on for more than about ten years. i had a little fun the other day looking up the word globalization in a dictionary from the early '90s. there wasn't even one, so this is a relatively new debate. yet, we can see that it goes through different phases. the message from both davos and porto alegre is that what is now at stake is extremely important and interesting, namely to try to define more clearly the rules and the framework within which we will live for the foreseeable future. it is hardly a question of being for or against a market economy, but of the rules and the framework that should apply in a market economy that has now spread to most parts of the world. it is quite obvious that there are a number of shortcomings and problems today. let me ask you a few questions. firstly, poverty and the situation of the poorest countries. it is difficult to see how many of today's poorest countries will be able to compete in the increasingly knowledge-based economy that is developing. trade is important, of course. i agree that we can do a lot to help the poorest countries, not least in the agricultural and textile sectors. we shall do so, but for countries which have almost nothing but raw materials to offer on the market, it is difficult to see how they can secure a stable base in the long term unless we help them in a completely different way with capacity development and capacity building. it is about everything from research and research institutions to helping with education, business development, etc. with the way the trading system works today, and with a number of countries totally dependent on raw materials, raw materials which have also fallen in price, it will be like a boxing match between a heavyweight and a lightweight boxer. i think we need to take a very different approach in this area, a much more generous approach to capacity issues. secondly, the whole environmental problem. the rio conference itself has already shown that the current patterns of production and consumption are not sustainable for a world of six, seven, perhaps eight billion people who would live at about our level. the solution, of course, is not to hold back development in the poor countries, but to develop entirely new technologies. commissioner, i have to ask you. i fear that we are doing far too little to try to help the poor countries make technological leaps. it is an area where we could do much, much more. there is no reason at all that all these poor countries should go through the exact same stages of development as we have in terms of energy production, transportation, chemicals, agriculture and waste management. it must be possible to stimulate technological breakthroughs in a completely different way, but that also requires more targeted assistance in this area. these are just a few suggestions to give substance to this debate on globalisation, its framework, rules and conditions. in conclusion, i would like to say that europe and the eu that is now emerging in the context of the convention must take a strong, leading role in this area if we are to make good progress.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you, commissioner lamy, for introducing this important and complex matter. it is only a little over a week since parliament voted by a large majority in the first reading on a new regulation on the fight against hiv, malaria and tuberculosis in the poor countries. we had been working on this question for almost a year. the debate focused largely on the unequal struggle in the vast majority of poor countries between the spread of serious infectious diseases and the resources available to prevent the spread of these diseases and to help people who have been affected and become ill. a major problem in this context is the fact that so little of the world's health research is focused on the very diseases that affect so much of the world's population. the reason for this, of course, is that a lot of pharmaceutical companies don't see any real profitability in this kind of research. that is why we must look for innovative solutions. the market alone cannot solve this problem, but cooperation between public aid agencies and industry is needed. another problem raised in the debate was the imbalance which currently exists in the trips and wto systems as regards the possibilities of resorting to so-called compulsory licences in specific situations. i am, of course, aware of the negotiations in which the commissioner has been involved. i can only regret that no results have been achieved. this is a very important question. the countries most dependent on these trips exemptions are today in a discriminatory situation and position. i myself think that it would be ideal if the countries of the south which lack production capacity were treated in exactly the same way as countries in europe or north america. the compromise that commissioner lamy is now trying to work out contains a list. i think this list looks good, but ideally, of course, there would be no need for a list at all. if we are to reach a compromise, i think what you have done so far is commendable. i also think the link to the who is an interesting development. i think i can guarantee that you have the full support of this parliament in your efforts to try to achieve a result so that this issue can be added to the record and, above all, so that the whole doha process can continue. since what will hopefully be some sort of solution will have the character of a compromise, i also think it would be quite reasonable to set a time, say in a few years, when this compromise would be evaluated, so that we really see if it works well and if not we can develop it and improve it. in conclusion, i would like to say that it is extremely unfortunate that we have found ourselves in this situation. it is incomprehensible to me that there is actually resistance to this. i can understand the concern about fraud and parallel imports, but it is a general problem which should be solved, and not primarily the health situation in poor countries.
madam president, i would like to thank you. free movement is at the heart of ec law. we must achieve free movement within our union. i therefore welcome a uniform set of rules which can make it easier for citizens to move easily between the different member states of the eu. after enlargement, it will be even more important to make the rules on free movement clear and simple so that all union citizens, new and old, know their rights and the rules that apply. to encourage increased mobility within the eu, we must start by simplifying the rules. the rules must also be generous. i am therefore pleased that the committee on civil liberties, justice and home affairs has proposed rules to facilitate the free movement of different family members. i am particularly pleased that this committee has proposed an extension of the concept of the family. people who do not live in legally traditional family patterns must have the same right to live together as married couples in eu countries. they should have the right to feel confident that the same rights apply to all of us. this is of course a challenge for many in our union, as it challenges traditional thinking. but we must accept this challenge and the opportunity it offers precisely because we live in a common union and want to create a citizenship of the union. ultimately, this is about respect and the equal value of all people. the eu family policy framework must be generous and non-discriminatory.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr campos for the solid work he has done on this report, which has been marked by a great deal of agreement. it is a very important debate that commissioner chris patten is raising here. the european union is now on the offensive and is very active in its relations with belarus in a different way than before. we have a pariah state on our borders. nevertheless, it is important, as a number of amendments from the ppe-de group, among others, point out, that a structured dialogue with belarus be established. chris patten suggests what this dialogue should look like. it is our duty as the european parliament to demand that such a structured dialogue take place. at stake are common domestic and cross-border interests such as trafficking in human beings, sex trafficking, trafficking in women and illegal immigration. it is also about the european union now engaging in structured dialogue and putting pressure on the belarusian authorities, not by promoting isolation, but by outreach and dialogue. i believe that the european union should actually give priority to belarus at the moment. the european union is so divided in its views on iraq that it may be particularly important to give substance to the disintegrated common foreign and security policy laid down in the maastricht treaty in relation to belarus. at a time when we are divided within the european union, we should be rallying around the areas where we can act forcefully. i think we should examine ourselves and ask ourselves: have we had enough power and energy in relation to belarus? belarus, of course, suffers from enormous problems. these include a leader who very consciously rules his country in a dictatorial way, the disappearance of opposition politicians and others whose fate we do not know, such as victor gonchar, anatoly krasovksy and yuri zaharenko. further problems are a lack of freedom of association, which means that the regime tries to control trade unions, a religious freedom law that restricts the right of various new evangelical churches to operate freely in this country, and even politicians who not only disappear but are also imprisoned. however, we must apply a strategy towards the belarusian authorities which means that we say: we will not isolate you, but we will engage in dialogue with you. the isolation that belarus has been subjected to has been self-chosen. let us join forces within the eu in favour of new relations with belarus, with demands for democracy and human rights.

rule of law. we hope that a market economy will be introduced so that the hard-hit belarusian people can enjoy economic prosperity and have confidence in the future. unfortunately, we are infinitely mr president, i would like to thank you. there has been relatively little criticism of the lisbon process. i also think it is right that it should be critically examined. at the same time, we should not underestimate the benefits it has brought. if we look at how we're coping with this recession today compared to the recession we had in the early 1990s, there's a big difference. we have low growth. the reduction in unemployment has stopped, but we are coping much better than in the 1990s. could economic coordination and monetary policy play a role? i think so, at least. at the same time, i believe that the lisbon process must be reformed. there needs to be more coordination in the process. there is a need for coordination in the process and a greater focus on implementation. in this context, it is important that we see these three parts of the process as equivalent. it is also important that there is balance in the lisbon process. i come from sweden, a country which is used to implementing structural measures. we are in favour of an open economy, competition and structural reforms, but we are also in favour of a high social standard, i.e. social systems that provide security and the objective of full employment. if the lisbon process does not strike a balance between structural measures, the employment strategy and sustainable social systems, it will break down and fail. it is therefore important to maintain this balance. finally, i would like to say something about sustainable development. it affects the entire area. it concerns economically sustainable development. it concerns sustainable social systems, not least sustainable pension systems, i.e. both economically sustainable and socially sustainable, so that they are fair. however, sustainable development also affects the environment. the strategic solution in this context is investment. if we start investing in the new technology, the cleaner technology, we will also improve the environment across europe, while increasing growth and creating more jobs.
