- the next item is the report (a5-0021/2003) by mrs malliori, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, on the proposal for a council recommendation on the prevention and reduction of the risks of drug dependence (com (2002) 201 - c5-0270/2002 - 2002/0098 (cns)).
- the vote will take place at the next voting time.
madam president, i would like to thank you. this report deals with the prevention and reduction of risks associated with drug use. the position of us swedish social democrats has been from the outset that the recommendation focuses too much on harm reduction alone. our starting point is that the most important measure for reducing harm is prevention of the abuse itself. this does not mean that we deny the need for harm reduction efforts. however, such efforts must be part of a coherent strategy with the aim of achieving lasting change, aiming at a life free of drugs or other drugs. on this point the committee has accepted several of the proposals we have put forward. it is therefore very strange that the same committee has also accepted a number of amendments in the same report which go in exactly the opposite direction. this concerns the proposals on so-called 'de-cooling rooms', drug classification, positive drug information and special injection rooms. these are proposals which we cannot support under any circumstances. such measures would mean social acceptance of drug use, and we obviously do not accept proposals which are fundamentally contrary to the un convention on narcotic drugs. i think that the recent major survey carried out in swedish schools confirms that a drug policy based on the three components of prevention, treatment and control can indeed be successful. this survey shows that 92 out of 100 pupils in swedish schools have not even tried drugs. this is a result that, in international comparison, is very, very encouraging.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i am deeply concerned, commissioner. to hear our colleague davies is beyond dismal. more and more young europeans are falling into the drug trap. this is going in the wrong direction. chris davies agrees, but more and more eu countries seem to have given up. those who want to legalise, liberalise and divide drugs into dangerous and less dangerous drugs are increasingly heard in this house too. in this parliament, they want to tear down the un drug conventions. that is what we have to hold on to, commissioner. i am afraid that europe's drug policy is on the decline. the malliori report unfortunately goes in this direction as a whole, although some of its points are good, such as the call for drug-free prisons. at the same time, it is completely unreasonable to start talking about the 'positive effects' of drugs, about rest rooms and injection rooms. drug policy must free addicts from addiction, not make addiction a clean sweep. the only way to do this is to combine prohibition, care, prevention and international cooperation. madam president, i would like to thank you. the goal must be a drug-free society. it has to stay. we swedish liberals will vote against the report because it lacks this combined whole.
commission. – madam president, i very much appreciate mrs malliori's report and in particular the many wise remarks in her explanatory statement, which demonstrate a profound knowledge in this field and a positive attitude. let me first make it clear that prevention will always be our priority. the scope of the recommendation is, however, focused on risk reduction. this is not an alternative, but an important other element in the fight against drugs. most of the suggested amendments strengthen the recommendation. i am pleased to say that the commission agrees with 41 out of the 66 proposed amendments. a number of those amendments overlap and redrafting may be necessary for other reasons. those involving financial commitments should be dealt with according to normal procedures. i particularly welcome amendments 12 and 24 suggesting the addition of risk reduction methods related to the use of new synthetic drugs such as ecstasy. strictly speaking, this might be regarded as on the periphery of the scope of this recommendation, as most users of these drugs are not actually addicted. but the use of these drugs is on the increase. some users show a compulsive use pattern and they are frequently part of a network where drug use is very common. as i have said, many of the suggested amendments can be supported, but time does not allow me to mention all of them. there are 25 amendments that i cannot support, not necessarily because i disagree with the thrust of their content, but because they clearly fall outside the scope of this recommendation. i have arranged for a list to be handed over to parliament's secretariat for inclusion in the minutes of this debate for greater clarity and precision. commission position on amendments: see annex. may i remind you that this is a draft council recommendation on the prevention and reduction of risks associated with drug dependence. it does not deal with prevention of drug use in general – that is dealt with elsewhere. this recommendation should be seen as part of an overall comprehensive policy. i would refer the house to other legislation and in particular to the european drug strategy 2000-2004 to which i referred in my earlier speech. i understand the concerns of those who do not support risk reduction methods and those who talk about the risk of false messages. however, i do not believe that we are encouraging drug use by advocating exchange programmes for needles and syringes. others are of the same view as myself. for instance, the surgeon general of the united states has come to the conclusion that well-designed and implemented exchange programmes, as part of a comprehensive strategy, are effective public health measures that reduce the transmission of hiv while not encouraging the use of illegal drugs. however, let me emphasise that the programme has to be both well-designed and part of a comprehensive strategy. i shall finish by stressing that it is our ultimate goal to prevent the use of illegal drugs. but as mrs malliori points out in her report, we do not live in an ideal world with zero drug use - and as long as there are drug users in society, effective harm reduction measures must be applied. the aim of risk reduction is to reduce the problematic consequences of drug consumption, particularly aids and other infectious diseases, by a wide use of measures, all targeted towards a common objective.
- thank you, chief. the debate is closed. the vote will take place at 12 noon.
- the next item is the report (a5-0008/2003) by mrs stihler, on behalf of the committee on the environment, public health and consumer policy, on the commission communication on community and national provisions on breast implants (com (2001) 666 - c5-0327/2002 - 2002/2171 (cos)).
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank the commission for this proposal and congratulate catherine stihler on an excellent report on a pressing subject. it is an area which affects many aspects of health and consumer policy. thousands of women who have been harmed have contacted the european parliament and want us to speak out about the dangers of silicone breast implants. this shows that the people who perform these operations are not always serious and that the information is extremely weak. the image of young women, often portrayed in advertising as having a slim waist and large breasts, is causing a dramatic increase in the number of breast implants among very young girls. the weekly newspapers cut back by showing 'before and after' pictures. so it's high time that we, as responsible politicians, start cleaning up this jungle to protect patients. i agree with catherine stihler that this is only a step in that direction. i think that many more binding commitments are needed for those who perform these implants. the most important part of the proposal, i think, is the 18-year age limit, i.e. that non-medical implants should be banned for girls under 18. the proposal to require surgeons and clinicians to issue guarantees is also important. unlike my colleagues, i think that registration at both national and eu level is justified. when there is not enough research, you have to have a register. i am very pleased with the report. we in the green group fully support the report and the amendments which have been tabled.
in writing. (sv) we, the swedish social democrats, have chosen to vote against several of the amendments to the report on the monitoring of forests and environmental interactions in the community (emphasis on forests). we are very wary of giving community policy a greater role in the forestry issue. we think that the basic conditions within the eu vary so much that it is doubtful what kind of added value such a community policy could bring. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's forestry policy is not disrupted by the introduction of new technologies. there is a danger that, for the sake of monitoring, this would take away significant resources from other areas. in addition, fire prevention measures are already part of the eu's rural development regulation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we did so because parliament had included important formalities concerning the principle of subsidiarity and the importance of the participation of the member states in the work of developing this regulation.
in writing. - (sv) like the ppe-de group, i voted in favour of amendment no 53, which was rejected by 181 votes in favour, 264 against and 11 abstentions. the report would have been considerably more valuable if amendment no 53 had been adopted. the terms 'reproductive and sexual health and rights' would then have been clearly defined with regard to the protection of foetuses by the following wording: 'in the context of this regulation, abortion, which can never be considered as a method of family planning, cannot be considered as a service guaranteeing sexual and reproductive health'. instead, unfortunately, there is now uncertainty about whether aid organisations, at the invitation of the eu and with the financial support of the eu, under the pretext of guaranteeing 'reproductive and sexual health and services', also perform abortions as part of the aid they provide, even though such abortions are illegal in some eu member states such as ireland and portugal. the same uncertainty also exists as to whether this activity is practiced de facto in third world countries, despite similar legal protection of fetal life, i.e. in direct opposition to the legislation of the recipient country.
in writing. (sv) we, the swedish social democrats, have chosen to vote against the report on the prevention and reduction of the risks associated with drug addiction because parliament has adopted a number of amendments which directly or indirectly imply the social acceptance of drug addiction. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the safety of the community's fisheries is not compromised. our starting point is that the most important measure for reducing the risks associated with drug addiction is prevention of the addiction itself. the fact that parliament has chosen to support the committee's view on the importance of preventive action and drug-free programmes in the vote is encouraging, but not enough to lead us to vote in favour of the report.
in writing. (sv) we moderates believe that drug addiction must be fought with rigor. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens and to the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. we do not accept measures proposed in the report which make it easier to use certain drugs. the so-called risk reduction is in danger of becoming a gateway for young people to enter the world of addiction, rather than acting as a measure to protect safety. we also object to syringe exchange programmes because they can be interpreted as consenting to their misuse by the authorities and health services. the report does not agree with the guidelines on the principle of subsidiarity. the development of therapies is a matter for the member states themselves, within their health services dealing with drug addiction. the eu's task, on the other hand, is to prevent drug dependence through coordinated efforts to combat crime, for example in relation to the importation and smuggling of drugs into the eu. we, the moderates, therefore vote against this report.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against the report, which was adopted by 375 votes in favour, 46 against and 35 abstentions. the term 'risk reduction' is contrary to the spirit and the letter of the un drug conventions. it is therefore deeply regrettable that the council in four of its twelve recommendations in paragraph 2 clearly proposes 'risk reduction' as a method. in practice, this is a paraphrase of the key ideological term of the drug liberalisation campaigners, harm reduction, a term that has been criticised for easy to understand reasons. the council recommendation expresses in other words a capitulation to the question of the complete rehabilitation and recovery of drug addicts. the rapporteur stresses that drugs are circulating in prison and proposes that disinfectants be provided for needles and syringes in prisons. i am somewhat critical of this approach. this would legitimize drug addiction in prisons. efforts should instead be directed towards eliminating drugs in prisons. one of mrs malliori's initiatives is to relax the drug crime legislation by introducing alternatives to prison sentences, which is a very dubious proposal. prison sentences are only available for serious offences, i.e. for possession or sale of large quantities. the result of the reduction in sentences would be to send the wrong message to criminals and society alike, that drug-related offences are considered less serious.
in writing. - (sv) this report touches on a private and sensitive area for many women who, for aesthetic or medical reasons, have undergone breast implantation surgery. we support the idea of providing information on risks, alternative methods and post-operative monitoring to all patients concerned. we are convinced, however, that this can be done in other ways than through a compulsory register, which threatens privacy, a measure which we believe is incompatible with our policy. in addition, parts of the report deal with patient-related issues, which are outside the eu's remit. we therefore believe that the problem of breast implants is an issue which - despite its importance - is more appropriate at national level. we have therefore voted against the report as such.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against paragraphs 19 to 21, which state that the european parliament 'strongly wishes that the procedures for breast implant operations be registered in the eu through mandatory national breast implant registers to be established in each member state'. on a previous occasion, in 2001, i had expressed doubts about the relevance and usefulness of establishing national and european registers of people who have applied for and undergone breast implants. the public health sector is a national matter, with national legislative powers. rather than establishing a register to sanction breast implantation as a natural medical activity, it would be better to follow the recommendation in paragraph 19 inviting member states to follow the example of france which prohibits advertising directly to the public in favour of breast implants. it is essential to encourage the development of self-esteem and tolerance as alternatives to breast implants, as points 15 and 16 do. i also support, as proposed, positive campaigns in the member states to 'promote the image of women as they really are and their acceptance instead of imposing an ideal of beauty as the norm, in particular through uncontrolled advertising practices'. we couldn't have written it better!
madam president, i would like to thank you. free trade is basically a positive thing. it is good for development, democracy and growth - growth that brings more prosperity. equally, trade issues always provoke a wide-ranging debate, and there is reason for such a debate. the market must not take over the tasks of politics, and democracy must not be restricted. the requirements of openness and transparency are fundamental. no country should be forced to make certain decisions. neither the market nor other interests should be able to force a country to implement changes contrary to national political ambitions. developing countries are particularly vulnerable. they are eager to attract foreign investment, and international capital knows this. as a result, developing countries risk being put under pressure and forced to accept unreasonable demands. at the same time, we must be able to demand basic trade union rights, to ensure that the environment is not ravaged and that human rights are not violated.

number of proposals to this effect. we took very lightly, ten years ago, during the uruguay round, on the opening up of private universities in some of our member states. this time, we're not moving. trade organisation decisions on dispute settlement. the united states is not moving very fast in these implementations. i went there to speed up that process and to make sure that our intent was fully expressed, including to congress so that they could pass the necessary legislation, because at this point, it's not the administration that's the problem, it's congress. in the event that congress does not act in this direction, which it has not said, the wto gives us the means to concentrate the minds, as they say, in a language that is not mine. mrs mcnally, i have been particularly clear and unambiguous on the substance of the 'public services in the union' part of this negotiation on access to trade in services: nothing in our offer calls into question what is now the public service in the union. as regards the other argument, which mrs van lancker has taken up, namely whether we are not too demanding of a number of developing countries in terms of opening up services, i would first of all point out that many developing countries need competitive services; in most of their economies, the share of services in the community is between 40 and 50%, which is considerable; the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. i would like to say that i am not against the idea of a european parliament, but i am against the idea of a european parliament. we have a very extensive public service model in europe. i am one of those who are in favour of this - we may have different views on this - but i must also be clear that this model is not being imitated everywhere in the world, particularly in a number of developing countries which, for the moment, have neither the intention nor often the means to pay for - if i may say so - public services like ours. in these cases, the opening up of services markets is the best way to manage economies better, make them more competitive and integrate them better into world trade. i will leave it to my colleague viviane reding to reply to mrs fraisse's comments, but i will simply remind her that in all good teams, particularly in team sports, having a good defence and a good attack is generally the secret of a fairly good success and that, from this point of view, the distribution of roles seems to me to be fairly good. to answer your question on sport, mrs fraisse, there is no offer from the union at this stage in the field of sport or recreational activities, for the very simple reason that we have not received any requests in this area. mrs lucas focused on transparency issues. let us be clear about this: you have all been informed of the principles guiding the commission's action in these negotiations. i have spoken many times about the principles we are applying. under the institutional agreement between the various institutions, a number of you (one member per group and, in particular, the coordinators) have had access to the details of my proposals. i understand very well that you have doubts about the detailed content of my proposals in relation to the broad outlines announced and i understand very well that i could be subjected to this trial of intent, which is perfectly normal. each and every one of you can ask your coordinator to check whether or not the details of my proposals are in line with what i have announced. i know that the coordinators have had this information for almost three weeks and no one has come to tell me that the detailed proposals do not correspond to what i had announced beforehand. i'm very comfortable with that because it's not my habit to do otherwise. so, on this point, mrs lucas, there is indeed transparency and, moreover, if i do not make the offers totally public in all cases, it is because there are times when confidentiality is a good thing, either for consultation or for negotiation. this is something that the european parliament knows very well, since it reserves, for example, and rightly in my opinion, the right to confidentiality when it comes to a conciliation procedure which actually involves negotiation. as in any negotiation, there is a time for clarity and a time for discretion. i refer in substance mainly to mr clegg's comments. he is right: as long as the european parliament has not obtained from the convention, as it did not from the previous intergovernmental conference, a serious constitutional power on commercial matters, you will indeed, in this type of procedure which i am trying to manage as best i can, be theoretically deprived of any power in this field. we are well beyond what the ec treaty currently provides for in terms of information and transparency. if you want to go further, which is the commission's position, help us and, from time to time, i would like the interventions of the parliamentarians at the convention on this point to be as vigorous as they sometimes are with regard to the commission. mr abitbol and mr berthu, i would hope that we would not be shouting too much at the wolf, because shouting at the wolf when there is no wolf really does end up scaring people. now, there is no wolf in this case, and whatever you may think and whatever you may verify, those of you who know the case and have had access to this information know that there is no wolf. so it's not about opening some kind of pandora's box. it's not open, it's closed. mr. desire, i understand your precautions. i share a number of them. but let us not pretend that the pandora's box of deregulation is open. it stays closed. it is not a question of liberalising services, it is a question of making progress in opening up trade in services, and this has nothing to do with deregulation, liberalisation or privatisation. let us not, please, confuse two very different debates. on the one hand, the debate we have within the union on the degree of liberalisation of a number of sectors which have hitherto been protected - energy, transport, the postal service - and on how we organise our own society - is a debate which concerns a number of my colleagues in the commission, as well as in parliament and the council. on the other hand, the debate on what happens in negotiations with the outside world. and please do not bring the dockers into this matter, because the dockers are here, to my knowledge, and if i am not mistaken, for a draft directive concerning the internal market. this sector is not on the list, it concerns the internal market, and if we open up, or if we ask others to open up transport services, then our ability to regulate those transport services, as well as the ability to regulate those we ask to open up the market, will in no way - i repeat, in no way - be affected by this type of negotiation. we retain our sovereign right to regulate these sectors as we see fit and therefore this has nothing to do with the draft directive in question. i would like to say a few words about mr herzog's speech, to say that i agree with him. the real debate behind this, the anxiety, the fears, the caution, which i understand, and which is being expressed, is in view of what has happened inside the european union or what will happen in the coming years. i therefore agree with him that we must put the bulk of our efforts into this debate, into what the commission is preparing, and into what will be discussed with the council and parliament on the organisation of the public service, the universal service, the service of general interest within the european union. if our ideas were clearer, and if the agreement between us on what exactly services of general interest are meant were clarified, then i believe that a number of the fears expressed in this debate would not exist, and rightly so. let us now turn to the water issues, which i admit are sensitive. i hear mr turmes, mrs figueiredo and mr karlsson say that what has happened so far with the gats on water is not brilliant. i simply say that it has nothing to do with that. no country today has made any commitment to water distribution under the general agreement on services. not one ! so there is no commitment that has been made by any country. what has happened or is happening in a number of countries is the result of sovereign decisions taken by states in the field of liberalization or privatization, and this has nothing to do with international negotiation on services. many countries, particularly developing countries, have undertaken liberalization or autonomous privatization because they needed foreign direct investment and i think that gats, this type of negotiation, would allow them to attract investment without giving up their right to regulate, which they have sometimes been led to do when they have acted in a wild way. so from this point of view, it is more of a protection. i would remind you that our proposals in this area are only about distribution and, as mr turmes has acknowledged, this does not concern access to the resource. finally, before i give the floor to viviane reding, i will reply to those of you who have asked me to be more offensive: mr de clercq, mr harbour, mrs plooij-van gorsel, mr rübig. i'm not surprised. that is my intention, but i think it must be done in the light of the fears expressed here and there, which are generally the result of a confusion between international negotiations on trade in services on the one hand and attitudes or negotiations within the union on the regulation, regulation or deregulation of services on the other - i think the two debates are very different. however, there are fears and concerns, and we must respond to them patiently and as specifically as possible. the commission remains prepared to do so.
commission - madam president, my colleague pascal lamy has made it very clear that the pandora's box is closed and no one intends to open it in the field of culture, for a number of reasons. firstly, because we want to preserve, at regional, national and international levels, our capacity to protect cultural creativity. for that, we must have the freedom to support our products, if we want to call them that, cultural, of all kinds. secondly, because a cultural product is not the same as a commercial product. culture is something more, it is part of our roots, of our diversity. that is why, and mrs de sarnez is right, i have made it very clear that the gats, the wto, are not the places to discuss culture. that is why i agree with all those in this house who have said that we need an international instrument, for example unesco. madam president, it should be noted that there is no international legal standard of reference in cultural matters today, and i say yes to all those members who have said that there should be one. we need one to avoid the debates we have about culture all the time. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this international legal reference is therefore needed for the promotion of cultural diversity and for exchanges. it is an idea worth studying, which follows logically from the unesco declaration and which we are looking at with interest. my personal feeling is that the viability of an international instrument will depend, in particular, on its ability to advance, at international level, the reference standards of cultural diversity and, in advance, the reflection on indicators of cultural diversity. however, protecting our capacity to act in favour of cultural diversity is first and foremost an internal action within the european union. but those of you who said that we must go further because we have an international responsibility beyond the europe of the fifteen, beyond the europe of the twenty-five, and there were many of you, are right. today the treaty allows us to do so. article 151 (4) authorizes us to carry out international cultural activities and we do so systematically. i can assure you that such a filmmaker from a third country could only show his film when he was invited to european film festivals. we open up to others, we do it. i would like to say to mr van velzen that our erasmus world project is only possible because the treaty allows us to go beyond the borders of the union. yes, ladies and gentlemen, we must preserve this in the convention and in the next treaty. in the next treaty we must be allowed to preserve our internal capacity to protect cultural diversity in all fields and also our external capacity to act at world level, whether to make an international agreement for the preservation of culture, like unesco, or to make projects like erasmus world and to be able to help creators in third countries to express themselves in the forums we have made available to them. so you see that if we talk about culture, we talk about openness. it is very clear that this openness is not only for us europeans, but also an outreach to creators and actors in third countries.

- the next item is the draft recommendation for second reading (a5-0050/2003) by mr jarzembowski, on behalf of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism, on the common position of the council with a view to adopting a directive of the european parliament and of the council on access to the market in port services (com (2002) 01146 - c5-0533/2002 - 2001/0047 (cod)).
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. european industry needs efficient shipping. this is something that the proposal for a directive on port services, as it appears in the council's common position, is well placed to promote. unfortunately, several of the changes now proposed by the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism at second reading, if adopted, will decisively undermine the potential of the directive to contribute to more efficient maritime transport. this is particularly true of the proposed amendment to article 4 (9), which would remove from the directive the provision which would allow industry to manage its own land-based personnel. in this way, shippers will continue to be prevented from loading vessels with their own competent and authorised personnel and using their own resources. if this and other completely unnecessary and unnecessarily detailed regulatory additions are accepted, unfortunately, obsolete structures in ports will continue to exist and will have a negative impact on the competitiveness of maritime transport. we swedish moderates also believe that all port services, including navigation, should be included in the directive. this could be a positive development for european trade, for an efficient, safe and competitive maritime industry and for better opportunities to transfer transport from road to water. many want to achieve this, but very few work for it. a step in the right direction would be to reject all restrictive amendments and to support those which actually strengthen competition. i hope that parliament will do so.
mr president, commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. energy and the environment are two of the eu's most important challenges. both of these challenges are represented when we talk about biofuels and alternatives. action is essential to reduce climate impact and to increase security of supply. the transport sector is one of the most important areas for long-term security of supply. this is strongly linked to environmental work, but the transport sector is also important for economic development and the potential for growth. alternative fuels are very welcome. the use of biofuels can help to reduce the consumption of fossil fuels and to steer development in the right direction. the right direction is to reduce the environmental impact. the introduction of alternative fuels, including biofuels, shall be carried out in a cost-effective manner. the objective of sustainable development must be at the forefront, and long-term thinking must be the guiding principle of our actions. new technology is constantly being developed. today's new technology will be outdated and ineffective tomorrow. with today's decisions we must avoid making future technological developments more difficult than necessary. instead, we should behave in a way that facilitates future technological breakthroughs without compromising security or sustainable development. as has been said, the decisions we take must be long-term. the focus should therefore be on promoting biofuels that have particularly good life cycle characteristics in terms of climate, environmental impact and cost-effectiveness. in business, people need stable conditions to feel confident in their investment decisions. the depreciation of new installations must be possible in a manner that is consistent with a reasonable period of time in the light of technological developments and the cost of new installations. the use of biofuels in pure or near pure form shall not be disadvantaged compared to low mixing. it is also important that each member state can determine the most appropriate form of biofuel based on the conditions that exist. in view of this and the position adopted by the committee on industry a short while ago, we are on the way to a good and long-term position in the european parliament. this position is characterised by the ambition of sustainable development.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. let me make it clear from the outset that the greens are very concerned about the socioeconomic crisis in the whitefish sector and that we are in favour of financial compensation for fishermen who are affected as a result of the measures needed to save and conserve cod stocks. this crisis must not prevent us from taking the necessary measures to save the cod and whitefish stocks in the long term, and that is why paragraph 2 of the resolution is very important. in this context, parliament called on the commission and the council to ensure that changes to the conservation measures for cod stocks are designed to maximise the recovery potential of cod stocks and not to cause further declines in the stocks. we greens have also tabled a new amendment asking the commission and the council to take into account that isis scientists believe that several cod stocks in eu waters are now down to a critical low. i don't think any of the artificial arguments we've heard here will help. it is clear that the fish catch is the decisive factor. new sampling on the swedish west coast also shows that cod stocks are at their lowest level in 23 years and that the number of year-old cod is only one tenth of what it was a year ago. this is worse than we thought. that is why it is also an unprecedented scandal that the eu is preventing us from stopping cod fishing in these waters, which we want to do by means of a moratorium. the catastrophe is a fact. we're standing with our hands tied behind our backs, which is extremely frustrating. i would like to question the commission's approach to this matter.
in writing. - (sv) on the whole, we think the proposal is balanced and good, with the exception of the restriction on self-help, which we do not think is sufficiently justified. limiting, as seems to be the case at present, self-help to shipboard personnel by not including shore-based personnel, for example employed by a port company, means that the legislation ignores the way the sector works. the possibilities for competition in this area are therefore reduced. we have therefore voted against the committee on these points. as far as pilot services are concerned, we agree with the proposal that they are of such a nature that they should be excluded from the scope of the directive.
in writing. (sv) today we have voted in favour of the report on the reform of the budgetary procedure. the report contains a number of important measures in the budgetary field. the commission is proposing to abolish the distinction between compulsory and non-compulsory expenditure. we feel that there is no serious reason for such a distinction to exist. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. we support the proposal in order to make it easier to transfer funds between the different categories of expenditure in the eu budget. this is a measure which is important in creating a system whereby funds are used more efficiently in the eu. the report also points out that the rules governing the eu's own resources need to be revised. we particularly want to emphasize that we support a system which is clearer than the current one and better reflects the amount of money coming into the eu. however, we are completely opposed to a system whereby parliament would end up influencing the amount of money that flows into the eu. it is important that the member states themselves decide on the levels of eu funding, and that parliament has the final say on the money spent.
madam president, i would like to thank you. when thomas mann's report was discussed in committee, there was a great deal of discussion on many amendments. the disagreement was mainly about one area, namely the conditions of existence of small enterprises and their role in employment policy. two different viewpoints were then put to the test. the advocates of one approach wanted to facilitate the existence of small businesses through tax breaks, lower wage costs, reduced bureaucratic red tape, so-called mini-jobs and services that cannot be produced without public grants and subsidies, such as household services. the second approach was completely different. the advocates of this approach wanted to focus on small businesses that have a good business idea, competent management, growth potential and ambitions to grow and recruit. so these are strong small businesses or small businesses that can become strong, that can pay good wages, pay regular taxes, provide good working conditions for their employees and have a good relationship with their trade unions. so there are two different paths, but it is not a question of right-wing or left-wing politics. i know that many in the business world prefer good, strong small businesses and want to provide them with knowledge, capital and growth opportunities. at the same time, this is precisely the path my group wants to follow. in fact, we have too many small businesses in europe. the majority of small businesses are in the countries and regions with the weakest economies and the highest unemployment rates. we have a huge amount of businesses that can barely support a family. we have a large number of companies that start up with the help of government grants and disappear immediately when the grant period is over. in short, we have a situation where statistics show that out of every ten new businesses, maybe only one, possibly two, survive the first three, four years. you have to go for quality. we could unite all the political groups in this parliament around a progressive policy in this area, a policy which takes our overall objectives and the objectives of the lisbon strategy seriously, i.e. 'good jobs for all'. such a policy requires that workplaces, even in small businesses, be of good quality and provide sufficient productivity to generate a social surplus. the report by mr mann contains a number of constructive proposals for such a modern and forward-looking policy for small businesses. i hope that the amendments which point in the opposite direction will be rejected and that parliament will send a clear signal to the commission and the council that we want small businesses, like other businesses, to be of good quality and in line with the lisbon strategy.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank mr thomas mann very warmly for the work he has put into this report and for all the compromise amendments which have created a great deal of consensus in the committee responsible. it is very important for us christian democrats that an employment strategy and a strategy for social welfare in europe combine solid, stable values on the ethical and moral foundation of europe's cultural history with a social market-oriented economy. i would stress that in this context it is a question of both social and economic values, not of a free market economy but of ethical, moral and social values. i would like to draw particular attention to paragraph 37 for the commission. this section emphasises the importance of facilitating reconciliation between work and family life by giving parents more opportunities to spend time with their children, extending childcare facilities, providing parental leave, giving tax benefits to day nurses and foster parents, and providing care and support for older people. this item also deals with the allocation of increased resources for programmes to combat female unemployment. i would like to focus on the issue which is really the greatest challenge facing europe, namely the demographic challenge. i know that we have a commissioner who is personally strongly committed to how we are going to solve the question of sufficient manpower and population in europe in the future. paragraph 37 points to the value of reconciling work and family life and giving more time to children. this report therefore seeks to make the decision of the stockholm european council in march 2001 on the demographic challenge one of the most important issues facing europe. this is very clearly stated in paragraph 37.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? we all know that the ecb in a larger eu needs to be reformed, but the proposal that the ecb has presented is not sufficiently developed. the criticism has been harsh, too. friedrich's report is moving in the right direction, but i, like others here, wonder: why the rush? we should stop, think about it and come up with a well-founded proposal for the next igc. democracy requires that the voting rules be fairly simple and understandable. in fact, even ordinary people should be able to understand them and how they work. the principle should be: one vote one member. the requirement of efficiency, which is important, can be met in an enlarged eu, which huhne mentioned. commissioner, we must avoid an overly divided europe. a division into a, b and even c teams is not acceptable. the ecb must demonstrate greater transparency by publicly reporting on discussions and voting. it is an uncompromising demand. finally, we must also strengthen the role of parliament.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr friedrich, for his meritorious work on this matter. the issue of the voting rules of the governing council is of great importance, especially in view of the forthcoming enlargement and thus also the likely enlargement of emu cooperation. the current rules need to be reformed. everyone seems to agree that reform is necessary. however, it is not entirely clear what it will look like. the report by mr friedrich departs from the proposal put forward by the ecb on 3 february, a model with 21 votes, i.e. 15 governors and 6 auditors, and a rotating system when 16 or more countries join the monetary union. this means that very, very soon not all the member states will be heard. it's going to be a system that not everyone is participating in, but it's still going to be big and complicated. if the decision-making becomes too complicated, this will either lead to the bank not being able to react quickly enough to economic changes or to the decisions not being perceived as legitimate. the system of rotation makes it difficult to enforce responsibility, since it will be almost impossible to determine who or who is responsible for a decision. friedrich's report, which was successfully compromised in the committee on economic and monetary affairs, proposes basic principles: the principle of one vote one member, principles of representativeness, transparency and accountability, and to be a guide in the development of a new decision-making model with greater efficiency but which still involves all members. i think this is important for small countries. it will also be important for the new member states. for me, coming from a country facing a crucial referendum on the changeover from the krone to the euro, it seems right and reasonable to advocate a decision-making model that can be effective and fast in the long term, while allowing everyone to participate in a process that can also be accountable.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. the politicians who launched emu were bold and farsighted. many thought the project would fail, some even hoped it would. but the fears have been put to shame. emu has been very successful. however, further reforms and compliance with the stability pact by the member states are now required. the introduction of the euro was painless, not least because of the enormous logistical effort involved. there have been complaints about price increases, but the general price level has not increased. however, further studies may be needed, as we now also ask in this report. three eu member states are still outside the third stage of emu, namely the united kingdom, denmark and sweden, of which sweden has no special derogations. today's report is about sweden and the commission's biennial convergence report. i believe, as i hope you already know, that sweden should have been a full member of emu by now. when i started work on the report, i would like to thank everyone for their support, but there was still a lot of uncertainty about the swedish government's position. happily, the situation is different now. the swedish people will vote yes or no to the euro in a referendum on the euro on 14 september 2003. happily, the reality has thus eluded both the report and me. sweden has been approved by both the commission and the ecb for three of the five convergence criteria: price stability, public finances and longterm interest rates. however, in two areas sweden is still considered not to meet the criteria. the most controversial of the convergence criteria is the exchange rate, because unlike the others it is so vaguely formulated. erm membership may be considered a requirement, but the duration of membership may be relatively short. the exchange rate criterion has also been applied with flexibility to countries which have already adopted the euro; i am thinking in particular of finland and italy, which were not part of erm ii for two years. the question of erm ii membership is also important and, of course, very sensitive for the candidate countries. since several of them are far ahead in their adjustment to emu, i would still recommend some flexibility. i also noted that the commissioner, in a speech in hungary last week, was of a similar mind. another area where the commission and the ecb consider that sweden does not comply with the requirements of the treaty is the issue of legal convergence. it concerns the independence of the swedish riksbank. the issue has also been raised in an exchange of letters between the president of the ecb and the swedish minister for finance. the ecb expressed particular concern about the lack of clear rules for the transfer of reserves from the riksbank to the treasury. in his reply, however, the minister of finance stressed that the necessary amendments will be tabled when the introduction of the euro comes, including the necessary constitutional amendments, although time may be short in view of sweden's demand for an intermediate election in constitutional amendments. in my view, however, this does not mean that sweden is disqualified for the third phase of emu until 2006, 2007. one of the most important principles of the eu legal system is that eu rules take precedence over national law. sweden needs the euro, i dare say. at the same time, i would also like to say that sweden actually needs the euro. sweden has an orderly economy and can meet the necessary requirements. i can say that, even though i am in the opposition in my country. for me it is a matter of course and a natural step for sweden to become a full member of the whole of european integration and to adopt the euro as its currency and banknote. now it is the swedish people who will decide this on 14 september. the wait-and-see line advocated by some of my colleagues in this house deserves criticism. what would have become of european integration if the guiding light had been the deafness of robert schuman, or if helmut kohl, when the wall was torn down, had been guided by short-term economic interests, or if jacques delors's sign had been fear? it's not a big deal. it is my hope and conviction that i can come back to you, mr president, and to you, mr commissioner, on 15 september with a message: that was a yes. the swedish people want to be fully involved and influence the new and the old europe. in conclusion, i would like to say that there is some confusion about the amendments. i would like to say, however, that i hope to achieve the largest and broadest possible majority. i have listened to the criticisms and can accept that amendments nos 12 and 14 are being dropped because they are covered elsewhere.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to limit my remarks to the 2002 convergence report on sweden and first of all thank the rapporteur, mr olle schmidt, for a job well done. on 1 january 2002, the euro was introduced as the currency of 12 european union countries. the transition was very successful and the citizens welcomed the new currency. concerns about price increases proved to be almost unfounded. as everyone knows, sweden is not one of the countries that have adopted the euro as their currency. the reason for this is, of course, primarily political. however, for sweden to adopt the euro as its currency, we must formally meet five convergence criteria. the commission and the ecb consider that sweden fulfils three of these criteria: price stability, public finances and longterm interest rates. this is all very well, but sweden does not meet the other two criteria, i.e. the exchange rate criterion and legal convergence. as regards the exchange rate criterion, it is obvious that the swedish krona has fluctuated in recent years. however, it should be borne in mind that on the day sweden formally requests a new assessment, this will be made over a different reference period and with expectations that may tend to increase exchange rate stability. as regards legal convergence, it is essential that sweden initiates without delay the change in swedish legislation required for the introduction of the euro. the outcome of the forthcoming referendum must of course be respected, but in the event of a possible 'yes' vote, sweden should, in my view, be prepared to prevent the introduction of the euro being delayed due to a lack of legal convergence. with all due respect for the convergence criteria, the decision on swedish participation in the third stage of emu is a political one. it is a decision made by the swedish people. the swedish people will decide for a long time to come in the referendum on 14 september 2003 which currency will be used in sweden. on the other hand, the maastricht treaty obliges sweden to join emu fully when the convergence criteria are met. sweden does not have a special clause like denmark and the united kingdom, but hopefully we will get a yes from the swedish people on 14 september. the swedish government will then no longer have to delay or avoid, intentionally or unintentionally, the measures that will enable sweden to meet the remaining convergence criteria. i was pleased to note that olle schmidt accepted amendments nos 3 and 4 of the ppe-de group. this will allow the report to be supported by a wide margin, as the rapporteur wished and as i also wish.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as you will understand, olle schmidt's report is being followed with great interest in sweden, which is to hold a referendum on the introduction of the euro in september. paragraph 6 of this report states that the introduction of the euro is a treaty-based obligation for all member states, including sweden. paragraph 5 states that referendums must be held and that the result of these referendums must be respected, even if they reject the introduction of the euro. this seems very contradictory. olle schmidt hopes that the swedish people will vote in favour and thus resolve this contradiction. however, there is little to suggest that this is the case. on the contrary, the no side is currently leading very clearly, by the last count, by 10 percent. the trend is that that lead is getting stronger. it may also be of interest to parliament to know that among the 'no' voters are several ministers in the swedish government, including the minister for economic affairs, leif pagrotsky, at least two former heads of the swedish riksbank and a long list of highly respected economists, all of whom believe that sweden should vote 'no'. the situation does not reflect the classic notion of an educated, pro-eu elite and an ignorant people. it is a political debate, with a need for objectivity and arguments on both sides. sweden has a well-managed economy. during the years that sweden has been outside the euro, it has strengthened in relation to the economies of the euro countries, in almost every respect. swedish businessmen often claim that the euro is good for trade. the magazine business world recently showed that this is not correct either. the pro-emu economists cannot deny this fact. they therefore say, as grönfeldt bergman recently said, that the arguments for emu are not economic but political. it does not sound very convincing when it is economists who say this, as if they were arguing against practical economic sense to join this political union. it is not easy to sell this message to the swedish people. the truth is that emu has not been the success that was expected. the stability pact is facing increasing criticism and economic stagnation is increasing. for sweden, the german economy is of particular importance, but it is a difficult task to convince the swedish people to make their future dependent on the german economy at the moment. this is not just a swedish issue, but also one affecting the united kingdom and denmark. we will soon also have a number of new member states which are also outside the euro area. in these countries too, there may be referendums on the euro, and the results may be negative. in that situation, you have to ask yourself the ultimate question: what will the eu and the european central bank do if sweden and perhaps other member states choose to remain outside the union? can a euro community be conceived as a voluntary community? what do the commission and the council really say about this? (the president cut the speaker off)
mr president, i would like to thank you. my home country, sweden, is holding a referendum on the euro this autumn. although we are not yet in the euro area, we are actively trying to meet the convergence criteria and respect the rules of the stability and growth pact. the commission and rapporteur olle schmidt point out that sweden fails to meet the requirements on the exchange rate and the legislation on the riksbank. on these points i believe that the demands will be met quickly if the result of the referendum is a 'yes'. our respect for these rules is not only because they exist as an external requirement, but mainly because we ourselves see that they are beneficial to sweden's economy, which has also steadily improved. today we have relatively low inflation and unemployment, and stable public finances. and we have higher growth than many other countries. basically, these rules are good. it would therefore be dangerous to start disregarding them and budgetary discipline as soon as some of them get into trouble. it would erode confidence in the euro and the emu. however, contrary to the line taken by the ppe-de group in certain amendments, there is reason to develop the rules of the pact in the long term. let me give you a few examples, including price stability. of course we will stick to the inflation target, but perhaps the 2% requirement could be applied, for example, as an average for a business cycle, so that inflation could be 1% one year and 3% another. this would foster growth and increase flexibility. the same is true of the public finances. the commission has put forward an interesting proposal, namely that countries with low public debt should be given greater opportunities to borrow for long-term investment. i think that would be good. let me say finally that, for political and economic reasons, i hope that sweden will soon join the euro. if we can strengthen and unite europe in a situation where the dollar and the us dominance is so strong and at the same time reduce the risk of new currency and interest rate crises, then i think that the swedes should of course vote yes to the euro. i thank mr olle schmidt in particular for his excellent report, for which i shall be voting in favour.
mr president, i would like to thank you. my comments are solely on olle schmidt's report. i would like to congratulate him on this work, even if i do not share his conclusions. he has not only succeeded in responding to the commission's convergence report on sweden, but he has also made a contribution to the emu debate in sweden ahead of the september referendum. i would ask my colleagues to pay attention to what is said in the report. i find some of the content both inaccurate and shocking. first, olle schmidt writes that prices have not risen in the countries that have adopted the euro. according to both wim duisenberg and a report in the swedish daily dagens nyheter, prices have risen with the introduction of the euro, contributing to a 0.2 per cent increase in inflation in the euro area. secondly, schmidt gives a wrong picture of the swedish economy. he claims that sweden has fallen behind in terms of trade because we have not adopted the euro. he does not mention, however, that sweden has higher growth and lower unemployment than most euro countries, without sweden having either high inflation or a budget deficit. what i find striking, if not serious, in the schmidt report is paragraph 6. he wants to make the electorate feel powerless by the content of this paragraph, by stating right now that sooner or later we must become members of the euro area. in this way, it is a party contribution to the emu debate in sweden.

mr president, mr president of the council, mr commissioner! the question is not about the hateful regime, but about the best and most effective way of disarming iraq. the regime is obviously hateful - mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? there is a lot to be said about the future consumer policy of the union, but in the short time available i shall try to stick to one point relating to the patrie report. we all agree that the precondition for a free and fair market is that consumers have access to good and accurate information. this is relatively easy to satisfy in terms of the 'old' consumer demands, namely the link between price and quality. it is also ok when it comes to declarations of contents, wash markings, etc. the modern consumer, however, is a different kind of citizen. this is of course also true at the time of purchase. this means that today's consumers demand many different types of information. people want answers to questions about the ethical, ecological and social aspects of shopping. added to this is a variety of health claims of a more or less strange nature. how are we going to ensure that legislation meets all these needs of strange labels that are nevertheless part of the debate and must be respected? we must probably use the principle known in swedish legal parlance as the principle of fairness, i.e. that one may make claims about one's goods but that one must be able to prove fully that the claim is true. i think it's the only way to deal with this new brand. it's a general rule: do what you want, but you absolutely must not lie.

mr president, i would like to thank you. gislaved, bengtsfors and skövde are some of the swedish towns where many people have experienced the destruction of their lives as a large number of jobs have been moved to other eu countries. through dubious and sometimes illegal state aid, tax money has been used to move jobs around the european union. one way of defending fair play would be for the commission - in writing. - (sv) the moderates voted today in favour of the resolution on the closure of firms which have received financial aid from the european union. the report also calls for the commission to take a more active role in the fight against fraud and for the commission to be able to take action to combat fraud. we believe that the structural funds lead to a cumbersome and cumbersome system of subsidies which, when used to help businesses, leads to a shift in unemployment between eu member states and distortion of competition for individual businesses. to create more jobs, we need to deregulate, reduce taxes and remove barriers to trade. if the european union is to have any chance of achieving the lisbon objectives, structural fund aid must be phased out as soon as possible.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in recent months the common foreign and security policy has clearly shown itself to be devoid of any substance. it might seem that it should be possible for the members of the union to stand shoulder to shoulder when democracy is challenged by dictatorship. it may seem that the members of the union should be able to unite in the choice between freedom and oppression, but unfortunately a number of member states have made it clear that in their foreign policy they are not guided primarily by what can be considered right and proper. it was very gratifying to see the majority of the new member states taking such a strong stand for the values which form the basis of our european cooperation. it was equally sad to see former members react to this position with threats and blackmail. you might ask yourself: what right do we have to demand that countries which have experienced decades of dictatorship abandon their democratic ideals? it will take time to heal the wounds inflicted on european cooperation in the weeks and months that have passed. nevertheless, we must work more determinedly than ever to build a union that can speak with one voice in the world and act with an agenda. but it must be a voice and an agenda that we can be proud of, a voice that speaks for freedom, democracy and human rights, not just on our continent. the first thing we can do is to help build a liberated iraq, both financially and with practical assistance in building the first democracy in the arab middle east.

mr president, i would like to thank you. it has been several years since the lisbon objectives were set. since then, many fine words have been said about the importance of the lisbon process. the goal was to catch up with the united states economically by 2010. unfortunately, it's mostly been about words. i have not yet seen the necessary labour market reforms which are a condition for growth and therefore also for more jobs. equally important liberalisations of the gas and electricity markets have also not been implemented. a comparison with the united states clearly shows how far we are from achieving the objectives set in lisbon. the economic situation in the united states is considered very worrying, despite the fact that growth there is much higher than in the eu. the united states has clearly shown that it will not sit idly by and watch the economy deteriorate. they have responded with massive tax cuts. the eu member states should also reduce the tax, and urgently. if a country like the united states with relatively low taxes can reduce taxes, then it is reasonable that eu member states, where taxes are consistently much higher, should be able to do so as well. the european council discussed an employment strategy, but no employment strategy will ever lead to more growth and lower unemployment. there are no shortcuts. we don't need any more fancy words or proud declarations. we need action. as long as the eu talks and the us acts, the economic distance will continue to increase to the benefit of the new world. the year 2010 is constantly moving further and further into the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. nistico is not here, but we are dealing with the twenty-third amendment to this directive, which restricts the use and placing on the market of dangerous substances and preparations classified as carcinogenic, mutagenic or toxic to reproduction. of course, my group and i support the addition of 25 more dangerous substances to the list of prohibited substances. i am also the rapporteur for the twenty-fifth amendment to this directive, which adds a number of additional substances. it is not at all the controversial aspect of this recommendation, but rather the lack of it and the shortcomings of the current legislation. the term substances and preparations does not cover the use of c/m/rhenes in consumer products, i.e. in the products with which we consumers come into contact in the first place and through which we are exposed to these dangerous substances, for example when we buy toys, textiles, a new floor, etc. the directive does not therefore cover the main source of our exposure to carcinogens and dangerous substances. at first reading, the european parliament asked the commission to extend the directive to include the sale of consumer products, and we asked for a proposal for 2002. in the common position, the council states that a full risk assessment is needed before restrictions on products can be introduced. in addition, the council makes the astonishing argument that this will be very difficult, because there are so many products involved. that is precisely why a directive is needed, namely to ensure that people are not exposed to carcinogens via consumer products. the compromise we are now going to take up, and which is the recommendation of mr nistico, is a compromise which i believe is totally contrary to the new chemicals policy we have adopted, which proposes that c/m/rbenes will not be allowed to be used in products unless they have been shown to be safe, i.e. the so-called reverse burden of proof. what we have now in the result that has been achieved is no reverse burden of proof and restrictions only if it can be shown by a risk assessment that there is a risk, that is to say, precisely the policy that all the institutions have acknowledged is not working to protect people's health. unfortunately, it will be many years before the chemicals policy is put into effect. we needed to take action on c/m/n in consumer products immediately to protect human health. the green group will therefore not vote in favour of the recommendation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by congratulating all those who have been involved in this work, caudron, sandbæk, the commission and the council. this is a much-needed action programme. we have promised developing countries that we will eradicate poverty there, which is not possible unless we defeat diseases such as hiv/aids and malaria. i think the proposal has improved in the way it looks now after the debate in parliament. the commission proposal placed too much emphasis on the purely medical aspect. however, like other speakers in the debate, i believe that prevention and action before the disease has broken out is crucial to success. the question of availability and prices is, of course, incredibly important. however, treatment with so-called 'brake drugs' is a very complex medication which also requires a functioning health care system; we must not forget that. finally, i would like to ask the same question that sandbæk asked the commission: has any work already been started? what have the member states done? what has the commission done in practice? this must not be just beautiful intentions and beautiful words on paper, but concrete action is needed now!

in writing. - i voted in favour of the report. this is because fraud against the eu amounts to around €1 billion a year, and this figure is increasing every year. the exercise of criminal law is hampered by the diversity of national systems. in addition, it is clear that member states do not sufficiently pursue fraud. we have to do something. this is why it is important and necessary to create a prosecutor's office to fight financial fraud against the eu. i voted against point 1 on '... common rules in the field of criminal law and criminal procedure in the european union'. the same applies to point 4: 'the european parliament insists that a european judicial area be created in addition to the european area of freedom'. in both cases, the requirements go too far and interfere with the exercise of justice by the member states. the seventh indent of point 21 allows the current system "to be extended, if necessary, to other crimes". the wording allows the mandate of a future european prosecutor to be extended to include new areas of competence, other than financial crimes against the eu. apart from the fact that the proposal is very vague in principle, it must be radically amended before it is submitted.

commission. – madam president, it is particularly appropriate that we are having this fairly in-depth discussion in parliament on this issue today, world health day. i shall respond to a number of issues which you have raised. specifically, mr whitehead asked me about the need for a rapid response system. we have such a thing in place – the communicable disease network – which does valuable work in this area and has been doing valuable work in response to this particular outbreak of sars. we are in very close contact with the world health organization, not just from institution to institution. since last year, a transfer of experts has taken place, with some who people working with us and some of our people working with them. this assists greatly in the transfer of information and knowledge between the institutions. the who has a team presently in place in china, carrying out the important work of determining what further needs to be done. i will review all of these issues with dr brundtland, the director-general of the who, when i meet her at my next meeting on 6 may. it is also particularly appropriate that many of you asked about the extent to which we may need to extend the competences at eu level. i am a little more optimistic about this than some of the speakers in this debate. i have discussed this with a number of members of this house, and with a number of government ministers, for quite some time now. i am happy to say that this idea has found its way into a number of different fora, not least in the committee chaired by mr katiforis in his report that was presented to the convention some weeks ago. that report contained a number of recommendations, the weight of which was that there should be something in the treaties creating an extended competence for public health which would provide us with a legal base to bring forward harmonising legislation in the area of public health. as someone said earlier in the debate, we have far greater powers in relation to animal health than to human health. i know that a lot of people in the house are concerned about this. i am also concerned about it. we need to change that. i have raised this matter a number of times at commission meetings. i am very happy to tell the house that it is commission policy that we pursue this issue in the convention. we have already pursued it in mr katiforis' committee and elsewhere. one of the issues i am currently working on in my own directorate-general is the shape of a new article 152, which it would be desirable to include in a new constitutional treaty; this would provide the kind of legal base which, as many of you have argued here this evening, is necessary to establish a sufficient base for harmonising legislation in the area of communicable diseases and other areas. i have some cautious optimism that the convention will take these ideas on board, that they will find their way into a final report and ultimately be discussed in an igc and, i hope, eventually take their place in the final shape of the convention that we ultimately put before the people of europe.
- this debate is over.
- the next item is the recommendation for second reading (a5-0083/2003) by mr grosch, on behalf of the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism, on the initial qualification and further training of drivers of certain road vehicles for the carriage of goods or passengers, amending council regulation (eec) no 3820/85 and council directive 91/439/eec and repealing council decision 76/914/eec (com (92) 0403 - c5-00615/2002 - 2001/0033 (cod)).
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to begin by thanking rapporteur grosch for the excellent cooperation he has shown during both the first and second reading. this directive is important for several reasons. i'd like us to do a thought experiment and imagine that we would build a road transport system today. would that transport system then look like today's transport system? i don't think so, you know. we would have much higher safety standards, we would build roads differently, and we would definitely require a lot of training for those who work on the roads. that is why this directive is important. firstly, we have the opportunity to impose certain requirements on those who work on the road for the transport of goods and passengers, where we must impose high standards of safety, among other things. secondly, this directive will raise the status of the profession of road workers. i think that means a lot, because the quality is guaranteed by the people who do the work. thirdly, safety is obviously improved by well-trained drivers, because they are more aware and more aware of the consequences of different situations. this is also reflected in the directive. i and the pse group have the ambition to avoid compromise on this directive. i would have liked to see training during working hours. we have tabled amendments to that effect, but unfortunately they have not been adopted. after careful consideration, we have accepted that you can choose between tests and courses. we know that there are a great many people to be trained. what is important now is that we have quality education and that there are authorized training centres. it is therefore important that amendment no 8 is not adopted. the amendment would mean that we risk social dumping, dumping the conditions of training, i.e. choosing the courses that are fastest and in the place where they are easiest. if we accept that there should be a choice between tests and courses, we cannot, unfortunately, accept that there should also be a choice of the country in which the course is to be taken. i can't do that. it would be one too risky. i therefore do not think that we can accept amendment no 8. in the end, i hope we can avoid compromise. i understand from the commissioner that the road safety programme will be coming quite soon, for which i am very grateful. we have been waiting for it for a long time and will read it with great interest.

and with that i answer the second question you have raised. it is primarily from the point of view of safety that the directorate-general for transport can approach these kinds of questions. this directorate-general deals with security aspects, not with social aspects, except in so far as they are related to security. in this particular case, if only social issues had been dealt with in parliament, it would have been the committee on employment and social affairs and not the committee on regional policy, transport and tourism. i hope that tomorrow there will be an agreement to allow this text to be adopted at second reading, to improve safety and to avoid unfair competition in road transport. i would like to thank all those who have spoken, the rapporteur and those who have announced the withdrawal of the amendments which will allow this agreement to be concluded tomorrow, and i take note of the reflections which have been made to me in the course of this discussion on future commission proposals.
