in writing. - (sv) we wholeheartedly support paragraphs 46 to 53 of the report, which seek to highlight the high costs of the european parliament's travels between brussels, luxembourg and strasbourg. as the rapporteur points out, parliament's travel between brussels and strasbourg represents an additional expenditure of some eur 169 million per year for taxpayers. in addition, they pose serious problems of efficiency in parliamentary work. the solution would be to concentrate parliament's activities on one single workplace. the member states must therefore agree and amend the treaty so that parliament can decide on its own seat. in this context, the european convention can play an important role in advancing the debate on the seat of the parliament so that real change can be brought about.
mr president, i would like to thank you. in the vast majority of cases i am prepared to support a compromise for the common good of the european union, to listen and to take stock, but not today! i should like to point out that there are two distinct lines in the liberal group. there's a limit to compromise. i think that the buitenweg has crossed that line. there is no reason whatsoever to call for changes or a review of the un conventions on drugs. it is not the conventions that are wrong, but the member states' unwillingness to tackle the growing drug problem. to those colleagues - there are some even in my own group - who say that i am rigid and do not realise the importance of change, i want to say that i may be rigid, but i also know what is behind this resolution. we are in the business of politics, mr. evans. you don't have to be einstein to understand that. a visit to amsterdam's glamorous neighborhoods earlier this year made me even more convinced. the fact is, mr evans, that those who are pushing for a review of the un conventions are the same people who want to legalise and liberalise drug policy. so capato's rhetoric sounds completely false. we know what he wants. we also know what buitenweg wants, namely to legalise both light and heavy drugs. do not forget that the rest of us in this chamber are backed by millions of europeans! over a million people have signed the so-called vienna declaration, a no to the erosion of the un conventions. the message we must send to vienna and to all the young people of europe, including those who are sitting here applauding, is: no, we don't want to legalize! no, we do not want to liberalize! no, we have not given up the fight for a drug-free europe! reject the resolution.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i and the swedish social democrats believe that the un drug conventions should form the basis of the eu's drug policy, and we want this basis to remain. it is one thing to evaluate efficiency, but to suggest that well-functioning international conventions should be abolished is to go too far. that is what is happening in this report. i also see no reason for reclassifying preparations, as the report also calls for. we must remember that even today, under un conventions, scientific evidence of medical benefit and legal use are required. i therefore do not support this report as it stands today. for me, as an elected representative, it is an important mission not to give up in the fight against drugs. it is sometimes difficult to discuss these issues because we are far apart in the use of the tools, as was reflected in the debate today. let me explain my position and that of the swedish social democrats: we believe that decriminalisation or legalisation of so-called 'light drugs' would be directly harmful, as they lead to the use of 'heavier drugs'. we know this from experience, and i don't think anyone here can disagree with that. we support a drug policy with three parts, each of which is important: the first is prevention, the second is control policy and the third is treatment. the first part, prevention, is the most important and that is where the emphasis should be. that is why we cannot dismantle the international conventions on prevention which we have and which promote a sound drug policy. the second part, control policy, is also important, but it is only one of three parts. treatment and rehabilitation are essential if we are to have a humane and effective drug policy aimed at stopping demand, proliferation and crime.
mr president, i would like to thank you. malliori recently took the first steps in the european parliament to open up the minds of those who might lead to the legalisation of drugs. now advocates of legalization are going further and challenging existing international agreements. there is therefore reason to support the wise policy pursued by the european parliament. the buitenweg report calls for a review of the un conventions and the reclassification of drugs. the long-term objective seems to be a free market for some drugs, then for all drugs, and then decriminalisation of drug use. neither i nor the ppe-de group can support this. buitenweg presents this in a very intelligent way. it is only when you study the motivation that you see that it is really about the legalisation of cannabis and that the aim in the long run is to legalise all drugs. we can read that in various places. the tone of those advocating legalization has changed and been sharpened in recent years. that's more reason to worry. harm reduction sounds innocent, like harm reduction, but if it's used in the wrong way, it can become a tool for those who want to legalize. it is not just about methadone and injection programmes, but also about safer use of drugs, as if there were such a thing! pressure is mounting on young people and families. no wonder most parents are opposed to the legalization of cannabis. the best way for society to help is not to increase the availability of drugs of any kind. the eu is making a major effort to combat drug trafficking. then we shall not open the door behind us to another drug trade. i have three pieces of advice for governments and the commission ahead of the vienna summit. first of all: it does not touch on the un conventions but instead works towards their full implementation by all member states. secondly: let health care and treatment remain national competence. on this point, i am obviously on the same page as davies. respect the different experiences and traditions of the member states in this field. thirdly: the availability and adequacy of personalised care and rehabilitation services is a better way out of addiction than the reclassification of different types of drugs. market instruments should be used for what you really want to develop. it is not about more drugs or more addicts, but about more effective treatment to get rid of drug problems.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the word 'drug' in the wording of the directive. will he find the solution in the netherlands? is that where the great role models are? is the netherlands an example for the rest of europe? the scourge of drugs is too serious to be left to individuals who are not prepared to take full responsibility for present and future generations. my question to many of you in the pse group and to the majority of you in the verts/ale group, the eldr group and the gue/ngl group is what support are you prepared to give to the parents when a teenage girl or boy tells his mother and father that 'the wise politicians have decriminalised this, society has no objection to it'. what support do you offer the mother, mr. de rossa and mr. evans? what support do you offer the father, mr. de rossa and mr. evans? what's your message? is the dutch model the new fashion? is this the outward-looking, the forward-looking model? there are so many different terms in circulation. imagine the concrete situation of a family and the message it conveys to its children and young people! attempts to legalise drugs constitute a serious breach of the content of the un conventions on drugs and the un convention on the rights of the child, in particular article 33 of the latter. and i quote, "i was a little states parties shall take all appropriate measures, including legislative, administrative and social measures for information purposes, to protect children from the illicit use of narcotic drugs and psychotropic substances as defined in the relevant international treaties and to prevent the exploitation of children in the illicit manufacture and trafficking of such substances. my question to the rapporteur, mrs buitenweg, is how she thinks dutch policy protects children's rights, how it gives dutch mothers and fathers the strength to say to their children and young people: fight the drugs! stand up to hashish! i also wonder how the netherlands is a model for the rest of europe. evans also raises the question of whether hashish is comparable to alcohol. obviously the message is that hashish should become as culturally acceptable as alcohol is today, not least in britain. does this mean that you want hashish to become part of the culturally accepted drug use? not only do the swedish christian democrats reject this, but a majority of the members of the european parliament will also reject it tomorrow.

mr president, i would like to thank you. this is truly a historic day. i am proud to stand here in this house on the solemn occasion when we vote to invite ten new countries to join our european community. this is a historic day for europe. for the first time, we are uniting almost the entire continent by peaceful means, by agreements, compromises and treaties, not by war and conquest. it is a historic day for the european parliament, which has been pushing and supporting the whole enlargement process for so long. we are going from 15 to 25 member states, from 626 to 732 members. in just one month's time we shall have a first taste of how this will go, when our future colleagues come here as observers. it is a historic day for the liberal group as well. we have been passionately committed to enlargement. we started inviting future colleagues as 'virtual members' to get to know our work here in parliament three years ago. perhaps we are the only group in this parliament that unanimously votes a strong yes to all ten countries, of which i am very proud. we are happy to welcome our friends as colleagues and to work with them for a better future for the citizens of europe. a united europe offers us enormous opportunities to work for peace, stability and democracy across the continent, to combat environmental degradation and crossborder crime and to increase the well-being of all our citizens. the new countries will bring fresh eyes and ideas to improve cooperation, giving it a new dynamic. moreover, the convention will give us a constitution within the institutional framework to do so. although it may be a little chaotic at first, something very good will come of it, if we are patient. a famous swedish poem is called "chaos is god's neighbor", and it's very appropriate today. the eu will be in a constant process of enlargement for many years. that's why we might as well get used to it. of course, we are concerned, as are other colleagues, about this debacle in the way the council handled the budget and the interinstitutional agreement. i would like to thank the colleagues who have made it possible to find a solution. but this has never been an argument for voting against the candidate countries. while we regret that we in europe have not been able to agree on a common line on the iraq crisis, it would have been deeply immoral to hold the citizens of the candidate countries hostage to what their governments have done or said. how can we ask the applicant countries to take a line with the eu when there is no eu line? although we all vote yes today, we are not uncritical. there are still some problems and shortcomings in the candidate countries, for example in respect for minorities and the fight against corruption. this was the point made by commissioner verheugen. we also deeply regret that it was not possible to reach an agreement on cyprus, so that a united island could have become a member. we assume that all candidate countries will use the time remaining until full membership to work on improving the rule of law, minority protection, the fight against corruption, environmental issues and efforts to combat widespread trafficking in human beings. after enlargement, we expect there to be mechanisms to ensure that all member states, new and old, follow and respect the values that the eu stands for. liberals will never be silent wherever there is a violation of human rights and the rule of law!
mr president, i would like to thank you. the european union is not only a historic moment for the eu, as it will be enlarged with ten new member states. this fact also raises the question of what our common value base is. i would venture to say to this house that the common basis of values was actually expressed in the way in which communism and dictatorship in the former eastern europe were dissolved. in fact, communism and dictatorship were finally dissolved without violence. it is an expression of the fact that we in europe can unite without military solutions. we share a common culture and values. it is based on a jewish, christian spiritual heritage and a humanistic education. this is the foundation of the common construction of an eu of 25 member states. mr president, i would like to mention something about cyprus, which is the country i have been particularly committed to in relation to enlargement to the east. i would point out that the turkish occupation of northern cyprus, 37% of the island, is actually threatening the eu as a peace project. we must never forget the absurdity of turkey being able to join the european union with the continued occupation of a new member, cyprus. we must not forget cyprus and we must realise that the withdrawal of turkish troops from northern cyprus is absolutely necessary if the helsinki decision on turkey's candidacy is to be implemented. it is important that we now contribute to the realization of the wishes of the turkish cypriot majority for a reunified and united cyprus, as expressed in the major demonstrations this year in nicosia. mr president, i would like to thank you. germany and berlin were divided for 28 years. cyprus and nicosia have been divided for 29 years. let's get this over with!

fast on the culture and democracy of athens!
madam president, i would like to thank you. two thousand years ago, the roman cicero said that there are two ways to resolve conflicts, namely, either by negotiation or by violence. the first road is for humans, and the second road is for wild beasts. cicero's words still apply today. do we resolve conflicts as humans by negotiation or those that persist by violence? should the eu invest in active conflict prevention and resolution or should it invest in increased resources to fight conflicts, i.e. more resources for military methods? can the conflicts of today and tomorrow really be resolved by military means? i don't think it was. what lessons can we learn from the war in iraq and the fragmentation of the eu's common foreign and security policy? the commissioner also stressed that the eu should strengthen its international work, strengthen the united nations and strengthen its capacity to prevent and resolve conflicts, rather than devoting more energy and resources to a common military defence which is ultimately a matter of national concern. the eu should insist that the reconstruction of iraq be carried out under the auspices of the un. a belligerent is not impartial and cannot be trusted and held accountable for it. my country sweden is strongly committed to conflict prevention, peacekeeping and crisis management. nor do sweden and i support the idea of a common defence or the joint financing of such a defence, nor do we support the idea of allowing certain countries to take the lead in joint defence work or the introduction of a clause on collective defence. war is too serious a matter to be left in the hands of the military, not even my good colleague general morillon. the key today must be to uphold international law, to strengthen the un and its work and to build up the eu's great capacity to detect, prevent and prevent conflicts. this is the essence of the eu's major peace effort. cicero knew two thousand years ago that only wild beasts seek to resolve conflicts through violence. it is high time for the eu to focus on resolving conflicts, not fighting them.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the report and the proposal for a directive are important from the point of view of health and safety. the rapporteur, mr liese, has added a few more urgent dimensions by also opting to address ethical aspects on several points. first of all: the charter of human rights introduces a new introductory recital on the human body, which reads: the human body is inviolable and indestructible. it is forbidden to make the human body and its parts a source of economic gain. secondly: amendment no 79 proposes the drawing up of a code of conduct, if possible at un level, for the protection of human dignity. the amendment states that principles such as the prohibition of the human body and its parts as a source of economic profit, the principle of informed consent and the prohibition of cloning should be respected. thirdly: amendment no 46 points out that no fetal tissue in the context of voluntary abortion should be granted. this is an important position to take so that a distorted utility argument cannot be used as a pressure when someone is faced with a possible decision on abortion. fourthly, the commission amendment no 31 requires that the use of germ cells, embryonic stem cells and foetal cells be regulated in particular. cells of ethically sensitive origin need to be specifically regulated if this is not the case in the member states. fifthly: amendment no 82 does not include the consent of donors. there is a need for clarification of what should apply to donation and what the mandatory requirements for consent mean. these are important clarifications on the rules applicable to persons who cannot give their legally binding consent and to deceased and living persons. the sixth: amendment no 41 deals with important clarifications and voluntary donations. at the same time, it is the responsibility of the member states to define the rules more specifically. i would like to commend the report by mrs liese.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it has been an exciting journey to produce this report. of course, like my colleagues, i am very pleased that we have a directive to tighten up the rules on detergents, because it is absolutely necessary. however, it is questionable whether this is sufficient, as was also questioned by the previous speakers. my answer is that it would of course have been desirable to have made a statement that was much more forceful than what has now been the result. it is particularly on two points that i see the amendments we have tabled as being of great help, namely amendment no 48 on eco-labelling and amendment no 55 on threshold values for biodegradable substances. as for the latter, the requirement of biodegradability is certainly an important part of this legislation, but the requirement must be that it be set out in an article and not in an annex. it is also appropriate in the light of the codecision procedure and the involvement of the european parliament in the legislative process. the commission proposal provides that if a surfactant passes the full biodegradability test, no further testing is required for its placing on the market. i do not think that is enough. the fact that a detergent has passed the biodegradability test does not exclude the possibility that it may have generated other highly non-biodegradable metabolites that may cause very serious environmental problems. swedish studies have shown that there are more and more chemicals and ingredients in cleaning products than were previously included. it is therefore not sufficient to ensure biodegradability. just a few brief comments on labelling, which i support. the consumer must have a choice. good environmental choices, which the swedish conservation society has really been at the forefront of, have made me believe that we have the absolute safest detergents available in the trade in the world today. it is the consumer who, by his choice, has driven this development. that is why i want you to vote for amendment no 48.
the rapporteur. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. the purpose of this proposal is to amend for the 25th time council directive 76/769/eec on the restriction of the use and placing on the market of certain dangerous substances and preparations. the current chemical legislation states that once a substance or preparation has been classified as carcinogenic, mutagenic or toxic to reproduction, it must be included in this annex and not placed on the market. this proposal concerns 44 new substances to be added to that list. i think we all agree that this is a very good thing to do and that we should of course accept the proposal to add these substances. when parliament first dealt with this matter, we felt that there were two shortcomings in the current legislation which we very much wanted to correct. we felt that we did not need to wait for the new chemicals legislation, but that for the sake of public health we wanted to make changes more immediately. the first deficiency was that the use of cmr substances, carcinogens, mutagens and toxic substances for reproduction, in consumer products was not prohibited. this applies to floor coverings, children's toys and textiles, among others, with which we consumers come into contact on a daily basis and where we are really exposed to these chemical compounds. that's why we wanted a ban. the second shortcoming was that we naturally wanted to ban the use of cmr products in cosmetic products. the council has since rejected parliament's amendments. as regards cosmetic products, our proposal has nevertheless been accepted. in a conciliation procedure it was agreed to ban cmr substances in categories 1 and 2 of the cosmetics directive when the seventh amendment was made. the commission has therefore decided to propose a new regulation on the protection of the environment. as regards the first type of product, there were three main reasons why the council wanted to reject parliament's amendments. firstly, it was considered that there is no scientific evaluation or risk assessment for this. there have been risk assessments. there has also been a proposal from the commission that, since the use of chemical agents by consumers cannot be controlled, safety can only be guaranteed by prohibiting carcinogenic, mutagenic or toxic to reproduction substances and preparations for general use. in my view and in the view of the committee on the environment, there is no reason not to make the same assessment for the use of consumer products. the argument does not therefore hold in that regard. secondly, the council referred to the fact that this concerns a very large, undetermined number of products. to me, that is a completely astonishing argument. i suppose it should be the other way around. if there are so many products containing carcinogens, for example, there is a real need to act rather than simply pointing out that there is nothing to be done because of the quantity involved. thirdly, it is said that this issue will be taken up in the context of the development of the new chemicals policy. it's very good, and i'm really happy about it. i believe that the proposal which may be able to be put before parliament sometime in june on the new chemicals legislation will also take account of these products. however, it will take a very long time for that legislation to come into force, so it would have been good if we had had this amendment already. now, at second reading, it would have been conceivable that we would have tabled the same amendments. i have, however, chosen not to do so, and my colleagues in the committee shared my view. we brought this discussion to a head when the twenty-third amendment was adopted by the nistico report. the same discussion was then held, and it really came to nothing. i have therefore chosen to recommend that parliament adopt this recommendation for second reading on the council's common position on the grounds that our proposals have been accepted in the field of cosmetic products. we have reached the end of the road with the proposals for the second type of products. we'll just have to take it from here.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank commissioner liikanen for his opening remarks. as he says, the price of coffee has fallen dramatically in recent years. we are now at the lowest real price in decades, and it does not seem very likely that prices will increase for the foreseeable future. the price of the raw material itself is currently around 1% of the market price. the average coffee farmer in tanzania or kenya earns at best a few dollars a week. this means that he or she - and indeed it is often women who work in the coffee plantations - has to work for a week or more to afford a single cup of coffee in the bar here in strasbourg. until the end of the cold war, prices were kept reasonably high. we could survive by growing coffee. however, the developments of recent years have been devastating. the competition has gotten tougher. one of the many reasons is that the world bank has helped new countries outside africa to organize large-scale coffee farming. as is so often the case, subsidies, however generous they may seem in individual cases, have damaged the price picture and thus the survival prospects of millions of small coffee growers, particularly in africa. the price race for coffee has led to a widespread crisis for tens of millions of coffee farmers in many dozens of developing countries, where coffee is not only an important source of rural jobs and income but also of foreign exchange earnings. a problem in this context, which was also raised by mr liikanen, is that farming is often too one-sided. this, of course, reinforces the impact of the crisis. as long as small-scale coffee farmers are dependent on a single crop, their economic situation is and remains extremely fragile. the diversification of production thus appears to be of the utmost importance, and there are opportunities which have not been exploited. however, it requires a holistic approach to the farming system. i shall not go into a detailed scientific analysis, but let me give an example: the coffee bush cannot be used as animal feed today. it contains caffeine, which the animals cannot tolerate. interestingly, though, it is possible to grow excellent, nutritious mushrooms from the leftover material of the coffee bush. these mushrooms, such as the shitakis, are highly nutritious and command a much higher price per kilo on the market than coffee does. once the fungi have done their job and sucked up the caffeine, the remaining material can be fed to the animals. suddenly, there are multiple income streams and more job opportunities for the poor coffee farmer. interesting projects are currently under way, including in colombia, to promote a broader view of the farming system. however, as everyone will understand, this requires knowledge from different research disciplines and not a one-sided focus on a single crop. like commissioner liikanen, i do not believe that there are any quick and easy solutions. however, the issue must be addressed and it must be addressed immediately in the context of the entire doha process, and in particular in the context of our continued cooperation with the countries concerned. as we suggest in this resolution, a wide range of measures can be taken in the short term, particularly to help the coffee growers who have found themselves in this very difficult situation. it should be possible to use the large surpluses of the european development fund to increase efforts in rural development, which would then take a more diversified form than a one-sided focus on individual crops. it should also be possible, through dialogue with the big coffee producers and those who sell the coffee, to ensure that better wages are paid to the poor coffee growers. i have only had time to touch on a few aspects of this question. i thank mr liikanen for his comments and look forward to continuing the dialogue.

in writing. - (sv) i abstained in the final vote on the report, which was adopted by 275 votes in favour, 96 against and 11 abstentions. i voted against the vague clause which states that 'the eu's cfsp' must 'be based on credible political, economic and military capabilities provided by the member states and nato'. such a wording could mean that neutral states must provide military capability. it is wrong to propose to finance military interventions with community resources or to apply the method of in-depth cooperation in the military field. this could allow some member states to form a military alliance within the eu. nor could i support the point that 'in the context of future un reform, the european union should be granted a permanent seat on the security council'. at present the eu has two permanent members, britain and france. europe's role in the un could be diminished if these two national voices are replaced by a single european voice. the aim of the report is to bring european defence industries and military organisations closer together. this must not be at the expense of nato, but in the form of europe taking greater responsibility within the atlantic alliance, in particular by taking over the leadership of operations in afghanistan.

in writing. - (sv) the swedish social democrats believe that the community should not extend or introduce new aid to shipbuilding in order to protect its shipbuilding industry against south korea. the european shipbuilding industry is currently in a serious situation, with the construction of certain types of vessels having suffered significant damage as a result of unfair competition from south korea. south korean shipyards have been dumping in the gas carrier shipbuilding sector by charging different prices on the domestic market and export markets, with export prices being 25-30% lower. on the other hand, the eu has cleaned up its shipbuilding industry by reducing aid, with the result that production capacity has been reduced and social problems have been solved. sweden has already abolished these types of aid in recent years. the reintroduction of these is therefore questionable. in general, we are against this type of state aid, but we understand the concern about unfair competition from south korea. we believe that this type of problem should be resolved within the wto and its dispute settlement mechanisms. the continuation or reintroduction of aid granted by the european union for certain types of vessels would be of no use and would, on the contrary, risk leading to an even greater distortion of market mechanisms. in view of this, the social democrats in the european parliament have chosen to vote against any form of state aid to the shipbuilding industry. we believe that this report should not be favoured and therefore vote against the report as a whole.

the committee on employment and social affairs has tabled an opinion. mr president, i would like to thank you. my wife and i have had the privilege of having our three children in a parental cooperative in my hometown of dalarna. a parent-child care cooperative may not be the image of a european cooperative society, but we have very good experience of cooperative work. the regulation on the statute for european cooperatives and the directive on employee involvement are inseparable. the charter deals with the practical operation of the mechanisms for ensuring worker participation in european cooperatives. in the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs we have chosen to make three political priorities. first of all: the european parliament shall require a legal basis guaranteeing its right to co-decision. secondly: the applicable provisions on the right to information and consultation shall be fully respected during the procedure prior to the registration or restructuring of a european cooperative society. thirdly: the rights enjoyed by workers before the creation of the european cooperative society must be guaranteed. mr president, parliament has warned the council that it would not tolerate a repetition of the council's position on the statute for european companies. the council has ignored this warning in this case. in defending the role of parliament in the legislative process and in referring to the parliament's legal service, we in the committee on employment and social affairs have put forward article 95 of the treaty as the correct legal basis. it is a question of approximating the laws of the member states, not of transferring decisionmaking powers from national legislation to the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to take this opportunity to thank mr gebhardt and mr de rossa for their work on these two reports and for the diligent work they have done. we in the ppe-de group have tabled a number of amendments to the statute of european cooperatives. we believe that the procedure for establishing a european cooperative society must be transparent and open as far as possible. the existing provisions on the right of workers to information and consultation must be fully respected. a number of amendments guarantee that the effects on employment of the creation of a european cooperative society will be explicitly stated from the outset. the employee representatives must be given time to submit an opinion before decisions are taken. since the proposed regulation concerns the various stages in the life cycle of a european cooperative society, such as formation, transfer of the registered office and liquidation, a number of amendments are necessary to apply these principles to the various situations described in the proposed regulation. the committee on employment and social affairs, whose opinion i was privileged to be rapporteur for, also believes that the creation of a european cooperative society must not lead to the loss of rights which workers enjoyed before its creation. mr president, let me also say very specifically about the de rossa report that it is important to stress that the right to negotiate on worker participation should be included in the context of the formation of a european cooperative society and in the context of major structural changes. all participation must be an ongoing process. in the report we also call for the removal of the general clause under which member states may be exempted from the rules on employee participation when a european cooperative is formed by a merger between two cooperatives. i would also like to emphasize that the employee representatives on the administrative and supervisory bodies should be elected or appointed in accordance with national legislation.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is twelve years since the commission presented this proposal for the statute for european cooperative societies, and this discussion has, as mr de rossa said, actually been going on for over thirty years. the european parliament took up the matter at first reading in 1993. i was not a member of the european parliament at the time, but i was working as an adviser to small businesses and cooperatives. my colleagues and i could not have dreamed that it would take so long to come to a conclusion on the issue of the statute for european cooperatives. this has in fact put an end to a great many proposals and ideas for cooperation in the social economy in europe. cooperatives are a form of enterprise which should be given full support, not least from the political point of view, because in practice they are a participatory democracy. we cannot wait any longer with this charter. i would therefore, on behalf of my group, appeal to the european parliament and to my colleagues to avoid further delays and not to take issue with the council on the legal basis and our right to co-decision, even though i agree in principle with the arguments put forward on the legal basis. i am afraid, however, that a legal dispute will not benefit the cooperative enterprises and the social economy. there are so many tasks for the social economy in the new europe that we need this charter. let it not be caught up in principles and disputes about the legal basis!

terror. i was one of those who opposed the war, but i cannot help but express my deep satisfaction that a people who have lived under long periods of oppression are now being given the opportunity to shape their own future. of course, everything depends on how the reconstruction is organized. the united states entered iraq on the basis of arguments about iraq's alleged possession of weapons of mass destruction and links to al-qaeda. it was very often stated that economic interests had nothing to do with it. yet now an organization of reconstruction is emerging that is under total control of the americans. the bush administration has already awarded multi-billion dollar contracts for the reconstruction of iraq to american companies. this type of tendering procedure, which i know well from my work at the un, is normally conducted through the un or the world bank, and this should of course also be done now. otherwise, there are no guarantees against arbitrariness in the tendering process europeans. our own contractual legal basis has been created precisely to create stability and predictability in the relations between our states. mr president, i would like to thank you. i understand measures for long-term reconstruction. that is of course good, but it would of course be even more important if the union, under the commission's leadership, were to draw up such a plan. i do not have time to define the tasks involved, but it is clear that they concern in particular actions in the field of justice, health, education, etc. i therefore call on the presidency and the commission to in writing. - (sv) the proposal for a directive on environmental liability is a major step forward for the european environment and should prevent many accidents in the future. this proposal is welcome, although we believe that it should be strengthened in certain areas. the definition of biodiversity to which liability may apply is too limited. we believe that species and environments protected by national legislation should also be included in the directive. the proposal to abolish strict environmental liability for activities for which an application has been made is in our view a far too radical exception which is also at odds with national legislation. this should, however, be one of the factors taken into account when determining the amount of damages.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am the rapporteur for the report on guidelines for the employment policies of the member states. we were also supposed to vote on that report today. we should have had, for the first time, a timely coordination between economic and employment policies, but unfortunately this did not happen. i, as the speaker, worked like a dog to get us finished on time, but it didn't help. this means that this report will not be tabled until the june partsession, and by then it will be too late. this means that the council will decide on the guidelines on the same day as we vote in parliament, and the council will therefore not be able to take our views into account. this is serious, because it means that the vital balance between the two processes is being lost. instead, we get a total dominance of the economic perspective. it is a shift that we see in other parts of political life as well. this does not bode well either for the lisbon process or for the social europe on which so many people in europe have placed their hopes and their trust. the eu is now in a difficult economic situation with weak demand, low levels of investment and rising unemployment in a growing number of member states. of course, we would have needed a clear reorientation of the economic guidelines, but unfortunately the commission continues to praise the stability pact and budgetary discipline and to believe that it is possible to escape the crisis. when you cannot increase government spending and cannot regulate by means of floating exchange rates because you have a single currency, all that remains is the labour market. the measures proposed by the commission are therefore not so much economic as labour market measures, which are primarily aimed at reducing labour costs and the social costs associated with labour, but which reduce domestic demand, which is very important. it is the one that needs to be increased if we are not to enter into a vicious circle. the stability pact was created in its time to keep inflation under control during a period of growth when there was perhaps a justified concern about inflation. today is different. now, instead, the same pact is creating deflation and rising unemployment. emu policy binds all the countries of the eurozone to a common destiny, binds them to a policy of austerity that creates a kind of collective fall. united we stand, united we fall, you might say. on the contrary, it means that today it is fortunate for those countries which have the opportunity to say no to the euro and to remain outside the emu policy. my own country sweden is to hold a referendum on emu in september, and with each passing month opposition to the euro grows both among the general public and among economic and political experts. we in the gue/ngl group think that it would be infinitely better to continue with the lisbon policy, to invest in skills, knowledge and innovation. we would need comprehensive public investment programmes strategically designed to boost demand for private market products and services as well as for education and research and knowledge-enhancing activities. that is the way forward, and if market players cannot do it themselves, then it is up to us politicians to create the conditions that will encourage companies to think long term. you can't save yourself from this crisis.
madam president, i would like to thank you. the report states that the eu is currently in a difficult situation with sluggish growth, falling profits, reduced external demand, reduced business and consumer confidence and rising unemployment. yes, it is a disastrous situation, but the question is what could be the cause. i can agree with the rapporteur that a new approach to economic policy is needed, but what a new approach. neither the commission nor the rapporteur seems to have a good answer to that. could it be because the very starting point of the analysis lacks a reality anchorage? it is written that the eu is experiencing poor economic development. yes, on average it is, but the fact remains that this average conceals a great many different realities in the different eu countries. we all know that the situation in germany is extremely serious, but it is not so bad in the three eu countries which have chosen to remain outside the euro area, the united kingdom, denmark and sweden. in these countries, inflation and unemployment are lower than in the euro area and growth is higher. in 2002, growth in the euro area was 0.9%, compared with 1.8% in the three non-emu eu countries. in the nordic countries, the highest growth rate is in denmark, the only country to have voted against emu in a special referendum. at the same time, the german handelsblatt the other day said that one of the main causes of the german crisis is the high euro exchange rate, which makes german exports more expensive. in sweden we do not have this kind of problem, because fortunately we still have our own currency, whose exchange rate fluctuates in relation to other currencies and is thus continually adjusted to the real situation in the swedish economy. another problem for germany is that in a recession one cannot decide independently on the basis of german needs how large a budget deficit one should temporarily allow in accordance with normal principles of short-term economic policy. the eu is now allowing an increased budget deficit to prevent the economic disaster from becoming total, and is instead threatening to fine the country. all this shows that the attempt to pursue a centrally directed economic policy in 15 different countries through emu and the euro has failed. however, this is not the subject of the report. it gives the impression that the emu and the euro have become sacred cows that cannot be criticised at all within the eu. the rapporteur, while all the facts suggest that the eu countries should be allowed a greater measure of democratic self-determination, nevertheless chooses to dogmatically emphasise that the member states are obliged to coordinate economic policy. we now know that a centrally directed bureaucratic economic policy within the eu is a disaster. there are many tasks that the eu can usefully devote to, such as contributing to peaceful conflict resolution and countering transnational environmental threats. the experiment of imposing a uniform, uniform economic policy for all eu countries has proved to be a failure. this is something i hope the swedish voters will remember when they vote on 14 september on whether or not sweden should switch the krona to the euro.

mr president, i would like to thank you. it is urgent that the european parliament should have a statute governing the conditions of its members. it is also urgent that we have a system that is open and that our voters can review. what has been a stumbling block for us swedish social democrats is above all the flat-rate travel allowances, the level of wages, the community tax and the lack of transparency. we have set out some absolute requirements in order to support a new members' statute: firstly, that we can continue to pay taxes in sweden; secondly, that there will be reformed travel reimbursement systems based on actual costs; and thirdly, that we will have a system which is open, allows transparency and which our voters can review. the proposal before us now contains much of what we have asked for, but it differs from what we want in one crucial respect, namely the possibility of paying national taxes. we shall be voting in favour of an amendment which is aimed precisely at preserving the right to pay national tax. if this proposal is rejected, we will unfortunately have to vote against the whole proposal.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled an opinion on the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you. as rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on foreign affairs, human rights, common security and defence policy, i would like to thank mr belder warmly for his report and his excellent work. we can all see that the eu has a multitude of such frameworks instead of a single one. it is a matter of macro-financial assistance for the balkans and eastern europe. it is structural adjustment aid for non-member mediterranean countries. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a regulation on the establishment of a european union agency for fundamental rights. the committee on foreign affairs regrets that even in the area of macro-financial assistance, the court of auditors' special report 1/2002 has shown that it has not been possible to achieve a clear and consistent approach to the various operations. i would therefore venture to say that the committee on foreign affairs welcomes the fact that mr belder is proposing the introduction of the genval criteria, which will enable the european parliament and the national parliaments to carry out an effective review of aid. this macro-financial assistance shall be granted only exceptionally. it must meet the necessary political conditions and have a clear geographical proximity. it is intended to complement other funding. it shall be conditional and shall comply with the principle of financial discipline. it is difficult to see any valid reason for maintaining the current, difficult to manage and probably unnecessarily resource-intensive eu budget support system for non-member countries. the commission is calling for reform in this area. in conclusion, mr president, i would like to say that macrofinancial assistance from the eu must never be given and must never become a reward for bad economic policy by the government of the recipient country.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it was a pleasure to listen to commissioner vitorino. our work can have practical results, which is needed. we have economic stagnation and growing problems in the labour markets of the eu. the resulting contradictions in terms of 'the right policy' are growing, as we have also seen in this parliament. it has been my great endeavour throughout my work as rapporteur to try to bring parliament together behind a common line, despite the difficulties. the starting point for this approach has been the lisbon strategy, which calls for economic and technological development to be combined with a commitment to quality in the workplace, in cooperation between the parties, in production and, not least, a commitment to solidarity with the weakest groups. we have therefore put forward a number of proposals. i can summarize them very briefly now, because commissioner vitorino has already commented on some of them. firstly, we have proposed that greater attention be paid to unemployment. it's important, you know. it is not enough, as in the good old days, to increase employment levels. it's not the same as fighting unemployment. you can have access to the labour market, while the longterm unemployed remain longterm unemployed. this is a necessary and important innovation. secondly, we have emphasized very strongly that in a situation like this we need new jobs and new businesses, which certainly also requires political initiative. we are now presenting a much more comprehensive programme for entrepreneurship and job creation than the commission's guidelines. at the same time, we insist on and emphasise the need for quality, sustainability and a future-oriented approach. thirdly, we are strengthening the gender perspective very much compared to the commission's proposal. in particular, we propose that gender equality should be a priority objective alongside the three objectives proposed by the commission. the council is obviously not ready to adopt this yet, but we must continue to exert pressure. this is not the last time we'll have that opportunity. fourthly, we have made clear demands for regional and local employment strategies. i am glad to see that these demands are being met. for a while, things looked bad when this element had disappeared from what was the final guideline. it has now been reintroduced, which is important, because we need to be able to mobilise people and businesses from below if we are to create the 22 million jobs that vitorino spoke of. finally, we have agreed to recommend that the parliaments of the member states should take a position on the national action plans. they can then be much better anchored and combined with national employment policies. the problem has been that european employment policy has not been linked or integrated into national policies. it has therefore lacked much effectiveness. i understand that the council is not ready for this either. perhaps we can go through the member states and ask some member states to raise this issue in the council eventually. then they will have the support of parliament. we have also agreed on other matters, which i think we can be proud of. we made no less than 16 compromises in committee and got over 160 amendments down to a relatively manageable number. i am naturally disappointed that after all these compromises it turned out that the ppe-de group was not really satisfied. there are still some differences, which i would like to comment on briefly. firstly, the ppe-de group wants to delete the commission's paper on immigration and immigrant labour and does not want this subject to be dealt with in the guidelines. yes, this is a hot potato in some member states, but it does not help. whether we are a border country or a particularly vulnerable country, all countries in europe face a common global reality which means that we are facing increased cross-border immigration, which we must deal with in an employment policy document. i therefore oppose amendment no 62. another disagreement concerns tax policy. there are several amendments to this effect. i think tax systems are very national. if we are to comment on this, we must speak very broadly. you cannot come up with concrete tax policy proposals for 25 countries. i therefore oppose amendment no 66 by the ppe-de group, which is very radical and calls for a general tax cut for both individuals and companies in all member states, but says nothing about how to look at the financial situation. in my own country, sweden, it is not at all appropriate to reduce the tax burden, but i know that it is different in germany and in other countries. mr president, in conclusion i would just like to say briefly that the liberal amendments on taxation are of a completely different nature. it's interesting but very drastic. it is about shifting the tax burden from personal taxation to consumption and environmental taxation. i think that is interesting, but i do not think we can decide that in this type of document. in conclusion, i would like to say that we have reached a broad consensus. it is very important that we can agree on a document so that we have an answer to give to the council which is sitting and waiting and listening to what we are going to say today.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? i would like to begin by thanking mr schmid for the exceptional cooperation he has given me in the preparation of this proposal, which i consider to be important. employment policy is important in general. we now have a new framework to work within which there is a stronger coordination with the economic guidelines, more long-term thinking and more focus on the results of implementation in the member states. we are fully committed to this strategy. i would like to stress the importance of aligning the employment strategy with the economic guidelines, so that they are equivalent in this process. let me say something about the overall objectives. we've left the pillar structure. the fact that we are now aiming for full employment is extremely important. it is also important, as herman schmid says, to supplement the employment objective with an unemployment objective. although these two aspects are followed approximately, they are not followed 100%. it may be that employment is increasing, but that a high level of unemployment still exists. another objective is the quality of working life. in future, europe will not compete with cheap labour but with the quality of the products and services we are able to produce. for this we need a properly trained workforce. this is clearly linked to lifelong learning, to training the workforce to make europe competitive. a third important issue is integration. it is not just about integration between regions, that we have a high level of employment and full employment throughout europe, but it is also about groups which today are largely excluded from the labour market, such as people with disabilities and people from other countries. it's important, you know. the gender aspects have already been discussed and it is important that we highlight them. they had a pillar of their own in the last strategy. it is also important that national parliaments are involved and that the overall, common objectives can be supplemented by national objectives. let me say something briefly about the disagreement. we usually handle this in committee. we have slightly different views on flexibility and security, but we manage to agree on this point and say that we need a balance. on the tax question, i do not share thomas mann's view. it is only to look around the european union: in fact, some of the countries with the lowest unemployment and highest employment rates also have the highest tax burdens. this tells me that the taxation issue is somewhat more complicated than is shown by the amendment tabled by thomas mann. the level of employment is determined by a large number of factors. it is not as simple as saying that a reduction in taxation is also good for employment in general. it's not like that. i would like to say, however, that i will vote in such a way that we preserve the unity of the house. the worst thing would be if we in parliament did not express our views when we now have the opportunity to do so. it will guide me in the vote.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the treaty provides for guidelines for the employment policies of the member states. although i have serious doubts about the usefulness of eu guidelines, it is not worth discussing their usefulness or their non-use at this time. i shall therefore concentrate on the commission's proposal for a guideline and comment in particular on the report by mr herman schmidt as it stands after the vote in committee. much of the commission's proposal is good, albeit already a little too detailed. the committee's work has led to the addition of further detailed requirements and measures. in order to be effective, guidelines must be anchored in the member states. it is not useful to set out an endless number of detailed objectives and measures, as in the present proposal. today it is 15 member states, tomorrow 25! the fact that at least 33% of children under 3 years of age should be offered childcare is a matter which, in my view, has nothing to do with guidelines at eu level. the present report, apart from the general lack of too much detail, has the defect of containing points which i very much doubt would contribute at all to increasing employment. the rapporteur states in his text that the responsibility for implementing a successful employment policy lies with the member states. this is absolutely true. until the governments of the member states are prepared to take the necessary measures themselves to reform the labour market, employment will not increase to any appreciable extent. i am thinking in particular of reforms aimed at making it easier to recruit staff, but also at reducing staff. unfortunately, for a small company to have the courage to hire an extra person, it must know that it can also reduce staff if necessary. germany is an example of what happens if a rigid labour market is not reformed. unemployment has risen very sharply, partly due to rigid labour laws. in fact, the longer you wait to reform labour markets, the harder it will be. it may seem strange that i, as a swedish moderate in this house, should refer to what the british finance minister said recently, but that was good. he said structural reforms were needed to re-energize the european economy, more trade liberalisation was needed and, above all, the liberalisation of labour markets through fewer regulations and healthier levels of occupational safety and health. in the eu today there are 13 million people who have no work to go to. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. the guidelines should therefore be more comprehensive and focus on measures which, together with the economic policy guidelines, actually lead to economic growth and consequently to more employment and thus to resources for, inter alia, education and health.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank herman schmid for the constructive work he has done on this report. i would like to mention a couple of amendments to mr vitorino from the commission, which we christian democrats, and myself, have had the privilege of contributing to drafting. this is the demographic challenge which was identified in stockholm in march 2001 as one of the most important issues for the future of the union. by 2050, the european union is expected to have 40 million more pensioners than today, and thus 40 million fewer people of working age. this is included in amendment no 1 as an important text in the report by mr hermans schmidt. i would like to ask mr vitorino how he views the crisis of the pension system, of economic development and of the survival of entire settlements in the european union in the light of the serious demographic developments. it is good that a number of amendments, notably nos 28 and 38, which deal with the reconciliation of family and professional life, are now included in the report by mr hermans schmidt. this is a very urgent matter, but it is not good that amendment no 43 in the report says that reconciliation of work and family life should be achieved primarily through efforts to develop childcare. it should be more about the right of children to more time with their parents, than that we should necessarily build a childcare system that gives children less time with their parents. amendment no 9 is also important. it is a question of raising tax morale, creating a level playing field for companies and fighting undeclared work more vigorously. in conclusion, let me give a big round of applause to amendment no 60, which emphasises the value of families, family businesses and generational change. if we are to be able to do longterm jobs at all, the generational changes must be solved in family businesses.
