in writing. - (sv) the swedish social democrats support the proposal on the employment strategy. it is essential that the european parliament should support this strategy. the proposal has many positive elements, such as the emphasis on equal opportunities, the demand for improvements for disabled people in the labour market and the definition of full employment as the main objective. a proposal from the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and european democrats is based on the assumption that a general reduction in taxes would promote employment. we do not share that view. the reality is that there is no relationship between the level of taxation and employment. we have, however, chosen not to vote against this proposal but to abstain. if the epp-ed group fails to get this proposal through, it will vote against the report as a whole. the failure of the report as a whole is much more serious than the adoption of this particular proposal. that is why we abstain on this point.
in writing. -{sv) we believe in the principle of subsidiarity, which means that decisions must be taken as closely as possible to the people concerned. that is why we actively support the question of a constitution for the european union, in which the division of responsibilities will be clear for everyone. it must also be clear to all citizens that the eu should only deal with the issues for which it is best placed, namely crossborder issues. all other issues must be dealt with at local, regional or national level. social policy is one example of an area where the eu should have only limited competence, except for matters relating to the free movement of persons within the eu. each member state should have full responsibility and the right to decide for itself on its social security system. healthcare, childcare and care for the elderly are clear examples of areas that are not directly cross-border. this also applies to labour market policy. this does not, however, prevent member states from cooperating and exchanging ideas in the social field. at the same time, we would like to stress that we fully share the commitment of our colleague mr schmid to social cohesion, integration and above all equal opportunities.
in writing. - s7v) we, the swedish social democrats, would like to explain why we voted against the motion for a resolution on the report on the status of members of the european parliament. we cannot support the proposed statute, since our amendment no 30 on the national tax has been rejected. for us swedish social democrats it is of the utmost importance that we can retain the national tax because it is the system we adhere to and use. to be able to finance our common social policy, we need solidarity funding via income tax. that is why we believe that members of the european parliament should pay national taxes in their own country. elected politicians must abide by the same rules as other citizens. another key issue is the reimbursement of travel expenses. we have called for the development of new rules based on transparent accounts that can be easily checked by citizens. this is why we have advocated a transparent and fair system of benefits. at present, travel and other expenses are reimbursed on a flat-rate basis. we want a system where the reimbursed costs correspond to the actual costs. we also believe that the level of wages is also a source of controversy, since the new levels are very high.
in writing. -{sv) we have today voted against the european parliament's report on the status of members of the european parliament, which was adopted by 294 votes in favour, 171 against and 59 abstentions. without a system of reimbursed travel expenses based on actual costs, instead of the current system of flat-rate, the new statute will not increase transparency. while it is true that the bureau of the european parliament decided on 29 may 2003 to set up a new system for reimbursing travel expenses, this does not solve the problem. the decision on reimbursement of actual expenses should automatically enter into force at the same time as the new members' statute. in future, we would like the level of meps' salaries to be fixed in proportion to the salary level in force in national parliaments. furthermore, since meps live and represent their member state, taxes should in future also be levied at national level in order to take account of the cost of living and social security systems of the different countries.
in writing. - sv) we, the moderate swedes, support the report on the annual report on the meda 2000 programme. however, we believe that the eu, in view of the current economic situation and the difficulties in establishing fixed budgetary frameworks, cannot itself bear the increased costs involved.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. commissioner, what is it? there are few things more important in a future world than sustainable and environmentally friendly agriculture. i have supported commissioner fischler's forward-looking ideas from the beginning in proposing how we should reform the european agricultural policy. unfortunately, these ideas have deteriorated and continue to deteriorate. i think it was a deterioration when president chirac and chancellor schröder agreed on a ceiling, which would have been applied as a floor for the costs of agricultural policy. i regret to say that i do not think that what we have achieved here in parliament is an improvement but rather a deterioration of the proposal. i am concerned that when the ministers meet they will also make this situation worse. even the peasants of europe do not seem to understand their own best interests. the opposition to the commission's proposals for agricultural reform among farmers, among us in the european parliament and among the agricultural ministers must be due to a lack of willingness to see the big picture. i do not intend, mr president, to go into the details of a report, but to devote my speech to the broad context. we must remember that the bulk of the world's food production is produced at costs not covered by today's low world market prices. so most farmers in developed countries need support to produce, and farmers in developing countries need more pay. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. this lowers world market prices. we need to change the systems to create a healthy world market. world market prices should be able to rise to a level where most people can produce, and so it is quite obvious that all trade-distorting aid must be eliminated. the eu cannot change this on its own, but together with the usa we could do a lot in this area. the wto negotiations and the commission's proposal for agricultural reform are steps in the right direction in this context. however, we must remember that the world is not really that simple. the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the quality of agricultural products. we have very strict environmental and animal welfare standards. we have food safety and food quality requirements. we have a right to workers' protection. we want to preserve the agricultural landscape, the natural and cultural environments, and we want to develop the social situation in the countryside. in principle, this could be paid for by the market in terms of values such as quality. it could be paid for with direct support from society, from the eu, especially in the case of such things as public utilities. the road from high subsidies and destroyed world market prices to sound trade and pricing, however, must go through a change in current support, i.e. a decoupling of support from production. it is in this context that my rural development report comes in. unfortunately, there was too little money available when the commission drafted its proposal. i have been able to get a little money from mr cunha and some money from mrs jeggle, thanks to good and kind colleagues in this parliament, and have thus been able to put a little more into rural development. the proposal for relaxation poses the greatest risks in less favoured areas, and we must deal with them as quickly as possible. i think parliament has made some improvements on that point. mr president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, if we in the eu do not modernise our agricultural aid arsenal in time, i.e. move aid from the blue box to the green box or whatever you call it, the wto negotiations, if they lead to any positive outcome from a global point of view, could take the guns out of our hands in terms of protecting european agriculture and the european agricultural model. then we have nothing to do. it's better to be present than to be noticed. we europeans should understand that too. hopefully, when the agriculture ministers meet for negotiations next week, they will realise that a failure or a bad decision can actually lead to a disaster for farmers, for the countryside and also for consumers who want good food in europe.
mr president, commissioner and president of the council! i shall speak this time on behalf of the liberal group. in my last speech i was speaking essentially on my own behalf as rapporteur. the difference is marginal in itself. i am a decentraliser and would like to look after the small scale, but i would like to say to my colleagues that globalisation is also coming for agriculture. it does not help to stick our heads in the sand like ostriches and say that we have not noticed anything and that we do not want to change anything. when i listen to my colleagues, i find that many of them actually do the ostrich. the strange thing is that in this parish you seem to become more conservative the further to the left you are. i would like to comment on the commissioner's view on multifunctionality and sustainability. it is very urgent, and we have said so in the eldr, and important to deregulate agricultural policy in the long term, but now it is urgent to guarantee support for a multifunctional and sustainable agriculture in the near future. we're working on it. we're trying to change systems so that we have something to fall back on in the world we live in. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would of course like to thank everyone for all the work that has been done. it is amazing that we managed to get through all these amendments in the committee on agriculture. i would also like to say, on behalf of my group, that we support the compromise in the cunha report. we support it because it supports the principle of relaxation. it's important, and even if you don't run the whole line, you still get a piece. i would like to say to the president-in-office that, now that we have taken a decision, i regret that we do not have the right to take part in the decision-making process. i think, however, that the president-in-office and the council should take advantage of the opportunity that exists not to listen to parliament in its entirety but to go one step further. if you do, you will at least be serving my interests a little better, and perhaps the interests of the commission as well.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank commissioner byrne for his reply. the prisoners in cuba, some 60 of the 75 who have been sentenced in summary circumstances, have been sentenced to more than 20 years in prison. they feel that their lives are simply threatened. such harsh sentences have not been handed down in cuba for decades. my next question to the commissioner is how we can follow up the protests in practical policy. can we act within the international community, within the united nations, by trying to get the red cross to act? can action be taken through churches and other means? can we use different types of sanctions to put pressure on the cuban regime? these are people who have paid a very high price for democracy.
the rapporteur. thank you, mr president! when the cartagena protocol was negotiated in 2000, it was a major success for the european union. it was also a success for all of us who want environmental considerations and respect for consumers to be able to set the framework for free trade. the protocol clarifies the conditions for global trade in genetically modified products. today, 49 countries have ratified the protocol. it only needs one more country to ratify the protocol for it to enter into force. the draft law we are now debating sets out eu rules for the export of genetically modified organisms. by adopting these rules, the european union fulfils the requirements of the cartagena protocol. these new rules are timely and may also serve as a model and inspiration for states currently working on their regulatory framework. compared with the proposal put forward by the commission, the rules we are going to adopt tomorrow are a little stricter and a little clearer. they are more in line with the provisions of the protocol and go even further in certain areas. in parliament we have focused on tightening the proposal in certain key areas. we have ensured that the rules and definitions are not weaker than those laid down in the protocol and that the explicit approval of the importing country is always required for exports to be allowed. this is now clearly stated in the draft law. it is clarified that a lack of reply from the importing country can never be considered as tacit approval. the legislation of the importing country must always be respected. the proposal makes it clear that we shall not export anything from the eu that is not allowed within the eu. it is clear that a high degree of transparency is to be applied and that information is to be provided to the public about the exports that are made. the proposal also clarifies that there should be a clear liability relationship, so that the actual exporter is the one who notifies the export. in total, the text we are now going to adopt means that the european parliament has been given an estimated 80% of what we wanted to see passed at first reading. we have also discussed this proposal with the council of ministers. coreper has already adopted the text which we are going to adopt tomorrow. in this way we can avoid a compromise and the legislation will be ready when the protocol enters into force at global level. this has been possible thanks to a considerable degree of coordination between the council of ministers and parliament. there has also been a clear willingness to compromise and to work quickly between the political groups. this has been achieved thanks to good cooperation with the shadow rapporteurs, whom i would like to thank, no one mentioned no one forgotten. we have also had very close cooperation with the danish and then the greek presidencies. they deserve a thank you too. the same applies to the officials of the commission's directorate for the environment, who have been very helpful. by adopting these rules, we are demonstrating that environmental considerations and respect for the laws of developing countries are key concepts in the eu's approach to global trade in genetically modified products. it is needed now that the united states is pushing these kinds of issues for decision in the world trade organization (wto). we have recently seen examples of this country dumping genetically modified food in countries which do not allow it in their own national legislation. this shows very clearly the importance of the eu now adopting rules that point to a more responsible, credible and long-term approach to these complex and highly controversial issues.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there is one fundamental requirement which should always be attached to a constitution, whether it be a national constitution or a constitution for the european union. that is the need for clarity, clarity in terms of both the division of powers and the delimitation of competences. as a swede, i have had very little experience in drafting constitutions. when sweden's constitution, our constitution, was adopted, too many questions were left unanswered. sweden's parliament, the riksdag, was given virtually unlimited power to decide on virtually all matters by a simple majority. freedom of speech and the press remained strong thanks to a unique swedish tradition, but political power was given the go-ahead to spread in all directions. in a constitution for the european union, this swedish mistake must not be repeated. the most important thing is to create a clear catalogue of competences, which clarifies which tasks are eu-wide and which tasks should remain national or at another level. there are proposals to make social issues a shared competence of the eu. this would be, as i see it, a historic mistake. such matters are best handled by each individual member state. i also see no other way of doing things in an enlarged union with a very wide economic range from one country to another. the commission has also been asked to make a statement on the situation in the community. the euro is the joint responsibility of the member states. it requires budgetary discipline. however, taxes and decisions on expenditure must remain the responsibility of the nations. the differences between countries are large and require flexibility. low taxes are the only way for new members to eventually catch up with the present members in terms of their finances. it hardly contributes to a more efficient eu. the rotating presidency of the council is also important for the cohesion of the member states. it is also a unique opportunity for even small member states to influence the work of the union. i would therefore urge the convention and the forthcoming intergovernmental conference to be very careful in determining what the future eu will look like and how it will function.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would simply like to draw the attention of my colleagues to the conditions for this vote. there is a political agreement between us in parliament, the council of ministers and the commission which means that if we adopt it we can avoid a compromise. it requires that we vote yes to the first block and no to blocks 2 and 3. i therefore urge members to do so. we will still get through most of what parliament has wanted to achieve, while avoiding a compromise.
in writing. -{sv) i voted today against the european parliament's report on the new statute for members of the european parliament, after the commission delivered its opinion following yesterday's vote in plenary. the draft was approved by 323 votes to 167, with 39 abstentions. if it does not involve the introduction of a system of reimbursement of travel expenses based on actual costs instead of the current flat-rate system, the new status will not provide for increased transparency. although the parliament's bureau decided on 29 may 2003 to look into the development of a new system for reimbursing travel expenses, this does not solve the problem. a decision on the reimbursement of actual expenses should enter into force automatically as soon as the new members' statute applies. the new status of members must aim at fairness, transparency and efficiency. it also considered that the allowances of members of the european parliament should continue to correspond to those of their counterparts in the national parliaments in the future. similarly, they should continue to be subject to national taxation in order to take account of the variable cost of living and the different social security systems since members reside and represent their member state.

madam president, i would like to thank you. i am glad that mrs smet has drawn up this report, and i agree with her on many points. i am very much in favour of the open method of coordination. however, i am also a little concerned that it is often seen as a kind of soft legislation and a kind of inferior alternative to ordinary legislation, something to be used only in emergencies. i think the opposite. i think that legislation must be applied when there is no other option, when there is a real need for authority, strength and discipline. however, a number of public tasks can be solved in ways other than by laws, discipline, coercion and whipping. the open method of coordination is a modern form of project management. it is used for public activities aimed at change, development and continuous renewal, where the ability to change one's working methods in line with experience is required. it is perhaps no coincidence that the open method of coordination has grown out of market economies, where public action must be as flexible and as efficient as the market can be at its best. i therefore find it very moving to hear representatives of the bourgeoisie say that they do not want this flexible and efficient market instrument, but rather a bureaucratic, controlling, legalistic, old-fashioned form of government in the public sector. i find that remarkable. i think that the open method of coordination should be enshrined in the constitution so that it is generally accepted, which i think is important. i am very concerned, however, that it will be written in such a way as to prescribe how it will work, what methods it will use, what processes it will cover, and so on. this is what happens with a traditional legal text, but then you would tie the method that would be flexible, flexible and mobile and put a straitjacket on it. it's not gonna be as effective. i support the eldr amendment on that point. i myself tabled an amendment which, to my great surprise, was voted down in committee. i'll get back to you on that. the point is that the open method of coordination is also a method of cooperation, not just a method of coordination. it cannot rest on sanctions and punishments. it must, like any other free social relationship, be based on trust and respect. we cannot therefore use naming and shaming and worst practices. what we need is best practices. that's where the focus should be. otherwise, you are ruining this method and its possibilities. it is different from the legislative method and therefore it is completely wrong to think that, inspired by old legal traditions, one must discipline, punish and point out those who do not obey. this method is completely different. i would like to see this method spread. this is how it has come into use in some european societies. it is not as widespread everywhere, but it is common in the scandinavian countries from which i come. it plays a very progressive role there. it is also more widespread in the anglo-saxon countries, but it is absent in some other countries with a much more traditional administrative apparatus. i think we must move towards this open, democratic and modern method of cooperation. i would like to emphasize the word 'democratic' in particular, because it is democratic, but in a different way than through legal and judicial control.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i believe that promoting the economic participation of workers is very important for the development of both companies and their employees. perhaps greater participation and thus greater responsibility can even contribute to greater success and profitability for enterprises and thus to increased employment and increased access to capital. some even argue that the development of different types of economic participation systems could be a way to achieve higher growth and faster fulfillment of the objectives of the lisbon strategy. it would be a mercy to quietly beg for it, since the lisbon objectives seem to be increasingly taking the character of a bunch of cranes. in any case, profit-sharing schemes of various kinds can at least create good incentives for employees, create greater enthusiasm for work tasks and strengthen the bonds between companies and employees. that's where we agree, mr. menrad. as regards these aspects of the report, i think that mr menrad has done a very good job and a good analysis of the problems that are involved in this matter. however, i do not share the view that this is something to be decided or discussed at eu level. the rapporteur himself has written in his report that harmonization of the rules on economic cooperation is not necessary, nor is it desirable or possible, because it would mean that member states' policies would be less flexible. i agree with that. however, menrad later said that a european framework was needed to promote economic participation by workers by coordinating agreements on general principles, social security and taxation. why would you do that? the coordination of models and conditions would not benefit either companies or employees. i therefore see no reason why this issue, with the number of specific requirements and considerations that will need to be added, should be dealt with at all by any of the eu institutions. corporate profit-sharing schemes affect companies and their employees. that is where this question should be. the moderate delegation will therefore vote against the report. well, somebody's got to have a different opinion.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. when swedish golfer annika sörenstam wanted to compete with the men on the u.s. professional tour, the male golf world was rocked. some reacted with suspicion and envy, others with outright disapproval. what will happen now, perhaps she is better than some of us, the men wondered. the reaction that annika sörenstam faced when she wanted to measure her ability with men is symptomatic of many men's attitudes towards women and sports. i do not mean that men in general actively oppose women in sport, although there are those who do. but acting through recklessness and prejudice has the same effect. women are discriminated against in sports. this is something which is very clearly shown in the report drawn up by mrs fraisse, which i think is very good. women receive fewer resources and less training time and are often disadvantaged by sponsors. moreover, men often rule the sports world. to this must be added, as make an effort and work with determination for a level playing field in the world of sport. sport is important in many ways. it is almost always useful and healthy for the practitioner. it creates opportunities for social interaction and development. in the world of sport, we can fight drugs, we can fight exclusion, we can give people the space to grow and develop, but we must give everyone the same opportunities, girls as boys, women as men. the european parliament cannot eliminate all the causes of gender inequality in sport, any more than we can in other parts of society. but we can be clear about what we want and we can call on ourselves, the member states, sports associations, the media and athletes to always think about equality and equal treatment for women and men. member states can be more firm in their approach. you can stop funding to associations that don't meet gender equality goals. the commission considers that the aid is compatible with the internal market. if it's not even, you don't get paid. in this way, it is the will that determines whether we will achieve gender equality in the world of sport. where there is a will, there is a possibility. let us use it to bring about a level playing field in sport in europe.

in writing. -{v) we are critical of the above reports on the reform of the common agricultural policy. we believe that the reports set up an outdated agricultural policy which does not meet the requirements and needs of modern global society. examples include the protectionist requirement to 'provide for total compensatory area aid for rice producers and the introduction of a fixed import duty' and the introduction of a system of import levies and export refunds to stabilise the internal market against price fluctuations on world markets. in comparison, the commission's proposal appears to be a step in the right direction. we have therefore voted against all these reports.
in writing. -{v) we want agriculture to be gradually liberalised and the amount of aid to agriculture to be reduced, which is why we are voting against this report. the commission's mid-term review proposal was a step in the right direction towards deregulating agriculture in favour of agricultural production. however, it has important shortcomings. we are against any form of modulation as it is detrimental to rational agriculture. we also believe that compensatory aid should be linked to the area used. the compromise proposal on which we have just voted has a deadlock effect which is harmful to european agriculture and represents a step backwards in the reform process. fifty per cent of european farmers are over 55 years old. it is more correct to regard the commission's proposal as a good proposal for farmers' pensions than as a plan for the phasing out of subsidy policy. there are no clear rules of the game for the 8% of european farmers under 35 years of age, who represent the very future of agriculture.
in writing. -{sv) there is no doubt that eu agricultural aid needs to be reformed in order to create a healthy global market. unfortunately, the reform proposal does not address what we believe should be the main change and which has the main distorting effect on world markets and on the ability of poor countries to exploit their comparative advantages; the commission has also examined the impact of the measures on the export of the products concerned. the commission proposal, however, recognises the multifunctionality of agriculture and allows for greater modulation, i.e. greater flexibility to support tasks outside food production, such as the preservation of biodiversity and the rural landscape, or other types of environmental measures. it also allows for the implementation of subsequent reforms. at the same time, decoupling could lead to disengagement and resistance to change, which would hinder the necessary intergenerational transmission of knowledge within the agricultural world. in view of these two aspects, the european parliament's proposal therefore appears to be a compromise which leaves room for the necessary future reforms. we have therefore chosen, despite many objections, to support the report in the final vote.
in writing. -{sv) we voted in favour of the report, despite the rejection of our proposal to delete the phrase 'the political values of the european union are essentially based on the judeo-christian and humanist culture of europe'. we disagree with this formula, but as far as the rest of the report is concerned, it is a good one, and we have chosen to vote in favour of it.
in writing. - (sv) a democratic turkey is welcome in the eu - provided that its conflicts with neighbouring countries and minorities are resolved. turkey is a test of principle for the eu. should the eu remain a closed judeo-christian fortress, an outward-looking fortress, or should it instead be a vast organisation of cooperation for all the peoples, nations, regions, linguistic and cultural groups of europe? for the greens and the european free alliance, the answer is clear: we hope that turkey will continue on the path of reform so that formal negotiations for eu membership can begin as early as next year. i am in favour of turkey's accession, provided, however, that the country reduces, for example, the role of the army in political life, that it eventually abolishes the national council altogether, that it grants national minorities - kurds, armenians and syrians - the same rights as other citizens and that it guarantees freedom of expression. the report also reiterates a longstanding wish expressed in previous european parliament resolutions of 1987 and 2002, namely that turkey should recognise the armenian genocide of 1915. the reconciliation between turkey and armenia must not be thwarted by a conflict of historical interpretation, but it is nevertheless remarkable that the current turkish political proxies have such difficulty in unreservedly acknowledging a historical fact - namely that the young turkish fascist regime had ordered the genocide of armenians out of desperation, a fact for which these leaders were convicted by the turkish courts after the war.
in writing. - (sv) in point 4, the report calls on the convention to 'give a legal basis to sport in the future treaty of the union which recognises its cultural, educational and social functions, including a reference to equal access for women and men to sport and responsibilities. i agree with everything that is said about sport in this house - but i cannot accept that the eu is also given legislative power in this area. that is why i voted against this report. a swedish proverb says that "the one who kisses too much, hugs too little". the eu cannot and should not assume legislative power in sport. this would be an exaggeration of the union's powers, which it would not be able to fulfil. the principles of subsidiarity and proportionality are very clear in this respect: centralised legislation at european level, far from sports associations, clubs and fans, will not bring any added value to sport, but on the contrary considerable disadvantages.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the convention method as a method for treaty amendments is something entirely new. the main objective of widening the debate on the eu and taking the negotiations out of closed, dusty rooms has been achieved. however, those of us who had hoped that the eu would leave the stage of technocratic union altogether and become a real democracy based on a common european debate have reason to be somewhat disappointed. there are too many rules of the game and too many exceptions to the norms. i hope, however, that this result will lead to the strengthening of the union's competitiveness, which is absolutely necessary. the next convention will have to pick up the pieces that are left behind. to see what this convention really means, we must analyse the results between the lines. the issues that will be on the table for the next convention will be foreign policy a clear example of a loose thread and merging the functions of the two executive presidents into a common function and continuing the council's evolution into a second chamber. this constitutional treaty is certainly not the last treaty amendment of the eu. the bill of rights created by the last treaty amendment did not come into force then but was incorporated now. that's how the eu process works. exceptions to the normal decision-making model of this convention may be dealt with at the next convention. however, the trend is in the right direction. the compromise must therefore stand. after all, the influence of citizens has been considerably strengthened. it may increase but never ever decrease!
high representative for the cfsp. - the agenda calls for the commission's statement on the future of the textiles agreement in the context of the wto negotiations.
commission. - madam president, i should like to begin by thanking the groups who have spoken for their support for this strategy, which i have tried to present briefly in reply to your questions. i shall now deal with the few additional points raised in your speeches. mrs ferrer, i share your vision of the future of the textile and clothing sector in europe. it has a word: dynamism. that's an offensive line. the textile and clothing industry is not a thing of the past, and it is not a matter of managing the gradual shrinkage as painlessly as possible. it's not my design. it's not your design. it is not the one which is the basis of our trade policy and our industrial policy. that is also what i understood from mr lage's speech. mr berenguer fuster, we will only continue to reduce our tariff protection if others do the same. whether you call it reciprocity or not, it is the essence of our policy. and you are right, as mr hatzidakis is right, to point out that behind all this there are issues of intellectual property and that it is important to continue to fight - as we are doing, for example, with geographical indications - so that everything that is quality, everything that is taste, everything that is design, and which often makes our comparative advantage, is not illegally imitated and, therefore, in a way stolen. mrs figueiredo, yes, we are working on the impact of further liberalisation at the level of employment, the regional fabric, or qualifications. this is part of the idea we are working on with erkki liikanen for a post-2005 strategy which we hope to see emerge in the autumn of this year. mrs lambert and mrs read stressed the link between trade and basic social norms. i answer very clearly and very frankly the question: can we make that link? yes, we can do that under our unilateral trade policy. when we give benefits, we can give additional benefits by making that connection, and that's what we're doing. yes, we can do this bilaterally. when we conclude association or trade agreements with a number of third countries, respect for basic social standards is part of our bilateral agreements. yes, we can do that by working, as we have begun to do - as mr bodrato suggested - towards fair labelling which allows consumers, consumer citizens, to choose between products manufactured under more or less sustainable conditions from the social and environmental point of view. but no, we cannot do this at the multilateral level for the time being. we europeans have tried. this is practically the only failure of doha: on this point we have been confronted, at the multilateral level, by a coalition of the united states on the one hand and the developing countries on the other. this link is therefore not part of the programme which will take us to the end of 2004. we'll have to come back to the charge later. mr bastos, i agree with your concept of fair negotiations. however, in social matters, we must distinguish between the basic social standards - child labour, equal pay for men and women, trade union rights, prohibition of forced labour - which are part of the multilateral commitments of most countries on the planet, and wage standards. we know that the level of wages in developing countries is lower than in our country. it is, in a way, by definition, otherwise it would not be developing countries. however, we all want to see them increase. mr ettl is right: non-tariff barriers often replace tariff barriers which disappear. this is a game that many administrations play. we have become experts in detecting, attacking and dealing with this kind of device and we are constantly improving our information system, in conjunction with industry and social partners, to detect these practices earlier and faster, which in many cases are incompatible with international trade regulations. that's what i wanted to answer. i will conclude by saying that this sector is in a way a case study in globalisation as we europeans and the european commission understand it. yes, globalization is having an effect. yes, globalization is creating relocations. yes, it challenges our competitiveness, our creativity, our skills. that is why we need public policies to create the framework for this sector to continue to move up the ladder, to continue to create added value, to continue to move up in quality. this is only possible if the rules of the game are established and respected. this was true at the national level. this has become true at european level. it's becoming a reality on a global level. - thank you, commissioner lamy, in particular for responding directly to the views of members. the debate is closed. (the sitting was suspended at 8 p.m. and resumed at 9.05 p.m.)
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. no country has been able to reduce poverty without a significant increase in energy use. however, the availability of modern energy varies greatly. more than two billion people in the world lack access to electricity or fuel and are reliant on firewood and animal waste for their energy needs. some examples: cooking with poorly ventilated stoves has significant health effects. hundreds of millions of women and children spend several hours each day gathering firewood for their households. the lack of electricity results in inadequate lighting, limited access to communications, and a lack of refrigeration and a range of labor-saving and income-generating facilities. modern energy services for these poor countries are therefore an essential prerequisite for the eradication of poverty in these countries and for the achievement of the so-called millennium development goals. most of today's energy sources create environmental problems at local, regional and international levels that threaten the health and well-being of present and future generations. finding new ways to expand energy services for the poor, while taking greater account of the environmental impact of energy use, is one of our key challenges. despite the primary importance of energy supply for development and growth, energy issues have been largely absent from the international development agenda and poverty reduction efforts. for example, energy projects accounted for less than 5% of eu assistance since 1990. last year in johannesburg, something of a breakthrough happened, and the two energy initiatives launched by the commission are more than welcome. both the communication we are discussing today and the eu's special energy initiative in johannesburg underline the crucial role of energy in the fight against poverty. the general focus of the proposed actions, i.e. support for capacity building, energy sector reform, demand side management, diversification of supply, development of appropriate regulatory frameworks and financial mechanisms, and public/private partnerships, is a good model. however, both initiatives suffer from some limitations and shortcomings. the proposals are not ambitious enough in view of the enormous challenges ahead. no new financial resources are proposed. of course, the majority of investment in the energy sector must be in the form of private capital, but there is also a great need for aid. otherwise, there is little hope of reaching the rural poor. furthermore, the necessary transition to renewable energy and more efficient energy use can only be achieved with targeted financial support to cover the higher initial investments. we therefore propose in this report that a special fund for sustainable energy should be set up, in much the same way as the one now set up by the commission for water actions, worth one billion euro. another problem: there is no clear distinction between the requirements in terms of policy development and investment, either at village level or in the modern sector. a clear specification and prioritisation of the village level is needed. most of the oil imported into developing countries is used for transportation. yet the commission communication hardly mentions the transport sector. there are examples, not least from latin america, such as the ethanol programme in brazil, of how concrete measures can actually bring about environmentally friendly developments in the field of transport. i do not have time to go into more specific suggestions and comments. i would just like to sum up by saying that the commission's proposals are generally positive. however, in order for these proposals not to be mere words, significant resources must be allocated to stimulating investment in new and environmentally friendly technologies, which are often more expensive initially but cheaper over the life cycle of the projects. furthermore, a clear priority must be given to action at village level where the majority of the poor are located. it is there that the need for aid is greatest. i hope that parliament will support my report and that the commission will take note of our amendments, particularly those relating to the requirement for substantial increases in financial resources. we must ensure that these good proposals do not end up in empty rhetoric.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. i shall not prolong the debate. i thank you for the comments made. let me just make a small point in relation to the debate on private investment or public investment. i think, like de palacio, we need all the resources we can get. the important thing is that there is a regulatory framework in every country to ensure that people with low incomes have access to energy, that is, that we have a good regulatory framework in this area. i believe that competition between different companies is preferable to monopoly, whether public or private. i thank you for the debate and hope that we will reach a good agreement tomorrow at the vote.
mr president, commissioner wallström, colleagues, i would like to thank you for your work. i would like to begin by thanking the commission for the proposal to amend the seveso ii directive. i also want to thank lisi for his hard work. however, i fully agree with the rapporteur that the seveso ii directive should include the points which have not been approved by the council and the commission, partly to avoid grey areas and partly to improve the possibilities of correcting the shortcomings of the existing legislation. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the future of the european union. in addition, potassium nitrate should also be included. this is not contrary to the aims of the directive. nor is it a question of reversing the whole of this proposal for a directive, but of addressing the problems we have seen in connection with the serious accidents which have occurred in recent years. i would like to make a special plea for amendment no 13 on a new recital 3a, which explains why we consider it particularly important to incorporate amendments nos 1 and 2. the commission's proposal for a directive on mining waste, which was published on 2 june, if i remember correctly, is more concerned with waste management. however, in my view, it does not cover dangerous substances in mining or other extension. i therefore believe that if the consequences of serious accidents are to be reduced, it is essential that these dangerous substances should be included. they belong to the seveso directive and not to the general discussion on waste management. i therefore welcome amendments nos 1, 2 and 13 in particular, but also the other amendments.

rapporteur. – mr president, in amendment no 6 to this report, mrs boudjenah proposes to add a sentence to paragraph 1. she stresses the important role of access to energy for development and suggests that we characterise access to energy as an essential human right. i am sympathetic to her reasoning, although i do not think we can make such a statement. access to energy is not part of any human rights convention. i would, however, be prepared to replace 'essential human rights' by 'economic and social rights'. if the house agrees to such an oral amendment, i would then recommend that it support the amendment thus corrected. if not, i would advise the house to vote against the part of the amendment which refers to essential human rights.
in writing. - (sv) the moderates decided today to abstain in the final vote on the funding of european political parties. political parties must be built on the basis of their members and operate without public funding. public money should not, in principle, be used to finance political parties. for the reasons stated above, we are voting in favour of amendment no 41, which calls for the rejection of the commission's proposal in its entirety.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against the report, which was nevertheless adopted by 345 votes in favour, 102 against and 34 abstentions. the reason for this decision is, firstly, that it would be preferable for the commission to determine the amount of party funding, rather than the parliament, which could thus grant party funding to its own 'european political families'. it would be very tempting to do that. secondly, the conditions for the creation of a european political party have been set at too high a level, with one representation required in one member state out of four. thirdly, there is an inherent risk of arbitrariness in that separate evaluation criteria are applied to groups called european parties before they receive financial support. who will have to decide how to apply these criteria? in practice, this is the dangerous system by which political parties must designate political groups that will gain party status and thus financial support, a system unknown to the swedish parliament.
in writing. - (sv) the resolution highlights a number of important areas for the continuation of enhanced cooperation between the eu and the us, but also areas where opinions differ. it seems to me that it is essential that the eu does not give in, especially when it comes to the us responsibility for climate policy. in particular, i am pleased that in paragraph 22 parliament 'agrees with the commission that the complaint lodged by the united states with the wto concerning the eu authorisation procedures for gmos is legally and economically unfounded and politically unnecessary'. nevertheless, i was not able to support the resolution because paragraph 5 also requires a qualified majority vote in the council of ministers in the area of foreign and security policy. i do not share this view and do not consider it realistic. european cooperation would be ineffective if the people, government or parliament of a country were forced to pursue a foreign and security policy that they do not want. that is why i chose to vote against this report, which was nevertheless adopted by 303 votes in favour, 109 against and 47 abstentions.
in writing. - (sv) as moderate meps, we believe that the marine environment is important and should be protected. the preconditions for a future clean marine environment in the baltic sea have improved considerably since the collapse of communism and are set to be further improved by the enlargement of the union. through eu cooperation, joint work towards a less polluted baltic sea will be stepped up. we voted in favour of this own-initiative report, even though it does not take account of what has already been achieved and the rapporteur wants to steer developments by using excessive central control and supranationalism. as moderate members of parliament, we believe that the problems of the baltic sea cannot and should not be solved in the same way as, for example, those of the mediterranean. the regions and countries affected in their respective geographical areas must be able to find solutions adapted to local conditions.

we need a broader definition of economics that also includes the conservation of natural resources, of clean water, of clean air and of biodiversity. this is exactly what has been said by the commission and the eu at large, and it has been said in johannesburg, for example, that public procurement must contribute to sustainable development. it is strange that there have been some who have passed through this chamber. i heard mr lehne say that environmental issues should be dealt with separately and economic issues separately, but that misses the point. public procurement, using our mr president, i would like to thank you. i, and probably my other swedish colleagues, have received a number of worried comments from the swedish authorities on this report. in sweden, these authorities are usually regarded as competent economic operators with a fair degree of freedom of action. they consider that the commission's proposals on a number of points would reduce their effectiveness without increasing either competition or legal certainty. they mention such things as the right to conclude framework agreements for intellectual services and construction contracts, which they often use. they mention the new article 41a, which states that a contract may be cancelled only in very specific circumstances. they wonder what happens when a political majority changes, for example, and whether it is not possible to break an agreement. they also mention a number of other points where swedish practice is much more effective than what is proposed in these directives. they say that public authorities are bureaucratic and inefficient. when they are, it is generally because we politicians draw up overly detailed legislation. i think the commission's proposal for a procurement directive is one such example - i would almost say a horror example - of legislation which creates inefficiency in the public sector, and which creates more problems than it solves. i actually think that the whole proposal for a directive should be withdrawn. the closest i can come to such a result is to support an amendment by mr manders of the eldr group, which calls on the commission to consider the situation and see if the proposal does not create more problems than it solves. i know that the situation differs from country to country, but i find it strange that the consideration of the lowest possible price should play such a dominant role in the commission's proposal. when private companies make a contract, they make an aggregate valuation of a number of factors, of which price is only one. why not look at public authorities in the same way and give them the same freedom to make a complex assessment of the tenders received? why should the law list the arbitrary considerations that may and may not be made? these lists are arbitrary. why is the commission so restrictive in relation to certain factors such as environmental considerations, social needs, employment considerations and other factors which are more about the quality of the products than the purely quantitative relations? i think the commission should consider instead putting forward a directive which sets out general principles and guidelines, i.e. which does not go into details in this way but is more concerned with how to develop the efficiency and democracy of public administrations in the member states. the problems could then be solved much more smoothly.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it is important that there are good and clear rules on public procurement. it is public money, taxpayers' money, that is being used, and therefore, of course, procurement must be carried out in such a way that taxpayers get the best possible value for their money. it is also important that the quality is good. this could be summed up in the words 'good goods and services at the right price'. now it is not so simple, because it is not only the price that will be decisive. it is important that the person or persons producing the good or service are allowed to do so on reasonable terms. it is also important that this be done in an environmentally sound way. laws, regulations and collective agreements relating to social protection, health, both at national and community level, shall apply to the award of public contracts for goods and services. the working environment must be good and the conditions of pay reasonable, but at the same time this decision must not create rules which interfere with the labour law applicable in the respective member states. i have tabled amendments which are similar to those tabled by bill miller and others, who are dealing with this. it is also important that the contracting authority or the contracting authority can cancel a contract if it no longer needs the good or service in question. a new municipal or governmental authority may wish to pursue a policy which does not have the same need for procurement as the previous political authority. the decision we are about to take must not restrict democracy or the ability of people to change course in general elections. public procurement is about meeting the needs of people who want to see the society they have chosen through political elections fulfilled. then the excessive ambition to exploit the interests of the market must not take precedence over the needs of people.

madam president, i would like to thank you. as we all know, this technology is deeply controversial. we also know that the majority of consumers do not want genetically modified food or the large-scale cultivation of genetically modified crops. nor can we ignore the fact that there is a link between the de facto moratorium and these proposals on labelling and traceability. we believe there is good reason for skepticism. there are well-documented risks to biodiversity from the cultivation of genetically modified crops. there are potential health risks if you don't test carefully enough. there are very obvious power relations between the big companies and multinational companies in these industries and the farmers in different countries affected by this technology. it is no coincidence that most of the gm crops grown globally are resistant to a specific pesticide. we therefore see little benefit, we see reason for great caution, and we support the de facto moratorium. however, this is a matter of labelling and traceability. we therefore support the idea of making the rules as strict and as hard as possible. we shall vote in accordance with that. in this way we can give consumers the best opportunity to choose what they want to buy and to choose out what they do not want to buy. it is quite clear that the european parliament's proposals make these legislative proposals better. i think that the compromise reached on the scheele report is a clear step forward. i would also like to thank the rapporteur for the open approach to these issues which has given other groups the opportunity to have an impact. the compromise is also important in terms of the possible measures proposed to enable member states to avoid unwanted interference by gmos and the provisions on coexistence. our group would like to go further, however. we will therefore vote in favour of the amendments tabled by the committee on the environment, which seek to tighten the thresholds down to 0.5% for authorised gmos. we also believe that it is unacceptable to allow genetically modified organisms during a transitional period which are not actually allowed in the union. the question is whether these new rules are sufficient to lift the moratorium. our answer is no! it is much more than that. the rules must be implemented in the member states. clear rules on coexistence are needed, and rules on environmental liability covering genetically modified crops are also needed. we think that this discussion can only be started then. in this context, the relationship with the united states is also used as an argument, but we can never satisfy the united states in this area. it is an illusion. our task is to advocate a more responsible attitude both globally and within the european union. we cannot then start by backing down, but must assert our right to put consumers and the environment first and to impose as stringent rules as we wish.

the rapporteur. the commission is not prepared to accept this. it seems that tonight and tomorrow we will be able to resolve the issue of the views on hormones and their use in animal husbandry, which is the subject of a dispute between the eu and the usa. i shall touch on this in three ways. firstly, i want to thank you, secondly, i want to tell you why i do not like the compromise and thirdly, i want to tell you why i support the compromise after all. i would like to thank very much everyone who has worked with me, the commission, the council, the secretariat of the committee, all my colleagues and the colleagues in the committee - the shadow rapporteurs - who have been very helpful in helping us to reach this solution. in fact, we do not want these hormones, especially estradiol 17β, which is used, for example in the united states, as a growth promoter. we don't want them in our food. then, of course, we should not use them so that they can be in our food. this will give us greater credibility in the negotiations, for example in the wto, with the united states. if you use them as growth agents, they're clearly in. they pose a health risk to humans; they are carcinogenic. if we use them therapeutically in animal husbandry for farm animals, there is also a risk that they will come along. we do that in the eu and it undermines our credibility and our negative attitude. therefore, in principle, they should be banned altogether in these contexts. if we had banned them altogether, as we proposed in the committee, they would have been virtually excluded from the market. there would have been no risk of misuse. the committee was unanimous, and parliament was essentially unanimous at first reading, in doing so. but this is a question that needs to be resolved. although i fully supported my view of a total ban, i have personally seen that it might be reasonable to try to reach a compromise. that is why i have contacted my colleagues in the other political groups and said that we might try. the council said that it wanted to retain the subjects for three reasons. one reason was to induce browning in cattle, which is the main area of application. the second reason was the hatching and mummification of foetuses, also mainly in cattle, and the third was pyometra in cattle. of these uses, the first is the major one and the other two are quite minor. the latter two are also of relative importance from the point of view of animal welfare. it means suffering for the animals if they cannot get this medicine at the present time. although there are alternatives, they were not considered available throughout the eu. my proposal, which became our proposal after contact with the other political groups, was that we should propose to the council that the subjects for the two slightly smaller areas, in the case of stillborn babies and pyometra, be retained. the council accepted this, and we will now be able to deduce it as a use for inducing browning, which i think is a great success. true, there will be a transition period, but after that they will be gone. in addition, we now say in the final proposal that the second use should also be reviewed and perhaps it could also be removed after a certain period of time. i regard this as a great success and perhaps we can solve the problem. i am very pleased that we in the committee have been able to unanimously support the proposal, and i hope that will also be the case in the vote in the house tomorrow. we can therefore eliminate the main use and thereby gain in strength in our conduct of the dispute with the united states. i therefore want to support this compromise, which is weaker than what parliament has put forward in the past but is still good enough to enable us to reach a solution to the mr president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. i have to go to corbey and say she's done an amazing job on a hopeless subject. this is a waste directive, which is not really about packaging at all, but about used packaging. to discuss this is to start at the wrong end of the production chain. if we wanted to achieve reduced waste and reduced transport distances, especially with trucks on the highway, we would start by discussing packaging, the role of packaging, logistics and how we could make enormous gains, environmentally and traffic-wise, by having fully customized packaging. it is also highly unreasonable that we are involved in recycling - whether it is reuse or recycling or energy recovery - and that we are constantly discussing the packaging that goes to households. it is clear that we should have a much more rational system whereby the vast amount of packaging goes to shops, supermarkets, wholesalers, etc. and the packaging we take home is as simple, thin and small as possible, almost non-existent. in this case, it's not about strength, it's about design. we will support in principle the compromise proposals of mr corbey, even though we have not signed them for various reasons.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. a successful eu budgetary policy does not just require decisions on figures and budget lines. the eu has serious problems in implementing the budget. in this case, a major part of parliament's budgetary policy must be monitoring and evaluation, monitoring the implementation of the budget. it is therefore very important that we give our full support to the annual report on the implementation of the budget which we are debating today. this year, there are a few things that are of particular concern to us. this is the case, for example, with outstanding commitments. at the end of the fourth quarter of 2003, total outstanding commitments, known as ral, had risen to eur 126 billion, compared with eur 110 billion at the same time last year. although we do not have all the facts about the reasons for this and what are actually abnormal outstanding commitments, it is clear that the trend is worrying. we are also concerned about the surplus in 2002 of eur 7.4 billion, although this is a step forward, given that last year it was more than double that figure, i.e. eur 15 billion. however, we still have an under-utilisation of eur 9 billion, which is an unacceptable figure. for 2003, implementation was even lower at the beginning of the year than in the same period in 2002; in particular, commitments entered into in categories 3 and 4 were lower in april than at the same time last year. there are a lot of different problems. our recent delegation visit to bosnia, like a number of other contacts, also raises concerns about the fact that the lack of information on the new financial regulation has created problems for the external delegations, for example. this may have negative effects on the efficiency gains of the reform process and decentralisation. we also saw some problems in the common foreign and security policy, for example the eu police mission in bosnia, in terms of tenders, contracts, etc. in this respect, we think that the commission should examine whether special rules are not needed to suit our actions in the common foreign and security policy. within the specific categories of expenditure, we remain critical of the handling of export refunds for the transport of live animals. we regret that the council did not adopt our proposal for the accounting plan, which would have made the system much more transparent. so we'll have to try again this year. i do not want to be critical, but we can also see that rural policy, for example, is actually exceeding our expectations in terms of implementation. however, we are most concerned about structural measures. the share of payments made in this area has fallen in the first months of the year, despite the harsh criticism of previous years. we are also concerned about the increase in outstanding commitments, which amounted to almost eur 94 billion as at 30 april 2003 - an incredible sum! if that's the case, something new has to happen. several colleagues have mentioned the n+2 provision, which we must ensure is applied effectively. as regards the structural funds, we welcome the simplification process initiated by the commission. we believe, however, that it must be considerably more powerful in the current regulation, but above all that preparations for a major review in the new regulation must be launched quickly. in category 3 we are a little concerned about research and development. however, we are pleased to see a positive development in the sme programmes and the sme pilot project for enlargement. we also welcome the inclusion of pilot projects and preparatory measures in the 2003 implementation plan agreed last autumn. as regards external action, implementation in april this year was clearly lower than in the same period in 2002, which is a cause for concern. however, we welcome the swift agreement to activate 79 million from the reserve for disaster aid to iraq, as well as the new proposals for southern africa and ethiopia. we can only hope that the situation in iraq and the middle east will not create such uncertainty for the 2003 budget that it could cause problems for the other areas of external policy. we're not going to accept that. finally, the commission one of the fundamental problems we have with budget implementation is that we have a rigid budgetary system on the whole which would need to be modernised and changed. but this is essentially a task for the convention, which will soon have completed its work, and for the future constitution. in that respect, i hope we will see radical reform in the future.

country. it is clear that the introduction of the euro was very successful. we have finally got a concrete common european symbol. the euro is already the second largest currency in the world. the euro has strengthened european identity and brought the twelve together. although economic developments are shaky, the euro has already demonstrated positive economic benefits, boosting trade and investment for euro area countries. one of the issues that berenguer fuster was also involved in and which has been discussed in connection with the introduction of the euro has been whether there have been price increases, and if so, whether there have been unreasonable ones. eurostat statistics clearly show that there was a very small one-off effect of the introduction of the euro, between 0.1 and 0.2%. however, in some sectors of the economy, in some sectors, there have been significant price increases, particularly in some countries. however, the overall impact on euro area residents has been very small. it is good that we are now raising this and asking the commission to look into it further. experience has shown that active consumers and double pricing have prevented surreptitious increases. but let us not be fooled into thinking that the price increases were greater than they actually were, nor let us use this argument in debates. the great advantage, madam president, is that the euro has provided economic stability in very difficult times politically and economically. despite the great unrest in the world, due in particular to the iraq war, europe's currencies have not been tossed around in a stormy sea of currencies, as actually happened in the 1990s. the euro has brought stability and security. it's a fact that should be mentioned more often. mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to warmly thank mr van den bos for his dedicated and far-sighted work on human rights in the world. i would like to highlight in particular the issues of defence of religious freedom, which mr van den bos has laid down in this document and which i myself have been able to help to strengthen. draws attention to the threat of punishment for conversion and the ban on missionary activities; we condemn acts of violence based on a misinterpretation of religion or belief. points out the vulnerability of religious minority believers around the world; an important finding of the document is that atheistic extremism can undermine the ability of people to live together peacefully. we also warn against intolerance based on atheism. it states that the rights of women and girls are threatened by child marriage. we also point to the important role of the media in spreading knowledge about religions and cultures. a key principle emphasized in the van den bos report is the right to change or renounce one's religion and to seek one's own spiritual home in freedom without legislation that represses or threatens punishment for changing one's faith and religion. we want to remove the ban on and the threat of proselytizing. we want to oppose punishments and other practices that are carried out in the name of religion and that violate human rights and freedoms. finally, this important document contains justified reservations on the election of libya as president of the un commission on human rights. criticism is also directed at cuba, where 78 human rights activists have been imprisoned for their work for human rights and freedoms.

the question for the commission and the council is of course: what are you doing to address these problems? i would like to comment on three areas. the first concerns the asylum policies of the eu and its member states, which deserve criticism. there is a political anxiety that makes all countries seem to accept the violation of fundamental legal principles. of course we must stop human smuggling and prevent the abuse of the asylum system, but we cannot send refugees back to be tortured or mistreated. we must give all the children who apply to the eu a chance at a life of dignity. there are many shortcomings in this area, including in my own country. the second area concerns prison conditions. we have overcrowded prisons, rampant violence and increasing problems with reintegration into society. europe must have prisons that are worthy of human life. the third area concerns a sensitive but important issue. the eu charter of fundamental rights clearly states that 'the freedom and diversity of the media shall be respected'. i agree with eurig wyn and wonder if it is the irony of fate that it is the italian presidency which is to get the member states to agree on a constitution which the country is clearly violating through the media monopoly of its prime minister. we can't have it like this. i would like to ask you, commissioner, what you are doing in this delicate matter. the media situation in italy is the responsibility of the whole of europe, including yours, mr vitorini.
in writing. - (sv) the european parliament's delegation of moderates considers it very important that parliament submits a report every year to see to what extent the member states respect human rights. human rights are the most important foundation of european cooperation. clearly, we must demand as much from our own countries as from the candidate countries and the other countries outside the eu. however, it is a pity that the report does not focus solely on fundamental rights but also expresses certain other political objectives. whether these political views are shared or not, this approach risks diminishing the importance of fundamental rights. that is why we cannot vote in favour of the whole of this report.
in writing. - (sv) unaccompanied minors and asylum seekers (paragraphs 51 and 96 of the resolution, explanatory memorandum page 69): the report contains gaps in this respect. in sweden, an unaccompanied child who is returned to his/her country of origin must always be accompanied by someone who will ensure that he/she is taken in by a parent or other person responsible for him/her. before a child is sent back to his/her country of origin (or to another country), a thorough investigation is carried out to determine who will take responsibility for him/her and welcome him/her on arrival. unaccompanied children should always be entrusted to trustworthy persons. the problem at the moment is that it takes a long time to name these people who are then entrusted with too many children. this type of inquiry must take the child into account more. sometimes there is not enough information about what happened to the child in his/her country of origin. discrimination (page 48): contrary to what is stated in the report, the additional protocol to the un convention on the elimination of all forms of discrimination against women of 6 october 1999 was ratified by sweden on 24 april 2003 and entered into force on 24 july 2003. trade union rights (pages 65, 101): sweden does not have - and has never had - a trade union monopoly. (page 97): in swedish law, strikes without prior warning are prohibited and considered illegal or unlawful strikes. the swedish labour market is regulated by collective agreements. any strike is prohibited during the period of the agreement.
in writing. - (sv) increased trade is undoubtedly the greatest chance for developing countries to improve their long-term economic position and the living conditions of the poorest. therefore, aid to developing countries should first aim at developing and improving such infrastructure to enable them to engage more easily in trade. this report is marked by a general scepticism about trade as a means of combating poverty and promoting economic development. what therefore influenced our final vote on the report was whether or not all the amendments tabled by the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and the european democrats to improve the report were approved. we are not against a code of conduct with ethical clauses for companies investing in or doing business with developing countries, but we believe that this code of conduct should be defined within the framework of the work of the wto and the world bank and not be limited to eu companies only.
mr president, i would like to thank you. thank you for your warm words about anna lindh. we feel sadness and loss for anna, both in sweden, europe and the whole world. as you said, our thoughts are with her family, her husband and her two sons. anna managed to combine what we often talk about, namely family life, parenting and a very politically active life. anna had daily contact with her two sons. she was going home to the parent-teacher conference that was being held at the school where her boys were attending. anna was an unusual politician of the very best kind: one day she was at the un, the next she met the foreign ministers, and the next she was in the square with the immigrants in sweden or in a discussion with schoolchildren. she was deeply committed not only to domestic and international politics but also to european cooperation. she did a lot to anchor european issues in sweden. she also did much for sweden in europe and promoted europe's cause in the world. it was a privilege to work with anna. she was knowledgeable, she was committed, she was almost always happy, and she was thoughtful. we can best honour the memory of anna by fighting for democracy, human rights, a more open society and an enlarged eu, which was one of anna's main issues. she fought for wider and deeper european cooperation and a more active foreign and security policy. by doing so, we honor anna's memory in the best way possible.
