the rapporteur. madam president, madam commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your work. energy is one of the eu's key issues. we must take care of the environment and security of supply, develop new sources of energy, especially renewable sources, use the energy we have in a more prudent and efficient way, and so on. this is a very important issue, and oil is also part of it, and oil security is also part of it. does europe need common rules for oil supply and greater coordination? that is the way to sum up the debate on the proposal for a directive on the security of oil supplies in europe. for most people, the answer would be obvious. it is clear that it is good to have more solidarity in europe, also in the field of energy supply, but at the same time we are entering areas where the member states consider that each country should be sovereign in determining the degree of joint commitments. my report attempts to balance these contradictions. most agree that there is a need to achieve better coordination of energy supply in europe, but at the same time member states want to retain a large measure of control. this is particularly evident in the case of oil supplies. my report amends the proposal for a directive on a number of points. i have tried, through a series of amendments, to find the balance between the interests of the member states and the interests of the community as a whole which is necessary to arrive at a solution which can work well for the oil market and for the needs of the various countries. the proposals to increase oil supply readiness to build up larger stocks from 90 to 120 days may seem good but also have drawbacks. the cost of increasing stocks is relatively high, and it affects the environment both in terms of transport and storage. i therefore choose to maintain the current commitment of 90 days. the proposal to be able to release oil stocks even in the absence of a physical shortage may seem good at first sight, but it is clear that such a system also involves major problems and risks. the market isn't always right. we have seen this in many so-called oil crises. at the same time, it is extremely difficult to establish a regulatory framework and to agree on clear and objective criteria that will work in all the different situations that we may find ourselves in when oil prices rise rapidly. i therefore reject the commission's proposal to allow the release of oil stocks even if there is no physical shortage. my proposal stresses the need for greater coordination and joint responsibility for oil supplies within the union. this requires changes in the current regulatory framework without negatively altering the existing system, in which countries outside the eu and europe also participate. existing structures and commitments will continue to apply, but coordination needs to be improved. the committee adopted a number of amendments which balance community and national interests. the majority of my own amendments were adopted, the most significant ones. some of my amendments were rejected. i choose to reintroduce only one of the amendments that were rejected in committee. it is about how the responsibility for emergency storage is organised. the commission wants a public body to have this task. today, the situation is different in the member states. i think that things may very well look different in the future. my amendment is designed to give member states the opportunity to choose the method which they consider best suited to their own country, without this being to the disadvantage of other member states. i therefore propose in amendment no 28 that it is up to the member states to decide whether this task should be carried out by a public body or whether the individual member states should choose a different form. two amendments propose that the draft directive be rejected. the proposal is that the proposers believe that there is already a functioning regulatory framework and that no changes are needed. this is not the case for several reasons. firstly, there are many shortcomings in the current regulatory framework, both in terms of coordination within the union and in terms of solidarity in a crisis situation. secondly - and i think members should consider this - we are probably missing the opportunity to have an impact in this important area. i find that remarkable. madam president, i would like to thank you. if parliament adopts this report on the proposal for a directive, with the proposed amendments, the consequence will be that we will also have to repeal a number of earlier directives, namely directives 68/414/eec, 98/93/ec and 73/238/eec.
the rapporteur. mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. i would like to begin by thanking my colleagues in the committee for the good cooperation. the committee almost unanimously rejects the austrian initiative to draw up a list of so-called safe third countries. we all want europe's asylum and refugee policy to be based on humanism and the rule of law. most of us want to see regulated immigration and asylum policies that provide protection and refuge to those who are persecuted, tortured or risking their lives and health. the european union's asylum policy should not protect unscrupulous smugglers, but it should not mean that we should attack refugee ships with gunboats or even, as an italian minister said, shell refugee boats, or treat people who come to our continent as second-class citizens, move them around and collect them in camps outside the eu. commissioner vitorino, if the eu cannot solve the integration of migrants and refugees and provide a framework for asylum policy, which is characterised by humanism and the rule of law, we have a ticking time bomb just waiting to explode in social and economic tensions within the union and in external pressure from all those outside who want to enter. let us never forget that the geneva convention gives every human being the unique and distinctive right to seek asylum in a foreign country to escape persecution and suffering! this proposal defines a safe third country as a state which has ratified the geneva convention, the european convention for the protection of human rights and fundamental freedoms and the international covenant on civil and political rights of 1966. the list of safe third countries in europe includes the current 15 eu member states, the 12 candidate countries, norway, iceland and switzerland. however, of the 15 countries mentioned in the list, only switzerland would actually remain. why would you do that? on 16 april 2003, the 10 member states signed the treaty of accession in athens, which means that the dublin ii regulation and the schengen acquis will apply to them. the commission has also proposed that the dublin ii regulation should not apply to romania and bulgaria until the council has taken a decision at a later date, article 6 (2) of the proposal. for norway and iceland, the regulation will apply through the dublin ii regulation and the schengen acquis as soon as they notify their acceptance and transposition. for these reasons, mr president, mr commissioner, only switzerland would be included in the list, which is a very limited list. the commission is also invited to monitor the development of the third countries on the list and to monitor compliance with the principles, but no specific procedure for adding or removing countries is provided for in the proposal. the committee stresses that, in line with the principle of subsidiarity, all the criteria for drawing up a list of safe third countries must be regarded as minimum standards which allow member states to apply more stringent standards. today, only four member states, the united kingdom, germany, finland and denmark, currently have lists of safe third countries, either by law or by administrative practice. the committee therefore questions the appropriateness of a regulation. many countries would then be forced to implement changes without a principled political debate. the committee also stresses that the issue of safe third countries is dealt with in the directive on the asylum procedure and should continue to be dealt with there. this is a personal comment on whether a common list is really desirable, which i strongly doubt. the italian presidency, which will be present here, has promised that the framework directive on the asylum procedure will be ready in the autumn. that was also said in thessaloniki. we in this house have said that we want to be consulted again after the directive has been reworked. what about that thing? i was going to ask the italian minister, but i don't see anyone in that chair. it would be interesting to know whether we will be consulted on this matter, but perhaps commissioner vitorino can answer that. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, on the whole and in the light of these arguments, we ask that parliament tomorrow reject the austrian initiative.
rapporteur. – mr president, i propose that we now vote on the draft legislative resolution, to confirm the rejection of the austrian initiative.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you. in line with our earlier decision to reject the proposal for a directive, i do not think we should vote on the following report, since it is a question of repealing the earlier directives.
the commission shall submit to the european parliament and to the council a report on the implementation of this decision. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the commission's preliminary draft budget contained few surprises as regards the matters falling within the remit of the committee on economic and monetary affairs. we therefore support the main points and have also clearly expressed our support for the amounts proposed by the commission. i would, however, like to make a few comments and recommendations. the actions supported under budget line 02 05 01 ('actions to promote industrial competitiveness in the european union'), such as analysing the impact of the information society on competitiveness and stimulating the development of electronic commerce, do not provide any real european added value and should therefore be discontinued. we therefore propose to transfer these appropriations to budget line 02 02 03 ('programme for enterprises and entrepreneurship, especially smes') where the funds could directly benefit european enterprises. we are also concerned about the problems and irregularities at eurostat and would therefore like the european parliament to request that 25% of the appropriations for eurostat's administrative expenditure be placed in title 31 (reserves) until the commission has given parliament sufficient assurance that the problems have been solved and those responsible have been identified and have received appropriate sanctions. we also support the information campaign on the euro and welcome the commission's proposal to increase its funding. these additional resources will be needed in view of the union's ten new member states, all of whom have committed themselves to adopting the euro as soon as possible. however, we remain concerned about the implementation of this budget line in recent years and would therefore again call on the commission to report every quarter to the responsible committee of parliament on the implementation of the programme in the current year and the planning for the next year. we also want to ask the commission to ensure that additional funds are available for the information campaign on the euro should additional needs arise, for example as a result of referendums in one of the countries that do not have the euro today. i would like to express my regret that my home country, sweden, will not be eligible for funding for the information campaign on the euro because of the result of the referendum last week. the committee on budgets, however, supports the commission's proposed budget with the reservations i have expressed.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to begin by thanking the commission and mr whitehead for the work that has been done. it cannot be overstated how important a proper consumer policy is for the eu. consumer policy has many important purposes. it shall contribute to sustainable development, production and consumption. it is also necessary to change our consumer behaviour, and in this context small consumer organisations are very important. i would like to mention in particular the support given to small consumer organizations and, in particular, to the new member states. they must not be deterred by the fact that it takes them a long time to be informed of the cofinancing projects, but the procedure must be changed so that they are informed quickly so that they can continue their good work.
the rapporteur. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. this concerns the commission's communication on adequate and stable pensions and an assessment of whether the member states have met the three overall objectives of the common pension policy in their initial strategy reports. firstly, whether pensions are adequate, whether we have a sufficient level of future pensions to avoid poverty and provide a reasonable standard of living. secondly, whether pensions are financially sustainable in the long term, so that future generations do not have an unnecessary financial burden. thirdly, whether pensions are modern enough to cope with the new situation, with atypical jobs and an increasing number of women in the labour market. we think the commission's communication is a good one. the commission has made a good analysis. we in parliament have tried to stay away from the national debates which are taking place around various national changes to pension systems. we have tried to stick to our task at eu level, namely to set objectives for the common strategy we will have at eu level. the member states themselves may deal with the concrete proposals for pension schemes. we have a number of views. firstly, there must be a balance between social and economic considerations. it is important for economic development that we have sustainable pension systems. this applies not only to the countries participating in the euro area but also to the other countries, so that we can achieve sustainable economic development. it is equally important, however, to address the social aspects and to touch on people's living standards. we must ensure that the elderly have a decent standard of living, which does not deviate too much from that which they had as workers, and that they do not end up in poverty. the pension systems still lack gender equality. women do not have the same pension as men. this is sometimes due to pension schemes, but the main reason is the situation of women in the labour market. women are more likely to be in lower paid jobs, parttime work and lower employment rates. this means that the lisbon process is the most important in this context. the situation of those in atypical jobs is also not satisfactory, particularly as regards the supplementary pensions agreed by the social partners. there are reasons for raising this issue. the most important thing for us to meet our commitments in the future is to achieve growth and full employment. it is therefore important that we link this to the lisbon process, the employment guidelines and the economic guidelines and see it as a whole. we often talk about solidarity between our generations in the here and now, and it is important that there is solidarity between generations right now. however, given demographic trends, it is just as important to talk about solidarity with future generations so that we - i am myself a member of the forty-something generation - do not overspend now, so that future generations of workers will have to bear unreasonably high costs when we retire. we must ensure that this does not happen in the new pension systems. we must show solidarity with future generations. the most controversial issue in the committee was the actual and statutory retirement age. we're not interfering with the statutory retirement age. paradoxically, the older population is getting healthier and healthier, which is good, while working less and less. we work less today than we did 30 years ago. there are many reasons for this. if there is anything missing from the commission's communication, it is perhaps the overall picture of how to raise the effective retirement age. it is about economic incentives, but it is also about lifelong learning, working environment issues, work organisation and so on so that people who have come of age can also remain in the labour market. we need to change working life in such a way that people can stay and be active in working life and not be excluded. then we will also discuss enlargement. finally, i would like to thank everyone who has contributed to the broad consensus we have reached in the committee, not quite complete but a broad consensus. we have had a good working relationship in the committee.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i and the gue/ngl group are of the opinion that a pension system must be based on solidarity between the generations. that is why i am in favour of a 'pay as you go' system which is much more stable. savings schemes that involve dependence on capital markets in one way or another are a much more unstable project. in my home country, sweden, which has been cited as an example in some quarters, we have moved towards a capitalisation of pension schemes and greater reliance on capital markets. with the victory of the 'no' side in the swedish referendum on the euro, we have gained a little more freedom in economic policy, including pension policy. i think that is exactly what the swedish 'no' voters wanted: greater freedom of action and solidarity in sweden.
mr president, mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your report. i should like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for his work. i share his view that the proposal is a small step in the right direction. back in 1999, pat cox wrote a similar report. if the council had followed his instructions, the conditions would have been different today. the long time it takes to deal with this case shows, as the commissioner says, how important it is that we have a fair decisionmaking procedure. today's environmental problems must be seen from a pan-european perspective. the eu is a unique tool for creating common environmental rules. the eu's environmental policy should be extended by means of stricter minimum rules and environmental taxes. such rules and charges shall be decided by qualified majority. as you know, this is a highly sensitive issue. some of my colleagues shudder physically at the mention of eu taxes. i hope, however, that the igc can find solutions on energy and environmental taxes and a robust decision-making system. the eu and its member states' role in the energy sector is to formulate safety and environmental requirements, to monitor compliance with these requirements and to promote research and development in the energy sector, research aimed at replacing fossil-based energy production with environmentally friendly energy sources. we should, however, be careful not to use taxes as a weapon to seriously disrupt competition in the energy sector. a rational energy policy is best promoted by free trade and free production of energy. the eldr group will support the majority of the amendments.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i would like to congratulate the rapporteur on an outstanding job. against all odds, she's managed to forge a viable compromise. today, stock exchanges in the eu have a virtual monopoly on securities trading, particularly in certain national markets. now we have competition and freedom of choice and, mr katiforis, good investor protection. i would like to comment briefly on the two sensitive articles 18 and 25. as regards article 18, we think it is important that the suitability test should be reserved for advisory activities, not for example internet-based securities trading. these clients have deliberately chosen a brokerage service where they will not have to pay for professional advice. it's only gonna cost more. we shall therefore vote in favour of the committee's amendments nos 53, 54 and 55. the most discussed article in the proposal is article 25, which deals with the publication of price information for transactions outside regulated markets or mtfs, the internalisation of trading. we see this as a small risk. it would reduce liquidity in the market and raise costs. the deletion of this article has not found sufficient political support, but we believe that the committee's amendments nos 33, 86, 87, 88 and 89 would remedy the negative effects that the proposed rules on the publication of prices would have on the market. finally, mr president, i would like to ask why we are debating such an important proposal so late at night.
in writing. - (sv) the european patent convention currently in force is interpreted differently in different member states. this is obviously creating a certain degree of legal uncertainty and has negative effects on the internal market. we therefore believe that a european directive is necessary. the proposal for a directive on the patentability of computer-implemented inventions has been the subject of considerable debate. we share the view of many who are opposed to granting too large a scope to the concept of patents, as this would be directly harmful and would lead to a reduction in inventiveness. we therefore voted in favour of the amendments which seek to restrict the concept of a patent. we supported the report in the final vote.
in writing. - (sv) in general, the draft european convention and the opinion of the parliament are satisfactory. we are particularly pleased with the inclusion of full employment, transparency, equality and sustainable development as important points in the draft constitution of the convention, which is also welcomed in parliament's opinion. however, our opinion differs from that expressed in the opinion on certain points: - it should be stated that community law has primacy over national law only where the eu has legislative powers. - the european parliament cannot be the sole parliamentary body in matters of common foreign and security policy. national parliaments must also be consulted. - we support the solidarity clause in the draft constitution and, on the whole, we welcome the provisions of the draft constitution relating to the european security and defence policy. however, we have many doubts about what is called 'structured' and 'closer' cooperation in the draft constitution and we believe that by saying yes to 'structured cooperation' the opinion is aimed precisely at this type of cooperation. - the provisions of the draft constitution relating to the commission are not satisfactory. each member state should have a full commissioner with voting rights. - we oppose the "bridge" clause in the draft constitution and the idea that the constitution can be amended without unanimity and without ratification. - the use of unanimity should not be abandoned in general for foreign and security policy and for fiscal policy, as proposed in the opinion. - the time at which the intergovernmental conference is to end must not be specified. the igc must last as long as necessary, regardless of the presidency.
in writing. - (sv) we decided to abstain in the final vote on the report. our position is on the report and not on the outcome of the convention as a whole. the report does not follow the outcome of the convention on certain points. we support the work to shape a constitution for europe. we supported the convention method which has changed the way european treaties are drawn up in a unique way. we are in favour of a consolidated treaty with a clearer division of powers. it is good that decision-making is improved and simplified and that the institutions are more accountable to the citizens. we support the compromise of the convention, even if we do not agree with certain points. the eu must be limited and effective. a constitution should clearly define the boundary between the public and private spheres and the eu's decision-making powers in relation to those of the member states. the treaty belongs to the member states. it is therefore clear that any amendment to the treaty must be approved unanimously and ratified by the national parliaments. this also means that we have some reservations about article i-24.4 of the draft constitution. the swedish moderates, like the swedish parliament, oppose the idea of a permanent presidency of the council. we cannot accept a move towards parliamentarism at european level either. we maintain that the eu should not be given decision-making powers in relation to social policy and taxation. we are looking for an intensive dialogue and do not see why it is essential that the intergovernmental conference should be closed this autumn.
in writing. - (sv) i deeply regret that the majority of the european parliament rejected (by 283 votes against, 211 votes for and 15 abstentions) amendment no 23 by the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and european democrats, which sought to expressly mention the judeo-christian religious heritage in the preamble to the constitution. this proposal should not be controversial insofar as it describes a historical fact. we want each member state to have a commissioner on an equal footing with the others and with voting rights in the future too. but parliament is against it. we also regret the support of parliament for the creation of an eu president, which would mean the abolition of the rotating presidency of the council and thus the right of small countries to lead the eu. i am very critical of the 'bridge' clause in article i-24, which allows the council's decision-making rules to be amended by unanimity without the approval of national parliaments and without the convening of an intergovernmental conference within the eu.
in writing. - (sv) i cannot support the draft constitution for europe for several reasons. i am opposed to the move towards a european state and always recommend intergovernmental cooperation. i cannot support the proposal to increase military cooperation, because i believe that this is a national issue. the construction of a state requires a common constitution, a common currency, a common court of justice and a common defence. if we add a common president, a common foreign minister and a common finance minister and if we abolish the right of veto on important issues, this further strengthens the construction of a state. such a fundamental change of direction for the eu should be put to the people of europe through referendums. the members of the convention, who have done a remarkable job, were also not democratically elected, and the decision-making procedures within the convention, where no voting took place, raise considerable objections.
madam president, ladies and gentlemen of the commission! i shall not criticise the commission or the european parliament. i will just say that i have personally become aware that something important has happened with the wto meeting in cancún ending in a meltdown. this does not have to be the end of the wto, nor should it be, but it does mean that the eu and the us will have to adapt to a new situation. we have actually been totally dominant as actors in the wto, but by setting the agenda ourselves, we have given the developing countries a feeling of being left out, a feeling of being overtaken. when we prepare -- and we prepare quite well -- we do so, of course, because we want to do well, but we haven't thought enough about the world's best interests. the time for this kind of policy is over. we must recognize that the world's most populous countries, such as china and india, and large producing countries, such as brazil, are taking on a clear profile on the world trade map, and the poorest developing countries are beginning to be recognized. it is important that we get a new map of world trade.

i suggest. madam president, i would like to say a few words, first of all to thank the european parliament for the contribution it has made to us today. thirty-one speeches underline the importance this institution - the most representative institution of the european union - attaches to the value of multilateralism, on which we must act. you have affirmed, reiterated and stressed the need to save and revive multilateralism, and at the same time many of you have stressed the importance of launching a process of reform of the wto, which cannot be done without the recognition of new actors, in order to achieve this. we cannot go back to the gatt mechanisms, but we must move forward, recognizing that the world is fortunately multipolar, and this applies to the north as much as to the south, as is also shown by the implementation of the g21 agreement with which we must cooperate. this agreement consists mainly of developing countries which already enjoy the benefits of globalization and which have therefore become strong competitors and rightly claim their own space, from china to brazil, for all. this triangle between multilateralism to be saved, the wto to be reformed and new actors to be recognised should lead us to ask for a process to be set in motion which could then lead to the creation of some intermediate body between the secretariat and the assembly, in which this new representation could finally find a point of synthesis between actors representing geographical, political, cultural, social and economic areas that are homogeneous but different, and which could find an important moment to untie the knots before they reach that assembly which in cancún seemed really a veritable babble of languages and positions. another important aspect that was underlined concerns the all-european problem of communication. some people have noted how the role of ngos has been dubious, perhaps ambiguous, between the language spoken in brussels or strasbourg and the language sometimes shouted in cancún, and at the same time how the european union and the commission have had difficulty in communicating what europe has done in these 22 months and what it does on a daily basis to the weakest of the weak. i would like to give an example. i believe that the commission had two press officers in cancún, whereas one non-governmental organization had as many as fourteen. there is therefore a communication gap, and perhaps a confusion of language, which we must overcome if we are to be able to give value to the extraordinary strength of european civil society, which must be expressed first and foremost - though not only - in its representative bodies. finally, ask if the mandate needs to be changed or given more flexibility. i think it is our duty to take your views to the european council, especially in view of, close to and after the meeting on 15 december, when we will perhaps be able to understand better what the impact of this failure has been on the other actors as well, even on those who perhaps unknowingly have to some extent too soon rejoiced in this failure. i think we will have to discuss this in the coming months, to see whether we need more flexibility on singapore issues - as some have called for - more flexibility on social and environmental issues, more flexibility also on agricultural policy. finally, i believe that your contribution in this respect is important, indeed decisive. (applause) i would like to thank
the commission is not prepared to accept the amendments tabled by the committee on budgets. first of all, i would like to thank all of you for your contributions to this debate. this debate was very important. in my reply i would like to focus on a few points which i think need further discussion and some clarification. firstly, there was a debate on whether the multilateral approach or bilateral agreements should take precedence. i don't think there is an alternative. there is no better approach than the multilateral approach and we must stick to it. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the future of the european union. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. finally, i think it would also be a mistake to fall into the trap of making a hasty turn in bilateral negotiations, opening up opportunities to reap what we might call a double harvest, first of all what we can take home under bilateral agreements, and then having no problem whatsoever with making massive additional demands, especially in multilateral negotiations. i think that from a european point of view, a certain degree of caution is required here. but what we have to do bilaterally, and what we have to do, is to have direct discussions, direct negotiations with many individual countries that are members of the wto, in the sense that we make our position clear to them in the multilateral negotiations. secondly, i firmly believe that the reform of the common agricultural policy was the right thing to do. we have these reforms - and i can also mention them here - because we have had this debate in this room. we have said that european society demands reforms and that is why we need them. we cannot now say in retrospect that we pursued our project to make european agriculture and european agricultural policy more sustainable, for example, simply because we needed an argument to make it in international negotiations. that would be turning things upside down. of course, this has given us a certain flexibility in international negotiations, and of course there is also the fact that we always have to reform internally first and that our negotiating mandate then follows from that. or do you think parliament would be prepared to give the commission a negotiating mandate which goes beyond the existing common policies? it is unthinkable, it will never work, and therefore we should not have any illusions here either. but we must also not tolerate attempts to 'simply put in our pockets' what we contribute to our reforms by saying, for example, that the reduction commitments are not set by what we agreed last time in the uruguay round, which would then be chosen as a starting point, but by what we are currently applying, for example, to distortive support measures. that would be unfair to us, and we must make that clear. thirdly, ngos have played a major role in this debate. we should not make the mistake of pretending that the ngos have not been good enough and turn off the money or something like that. i think that's nonsense. but what we have to do is to seek the discussion with the ngos. we must confront them and their arguments. there is a rather large need, for example, when i look at how different the positions of the same ngo are, depending on whether you are talking to the department responsible for development policy or the department responsible for environmental policy. it is not possible to say - and many ngos have done this - that european agriculture must be environmentally friendly and that we must use the budget for environmental services in agriculture! we are doing this now, but we cannot say afterwards that whatever form of support is given to agriculture, it is bad. it is not compatible, and we must conduct this debate in a sensible way. in this way we can also reduce the distrust of the developing countries, some of whom feel that the complexity of the negotiations and the difficulties are being built up only to make it more difficult for those who have not been involved in these negotiations for a long time to gain access to these things. we have to break down barriers and tear down. above all, we should try to get back to the facts. we should involve more independent institutions, such as the oecd and others, to achieve an objective approach to the debate. there is a saying in agriculture: it is one thing to lead a horse to the well, but it is another to make it drink. there must also be a willingness to listen to arguments and to really engage in dialogue. we'll get through this! as for the peace clause, i would point out that it is in any case due to expire at the end of this year. the wto's agricultural structure is not collapsing, but there is a possibility that the way in which we in europe have implemented the commitments of the uruguay round may be attacked. i also want to warn a little bit that different states think this is a good opportunity to start many panels. this would be another step towards damaging the wto process as such. so you should think about all this very carefully. finally, we must achieve one thing: better communication of the basic philosophy of what negotiations in the wto actually mean. negotiation can only mean that there is a center point to which the various parties are moving. it can't mean that you're going to extremes. if this principle is not properly observed in the preparation, the risk of failure in the future remains quite high. so i can only suggest that we not put things down as an ad acta, but draw the necessary conclusions analyse carefully and have a debate and then, if possible, decide together what the next steps should be to as they say get the whole thing back on track. (applause from the centre)

mr president, i would like to thank you. i too would like to begin by congratulating the rapporteur, mr michael cashman, and his co-rapporteur, mrs maij-weggen, on their excellent work in significantly improving this regulation, increasing transparency and facilitating access to the documents of the institutions for citizens. the results of the 2001 review are encouraging. clear progress has been made and progress is being made towards a more open approach which will hopefully also lead to citizens making better use of their rights. however, it is clear from mr cashman's report that there is still a great deal to be done and that there are still major shortcomings. one such serious shortcoming is that when electronic systems are created, they are created in the form of multiple systems rather than a single system. i am grateful to commissioner de palacio for emphasising this and the need for a uniform and coordinated electronic case management system, which allows cases to be followed throughout the process. otherwise, it will be impossible for citizens to participate in such a process. on the other hand, the council seems to interpret article 4 of the regulation consistently in a too restrictive manner, that is to say, in relation to public security. this must be dealt with immediately. in amendment no 2 we have proposed an amendment to paragraph 4 to make that happen. point 16 worries me a little. it calls for the harmonization of the publicity legislation of the member states' administrations. i do not think that this will lead to greater transparency and greater access to documents, but i suggest that you vote for amendment no 3 by the green group.
mr president, i would like to thank you. there have been shortcomings - a lot of them - in the area of transparency. in fact, however, it can now be seen that the three institutions have undergone a minor revolution in the area of transparency and public access to documents. if we start from 2001, when we started with a rather closed culture, we can now see that parliament provides 90 per cent of all documents requested and that the commission and the council provide 60-70 per cent. however, there is something to consider in this context, namely the basic principle that openness is the rule and closure is the exception. we all know that, but i don't think it's penetrated every part of the organization and every part of the citizenry. i am convinced that it will happen, but we have a responsibility to drive this process forward. a more detailed analysis of several issues of current interest is required. i am very pleased and i would like to thank mr cashman for expressing a to contribute to the lack of transparency. i therefore believe that this is a matter which must be carefully examined before positions are taken. there are areas that really need to be discussed. one such area which we have discussed for a long time is the sensitive issue of the opinions of the legal services of the institutions. we are now awaiting a court decision on that. i hope that some parts of it will be opened up soon and that we will not see a systematic classification. a first step might be to limit the confidentiality in part for a time. certain terms need to be defined more clearly to avoid misuse, such as overarching public interest and sensitive issues in the legislative process. however, transparency and access to documents are far from giving citizens only formal rights. it is equally important that the institutions and their documents should be made available. there is now, at last, a clear upsurge of interest in eu issues across europe. we are beginning to see a growing debate, but the institutions do not yet seem to have been able to take full advantage of this increased interest. i hope we will do better and make the institutions more citizen-friendly. in this respect, transparency legislation is a cornerstone. at the last meeting of the interinstitutional committee we discussed the possibility of producing material showing the success of the work done and the possibilities for the public. the eu and its institutions are sometimes far from the citizens. advanced computer skills and special knowledge of the functioning of the institutions should not be required to benefit from the transparency rules. as for that, there is much to be done. as many speakers have stressed during the debate, simple and user-friendly registers are needed. there should be easy ways to follow the legislative process. there are a multitude of data portals and websites and different ways of retrieving information, but it requires focus, consolidation, user-friendliness, simple language and, of course, a simplified approach to searching for material. it can't be too difficult. changing the institutional culture of the eu takes time, but we have achieved a great deal in a short time. i am proud that the european parliament is the leading institution in matters of transparency. i would like to thank mr cashman in particular for his report and also for the hard work he has put in over many years and the friendship that has resulted from all this work. i would like to say a few warm words to maij-weggen as she closes and tell her how much i feature of european cooperation. we've also developed a friendship. i would like to conclude by wishing her the very best of luck in her future recruitment.

mr president, i would like to thank you. first of all, i would also like to thank margot wallström. i am well aware of her commitment to these issues, and her information on what has happened since the johannesburg meeting was welcome. i would also like to thank mr de roo for the draft resolution which we are voting on tomorrow. we all, or at least many of us, had high hopes for johannesburg, but the results were far from it. i think part of the difficulty is that sustainable development work is somehow at odds with our traditional way of working. in this context, it is necessary to work horizontally, i.e. to integrate the objective of economic growth with the objectives of social development and effective protection and responsible management of the physical environment. this integration is difficult for many reasons. we have, for one thing, no education system that facilitates the understanding of these horizontal relationships. on the contrary, most of us are trained in vertical cutting. similarly, there are blockages within the scientific community. everyone talks about collaboration across disciplines, but the vast majority of scientists are specialists in narrow fields. few have the overview and few have the sense of system and the whole. the way we organize ourselves in different parts of society is also vertical or sectoral. we can take this parliament as an example - we should have a committee on sustainable development, but instead we are sectoral. furthermore, there is a problem with the whole economic model. it is very short-term and fails to capture the long-term aspects of development, including the qualitative aspects. we still measure everything in terms of gross domestic product, the total output of goods and services. the impact of this production, or consumption for that matter, on the environment or social development is not shown. i do not think we can solve these problems by negative growth or by reducing consumption, when the point is that we must pay much more attention to the form, content and quality of growth and not just to its quantity. margot wallström, i think this is a great challenge, not least for the commission. the role of the european union in the work of sustainable development is central, not least today when we are faced with a half-hearted attitude of the administration in washington. our responsibility in this area has implications both for our internal policy and for our cooperation with the poor countries. as regards our own policies, we need to adapt the economic regulatory framework so that we provide sufficient positive incentives for far more efficient energy and material management. it also requires that we gradually phase out the use of toxic substances over a long period of time. let me give you a concrete example. on the energy supply side, which is central to the lisbon process, we could agree on an ambitious target to reduce the energy intensity of our economies or, if we wish, to increase energy efficiency, for example by 2.5 to 3% per year. this is technically possible, but the political will is lacking. as regards our relations with the poor countries, commissioner wallström has already referred to the water and energy initiatives. i can only hope that this question of water supply, the billion set aside in euros, will be resolved soon. one way would be to ensure that the edf, the european development fund, became part of the ordinary budget, something we have been asking parliament to do all along. regarding energy, i am unfortunately a bit pessimistic. the proposal put forward is very good, but there is no extra money allocated. it is therefore essential that energy supply be given priority and that sensible action be taken in this area, not only by the commission but also by the beneficiary countries, which is by no means a given. i would urge margot wallström to try to free up extra money properly and earmark it for this area. the resolution on which we are to vote tomorrow contains demands and requests for continuous reporting by the commission on progress. margot wallström has also commented on this, which is excellent. i only hope that there will be a very strong emphasis in the report on what is being done in the developing countries. in that area, we are lacking today a report worthy of the name. we lack clear objectives and clear indicators. the debate we have about our aid and the effectiveness of our action in this area is therefore very general. i think that the commission as a whole in writing. - (sv) article 2 of council regulation (eec) no 4064/89 on the control of concentrations between undertakings lays down the guidelines on which the commission is to base its assessment of whether a concentration between undertakings is compatible with the common market. instead of the current model, which focuses mainly on whether companies would acquire a dominant position, we recommend a more flexible model (known as the slc test), under which a merger between companies should be prohibited if it is likely to contribute to weakening competitiveness or creating a monopoly.
in writing. - (sv) the commission has consulted the european parliament on the proposal for a council regulation on the control of concentrations between undertakings. the commission wishes to ensure that there will be no distortion of competition in the common market. measures should therefore be taken to avoid dominant positions and oligopolies, as they may lead to higher prices, regulation of production and a decline in quality. the commission's proposal was clear and firm in this respect. many of the rapporteur's amendments, however, were aimed at mitigating the commission's proposals, on the grounds that mergers between undertakings are perfectly legal in themselves and cannot be compared to cartels. the rapporteur therefore believed that the commission should not have the same powers of investigation in the field of mergers between undertakings as in the field of cartels. the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and european democrats believes that the more binding wording of the commission is preferable to the main idea of the amendments adopted by parliament. that is why i voted 'no' in the final vote.
in writing. - (sv) we welcome the european union's move away from traditional fisheries agreements in favour of new fisheries partnership agreements with third countries. the old fisheries agreements were not generous enough to the countries that transferred fishing rights to the eu. it is hoped that the new partnership will partly lead to more sustainable fisheries that take account of the local needs of third countries and partly allow third countries to exercise control over their own fisheries in the future.

mr president, i would like to thank you. the proposal for a new bathing water directive which we are now discussing unfortunately has the same fundamental flaw as the previous directive, namely a very vague definition of what is bathing water. as a consequence, the commission has been able to interpret the term 'large number of bathers' in its own way. it is therefore unsatisfactory for the sake of legal certainty that the new directive does not contain a simple and clear definition either. it is also noteworthy that the directive does not take account of the very different conditions prevailing in the various member states. in sweden, for example, we have 322 miles of coastline and 95,700 lakes larger than one hectare. we also have what we call the right of all, which basically means that everyone living in our country has access to these bathing waters. support for amendment no 76 means a yes to a simple, clear and therefore legally secure definition. member states with different conditions than the nordic countries would also not be adversely affected. this would make it possible to apply the directive throughout the union.

and is contributing - together with the first, to bringing about that revolution which our railways need if we want them not simply to be a relic of the past but a system of the future at the service of society and the common good.

in writing. - (sv) the eu has no competence in the field of forestry policy. despite this, the programme adopted by the european parliament means that forest monitoring, biodiversity and forest fires will be centralised. the moderates do not accept that the eu is indirectly given powers in forest policy. the different forests in europe are not comparable. sweden and finland currently account for 40% of eu forest production. in sweden and finland, the forestry industry is the most important economic sector and cannot be a european subject. in these countries, we have a long tradition of economically and ecologically sustainable conservation of our forests. our forests do not have the same biodiversity as the forests of southern europe. this is due to the fundamental natural conditions, the fact that the climate is colder. we see no reason to centralize the question of forests. however, we can support fire prevention activities. unlike air and water, forests are not a cross-border issue. the moderates will continue to protect sweden's forests.
in writing. - we voted against this report. we do not think that there is a need to develop a forest policy at eu level. issues concerning forest fires and the financing of firefighting are dealt with more effectively at national level or through direct cooperation between the member states concerned.
in writing. - (pt) the compromise adopted on forest fire prevention and the financing of the forest focus programme is unfortunately not up to the original proposals of the rapporteur, which we supported. now, after the plenary vote, the compromise has weakened the whole report, which contained more in-depth and broader amendments on forest monitoring and the development of forest fire prevention measures. we know that the compromise adopted is intended to ensure successful negotiations with the commission and the council, but the fact is that the funding for the programme has now been cut by eur 6 million, whereas it was originally proposed to increase it by eur 15 million. it is also regrettable that the financing of prevention measures has fallen below the required levels and has not been allocated precisely. following the disastrous forest fires last summer and the need to ensure that sufficient funding is available to preserve the eu's forest heritage, the reservations issued by the council and some national groups in the european parliament are incomprehensible. this compromise is only distantly related to the position adopted by the european parliament in its resolution of 4 september 2003 on the effects of the last summer heat wave.
in writing. - the redondo jiménez report gives an accurate assessment of the state of our forests but unfortunately does not propose any concrete solutions to protect them or to ensure their monitoring. as the elected representative of the paca region, i was outraged by the forest fires which have destroyed over 60 000 ha of land in france this summer, mainly in the var and upper corsica. it turns out that the vast majority of these fires are criminal in origin. criminal sanctions should therefore be strengthened against arsonists and those who order such acts. the criminalisation of these criminal acts is in line with the wishes of all the actors involved, who have also all agreed on the need to strengthen the prevention and protection of forests, which are a national heritage that we have to manage together. we also believe that human activities in the forest should be considered as a public service of general interest and that these activities should therefore be financially supported because they are not profitable. forest owners should also be given tax and financial incentives to encourage them to carry out maintenance and deforestation work. 'private forestry experts' could carry out this work. this is real environmental policy. i would like to conclude with a question: will france, like portugal, be entitled to aid from the new european solidarity fund?
in writing. - (sv) water quality is an important environmental issue and it was therefore important and commendable that the eu adopted a framework directive on water, directive 2000/60/ec. this framework directive sets the general framework for the protection of all types of water (river and lake water, coastal and groundwater). however, it is not essential that the eu has a directive regulating bathing water quality. this is an example of the eu regulating unnecessarily and in detail matters that are not even cross-border in nature. on the other hand, we would like to emphasize the autonomy of local authorities and the member states in this area.
in writing. - i abstained on the proposal for a directive on the quality of bathing water. i do not think that this directive is really necessary, at least not with such a wealth of detail. this is bureaucratic europe in all its glory! is there not a principle of subsidiarity to leave responsibility for bathing water quality to national or regional authorities?
- (it) this morning i took the seven-hour flight from milan-malpensa to strasbourg. a little sleepy, i found myself in a car with mr napolitano and mr fiori, who had taken the same plane as me to milan; i closed my eyes half, and i had a sort of vision. and what do i see, mr president? i see that this is europe in 2050: there are beautiful flowers everywhere, everything is colorful under the sun, lots of greenery; good people and good hearts, no more wars; everyone lives happily among the flowers. i approached a passerby and - what a coincidence, it was mr sacconi, the rapporteur of this report - he said to me: 'you see, despite everything that has been done in the past, the greenhouse effect has increased, it has increased, it has become enormous. the whole earth became a hot greenhouse full of flowers, like those used by those who want to grow flowers, and this is how all human beings became good people". i therefore voted in favour of this report, but i have a doubt, mr president: would it not be better, perhaps, to let the greenhouse effect increase and let things go on as they should, even with the help of humanity?
- madam president, during the previous budgetary debate, a plenary session at the end of september, i had expressed my concern about the allegations of irregularities within eurostat. since then, the commission has presented reports on the activities of this body, but it is regrettable that these do not clearly show what has happened and who is responsible. it is essential, in this respect, to stress the important role played by eurostat within the monetary union. the european central bank needs reliable statistics to make monetary policy decisions. it is therefore of the utmost importance that these irregularities be investigated without delay and that appropriate procedures be put in place so that eurostat's activities are concentrated on the production of statistics. i also believe that it is time for us to have a wider debate on the actual use of the eu budget. i would like to draw attention to some of the recommendations set out in the sapir report, which very accurately reflect the fact that the eu budget is obsolete. the expenditure, revenue and procedures are incompatible with the present and future situation. the report proposes, among other things, a considerable reduction in the cost of agricultural and regional policies and the setting-up of a reserve of funds capable of creating the basic conditions for employment and growth. each seems to agree on the importance of small and medium-sized enterprises for economic growth. it should also act in favour of those undertakings. in the future enlarged eu, it will be necessary to make a significant reduction in agricultural aid, reform the structural funds and encourage growth through private enterprise. the questions of eurostat and the future structure of the eu budget both touchent on credibility. for the eu to enjoy the trust of its citizens, it is essential that citizens' money is not diverted or wasted, but used judiciously, to the benefit of the citizen and development in the eu.
- (sv) mr president, i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. as i interpret it, this work is now entering a more intensive phase and is leading to the adoption of many practical measures. unfortunately, we have seen the commission more disposed to adopt measures which are likely to increase consumption rather than reduce it. the most recent example is the one on the last monday of the month. in a so-called reasoned opinion, the commission invited sweden to lift the ban on the private importation of alcoholic beverages via independent intermediaries - that is to say, to exempt the purchasers from the state monopoly of retail sales. such a measure would deprive us of the possibility of controlling the age of the purchaser and would make it considerably more difficult to collect the tax on alcoholic beverages. in the light of these elements, i would like to ask the commissioner a further question: if we are to develop a strategy on this important public health problem, would it not indicate that the different departments of the commission are converging towards a common objective?

- (sv) mr president, mr commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to say to the prime minister that i really appreciate the work done by mrs ferreira. i also support the proposals concerning, for example, the impossibility for parliament to accept the fact that the commission can decide, alone, on the substances which constitute sweeteners. it is also not acceptable for a substance considered as dangerous and not authorised to be sold until stocks are exhausted. health must have priority in this case. the greens/efa think that amendment 2 (article 1a (new)) is much too general and that it is disproportionately concerned with new sweeteners. in our amendment no 4, we want the commission to present a report concerning a re-evaluation of the authorisation granted to laspartame. the detailed criticisms emanating from the independent experts who submitted a question to the commission were given to me by the scientific committee on human nutrition (csah). according to these same criticisms, the scientific committee has ignored critical and independent studies and may be subject to the influence of the food industry and the manufacturers of aspartame. we cannot ignore the fact that certain information must provoke a reaction on our part in order to see the precautionary principle applied and to protect human health. a recent report published by spain shows that formaldehyde in aspartame accumulates at the cellular level, which may destroy dna and, for example, cause liver or brain damage, particularly in vulnerable people. to quote mrs oomen-ruijten, the political winds are blowing from the right and the left in this house tonight. however, this should not prevent the greens/efa group from calling for a new examination of the arguments put forward and the application of the precautionary principle when researchers diverge and adopt different positions. therefore, i call on my colleagues to vote, in practice, in favour of the amendment 4 demain.
in writing. - (sv) unlike the commission, the european parliament is in favour of compensation based on each accident rather than on the number of victims. as a result, the level of compensation per person would be half as high in the event of an accident in which two people are injured. it would be as if there was only one wounded. i find that strange, and even downright absurd. that is why i voted against the european parliament's position and support the line adopted by the commission.
in writing. - (sv) we welcome the more efficient management of the structural funds. at the same time, we believe that it would be risky to manage resources in a single fund, as recommended in mr pittella's report, as this could lead to the disappearance of smaller projects. on the contrary, priority should be given to harmonising the rules on the use of funds.
i believe that this is the eu's great opportunity to play a positive role in world peace - to refrain from trying to be a bad copy of the united states and its militarism and philosophy of violence. instead, the eu should develop a peaceful, non-violent alternative. unfortunately, the brok report is permeated by the old superstition that military intervention is the only way to bring about peace. should the eu not learn from the failures of the us in afghanistan and iraq? should not the european parliament be reminded of its own decisions in the other direction, such as the recommendation to the council on the establishment of a european civilian peace corps adopted as early as 1999? this recommendation has been ignored by the council for ten presidencies. the european parliament should also give its strong support to the hopeful talks now taking place between the beijing government and the representatives of the dalai lama. this can be done by endorsing amendment no 10, which reiterates our old demand that the eu appoint a special representative for tibet. through my contacts as vice-chairman of the delegation for relations with the people's republic of china and as a member of the european parliament's intergroup on tibet, i have contacts in both directions. i therefore know that both beijing and the dalai lama would welcome a strong initiative from the eu on this issue.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i agree with the report's analysis of the post-iraq situation, but not with all its conclusions. it is true that new threats and security risks must above all be tackled with political strategies to combat poverty, protect human rights and promote democracy. this in turn requires an effort to detect conflicts in time and to resolve them by political, diplomatic and economic means. these are the resources available to the eu and they should be increased, not the military resources. i do not, however, share the view that the credibility of the eu's foreign and security policy depends on the union's military resources. i also do not support increased cooperation in defence policy, which i believe should be subject to unanimity. i cannot support the proposal that joint military operations must be financed from the community budget and that in the long term the eu should have its own defence budget. nor can i support the idea of a common military defence of european territory. peacekeeping operations, as referred to in paragraph 34, are measures under article 7 of the un charter, i.e. direct military action, and can only be undertaken after a decision by the un security council. the eu's task is not to plan for war, but to prevent war. military defence is and should be a national matter. this is of particular importance for sweden and other non-aligned countries.
i want to start by saying that our relationship with russia is of the utmost importance. i wish i had a lot more time to talk about it, but i want to concentrate on the most pressing issue, namely the issue of chechnya. it's a very specific conflict, but there are still many parallels. we see in many places how great powers come into conflict with populations of different national identities. it is easy to say that one should do this or that, of course, but i think there is a pattern to be learned from. you can start by looking at yourself, at the eu and at europe. historically, we have had many experiences of this type of conflict. a recent example is northern ireland, where we are beginning to find a solution. another example is the basque country. no europeans today imagine that the conflicts in the basque country and northern ireland can be solved by military means. political solutions must be sought. we must find forms that are acceptable to both sides, and this of course also applies to russia in chechnya. we must insist on this with all our might. peaceful solutions must be sought and they must be based on respect for the right of the people to self-determination. the parties may discuss and agree on the concrete solution to the individual conflict. no one else can solve the conflict for them. the starting point, however, must be the self-determination of the people. democracy must be anchored from below, not from above. you can't dictate solutions from moscow. if the russian people do not realise this, it will be very damaging both for russia and for the future of cooperation between russia and the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the un charter of human rights states that everyone has the right to life, liberty and security of person. last year, 31 countries violated this vital article in support of the human, for the living and for a civilized and humane society. thirty-one countries considered themselves entitled to take the life of their own citizens. according to amnesty international, a total of 1 526 people were executed in these 31 countries, and 3 284 people were sentenced to death in 67 countries. over 80% of the executions took place in three countries: china, iran and the united states. commissioner, minister, you know all this. china is the worst of them all. it is, however, deeply tragic that the democratic united states should be placed on the same list as the two dictatorships. as a warm friend of the united states, it pains me very much. how can a democratic and civilized country like the united states continue to apply the death penalty? in the united states, the number of executions last year rose to 71, up from 66 the year before. i do not think, mr president, that many americans are aware that the united states does not meet the requirements for membership of the european union, and i am convinced that it is possible to influence the united states to change its attitude. it would make it so much harder for the other countries in the death league. would the american people be less receptive to the facts? thousands of innocent people have been sentenced to death and executed. would the american people be less humane than we europeans? no, it's not, but it would require more courageous leaders who are willing and dare to take the debate for justice and for human dignity. can the total abolition of the death penalty be gambled with, commissioner and minister? i'm not sure. i think you have to have a very clear attitude here. this popular uprising against the death penalty, the first step of which is a worldwide moratorium, must be given full support by the eu and the italian presidency. you must not hesitate here, gentlemen! the responsibility lies with you, mr minister and mr commissioner. the death penalty shall be abolished. it's not negotiable.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i think it's clear from the protocol that i am opposed to all violence. as a green and pacifist, i am of course opposed to all violence, but i also note that international law makes a distinction between the violence of the occupiers and the resistance of oppressed peoples in colonies and occupied countries against the occupier. i am a pacifist and i prefer that non-violent methods be used even against an occupier. that is my view, and it is stated in the minutes.

in writing. - (sv) the tasks described in mr savary's report and in part in mr sterckx's report are urgent. however, there is some question as to the need to set up a new agency when these tasks could be carried out by the commission and through consultations and exchanges at meetings between representatives of the railway sector in the different member states. this would save an agency and tax resources. if railway matters were dealt with by the commission, the examination of these matters could be phased out as soon as it becomes irrelevant. it is more difficult to abolish an agency, not least because of its symbolic value for the host country. the swedish christian democrats stress the need for coordination and cooperation in the railway sector in europe, but question the need for a permanent agency to manage the sector.
in writing. - (sv) the resolution calls on the intergovernmental conference to adopt 'provisions which improve' part iv of the convention's draft constitution, which deals in particular with the procedure for amending the adopted constitution. we would like to stress that such an 'improvement' should not lead to the abolition of the requirement of ratification by all member states in the event of such amendments. we also want to stress that we are able to support the establishment of a border control agency, but we are opposed to any operational structure or any form of operational cooperation.
in writing. - (sv) this report is positive in many respects. for example, it is important to note that a security strategy for the eu is currently being developed within the framework of the united nations system. the importance of international law is also emphasised on several occasions. it is clearly stated that, to combat the new threats and risks facing europe, political strategies to combat poverty, protect the climate, defend human rights and promote democracy and the rule of law are needed. however, there are some points in the report which we cannot accept. we want to maintain the principle of unanimity in foreign and security policy and we reject most firmly a common defence of the territory of europe. we are also opposed to any increase in military resources and to the creation of a community defence budget. that is why we cannot vote in favour of the report.
in writing. - (sv) i voted against the following items, which have now been adopted: paragraph 20 calls for 'at least decisions on proposals from the european minister for foreign affairs to be taken by majority vote'. it is clear from paragraph 26 that 'the common costs of esdp operations, including military operations, shall be financed from the union budget'. point 28 calls for the creation of a community defence budget and point 46 for a 'common territorial defence in europe' ('homeland defence'). the swedish christian democrats want to strengthen european cooperation in foreign policy, without member states being deprived of their own decision-making power in the military field or being obliged to co-finance military activities which they cannot support. the right of veto must be maintained for defence cooperation, as must the principle that eu military cooperation should not be developed outside the north atlantic treaty but within the framework of nato cooperation.
in writing. - (sv) i abstained in the final vote. we, the christian democrats of the group of the european people's party (christian democrats) and european democrats, voted against the motion for a resolution (now adopted by 191 votes to 179) on an 'international mandate in palestine including an international force on the ground'. that would take us back several decades. point 18 is unclear in that it does not distinguish between terrorist acts committed by palestinian suicide bombers and actions carried out by the israeli army. in recital g, the quartet peace plan is described as 'the last chance for the western world to gain credibility in the eyes of arab and islamic public opinion'. that's not the point. the peace plan is about peace and reconciliation between israelis and palestinians - and nothing else.
in writing. - (sv) we swedish social democrats agree with many of the points in the report, but we would like to make our point about a common tax policy. we are in favour of the idea of minimum levels for certain taxes, for example corporation tax and capital tax, as this should facilitate the implementation of an internal market policy. by introducing a partially common tax policy, we could, for example, increase the cost of activities harmful to the environment. we would like to stress, however, that we are opposed to any harmonization of income taxation. national parliaments must continue to legislate in this area, also in the future.
in writing. - (sv) this report is a step towards the very ambitious lisbon strategy objective of making the eu the most competitive and dynamic knowledge-based economy in the world, capable of sustainable economic growth accompanied by a quantitative and qualitative improvement in employment. while many eu member states are facing significant economic problems, notably germany and france, which do not comply with the requirements of the euro stability pact, this objective still seems far from being achieved. it is therefore welcome to start discussing the role of industrial issues in relation to this objective. industry is nevertheless the basis of the european economy. the report also calls for the harmonisation of national tax systems because they, it says, 'have a negative impact on the single market'. we swedish christian democrats are opposed to this formulation, because decisions on tax policy are a matter for the member states. that is why i voted against paragraph 1d.

madam president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. i share mr smet's view that the great challenge in the field of social policy is also linked to enlargement. the introduction of eu social legislation on health and safety at work and labour law will mean a great deal for workers in the new member states. but it will also mean something for the present member states. the risk of social dumping between our countries will be much less, which is extremely important. another important issue in the context of enlargement is the dialogue between the social partners. in many of the new member states there is no effective dialogue and no social partners who are accustomed to participating in the social policy agenda. a third issue is lisbon cooperation, which we must review and uphold. this should not mean, however, that we do not make proposals in new areas, for example in the field of health and safety at work, because of the new risks associated with atypical work and other issues. we must therefore cope with enlargement and with new risks and new social policy challenges.
